\mathcal{C}
. This
E^{\prime}
-parameterization is an
E
-parameterization when the corresponding representation is free. An
E^{\prime}_{2}
-parameterization
\mathcal{E}
for
\mathcal{C}
is a pair of
E^{\prime}
-parameterizations
(\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{E}_{2})
for
\mathcal{C}
, and similarly an
E_{2}
-parameterization is a pair of
E
-parameterizations.

Below, we characterize the abstract linear algebra data parameterizing codomains of the framings of the tangent spaces of thickenings and obstruction bundles of a framed flow category.

Definition 58
.

Let
\Gamma
be a
G
-poset. An
F^{\prime}
-parameterization of
\Gamma
is an assignment of inner product spaces
V(x,y)
for each pair of elements
x>y
of
\Gamma
, as well as linear isometries

\tau_{xyz}:V(x,y)\oplus V(y,z)\to V(x,z)

(47)

such that for every quadruple of objects
x>y>z>t
of
\Gamma
, and for every triple
(a,b,c)\in V(x,y)\oplus V(y,z)\oplus V(z,t)
we have

\tau_{xyt}(a,\tau_{yzt}(b,c,0),0)=\tau_{xzt}(\tau_{xyz}(a,b,0),c,0).

Moreover, there for all
g\in G
, there are isometries

g_{xy}:V(x,y)\to V(gx,gy)\text{ for all }x,y\in\Gamma,x>y,

(48)

satisfying

g^{\prime}_{gx,gy}g_{x,y}=(g^{\prime}g)_{x,y}\text{ for }g,g^{\prime}\in G,

(id)_{xy}=id,id_{xyz}=id,

\tau_{gx,gy,gz}(g_{xy}\oplus g_{yz})=g_{xz}\tau_{xyz}\text{ for all }g\in G,

where in the above we either refer to conditions ranging over all pairs
x>y
of
\Gamma
or over all triples
x>y>z
of
\Gamma
.

An isomorphism of
F^{\prime}
-parameterizations with underlying vector spaces
(\{V(x,y)\},\{W(x,y)\})
is a family of isomorphisms
V(x,y)\to W(x,y)
commuting with the maps
\tau_{xyz}
and
g_{xy}
in the obvious way.

An
F^{\prime}_{2}
-parameterization
\mathcal{F}
of
\Gamma
is a pair
(\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{2})
of
F^{\prime}_{2}
-parameterizations of
\Gamma
.

A direct sum
\mathcal{F}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{F}_{2}
of
F^{\prime}_{2}
-parameterizations
(\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{2})
with underlying vector spaces
(\{V(x,y)\},\{W(x,y)\})
is the
F^{\prime}
-parameterization assigns to
x>y
the vector space
V(x,y)\oplus W(x,y)
and maps maps
\tau_{xyz}
and
g_{xy}
the direct sum of the corresponding maps for
\mathcal{F}_{1}
and
\mathcal{F}_{2}
. A direct sum of
F^{\prime}_{2}
-parameterizations
\mathcal{F}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{F}_{2}
with
\mathcal{F}_{a}=(\mathcal{F}_{a,0},\mathcal{F}_{a,1})
for
a=1,2
, is
(\mathcal{F}_{1,0}\oplus\mathcal{F}_{2,0},\mathcal{F}_{1,1}\oplus\mathcal{F}_{2,1})
.

Lemma 8
.

Let
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
be a framed
G
-equivariant flow category. Then, in the notation of Definition
40
, the tuples
(\{V^{0}(x)\},\{W^{0}(x,y,z)\},\{\tau^{0}_{xyz}\})
and
(\{V^{1}(x)\},\{W^{0}(x,y,z)\},\{\tau^{1}_{xyz}\})
each define an
F_{2}^{\prime}
-parameterization.
\blacksquare

Definition 59
.

An embedding an
F^{\prime}
-parameterization
\mathcal{F}
of
\Gamma
is an
E^{\prime}
-parameterization
\mathcal{E}=\{V(x)\}
of
\Gamma
together with a set of isometries

\bar{\alpha}_{xy}:V(x,y)\oplus V(y)\to V(x),\alpha_{xy}=\bar{\alpha}_{xy}|_{V(x,y)},\hat{\alpha}_{xy}=\bar{\alpha}_{xy}|_{V(y)}

for every pair of elements
x>y
of
\Gamma
(where we are using the notation of Definitions
58
and
50
), which extend the embeddings
\hat{\alpha}_{xy}:V(y)\to V(x)
of the
E^{\prime}
-parameterization, and such that that these embeddings satisfy the diagram (
26
) with
V^{i}
replaced by
V
and
W^{0}
replaced by
W
. We say that this is an embedding of
\mathcal{F}
into
\mathcal{E}
.

An embedding of an
F^{\prime}_{2}
-parameterization
(\mathcal{F}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{1})
is is a pair of embeddings of
\mathcal{F}_{i}
into
\mathcal{E}_{i}
for
i=0,1
, respectively. We say this embedding has underlying
E^{\prime}_{2}
-parameterization
(\mathcal{E}_{0},\mathcal{E}_{1})
.

Lemma 9
.

An
(E^{\prime},E^{\prime}_{2})
-parameterization
\mathcal{E}
determines an associated
(F^{\prime},F^{\prime}_{2})
-parameterization
\mathcal{F}
together with an embedding of
\mathcal{F}
into
\mathcal{E}
.

Proof.

For each
x>y
objects of
\Gamma
, define
V(x,y)
to be the perpendicular of the image of
V(y)
in
V(x)
. For each
x>y>z
objects of
\Gamma
, set
W(x,y,z)
to be zero. For each such triple, define

\tau_{xyz}:V(x,y)\oplus V(y,z)\to V(x,z)

to be map that acts by the injective isometry
V(y)\to V(x)
on the second factor and then adds the two elements. For
g\in G
and
x>y
objects of
\Gamma
, define
g_{xy}:V(x,y)\to V(gx,gy)
to be the restrictions of the maps
g_{x}:V(x)\to V(y)
(this maps sends
V(x,y)
onto
V(gx,gy)
because
g_{y}
sends
V(y)
onto
V(gy)
and because
g_{x}\hat{\alpha}_{xy}=\hat{\alpha}_{gx,gy}g_{y}
). It is straightforward to see that these maps satisfy the required conditions.
∎

Definition 60
.

We say that an
(F^{\prime},F^{\prime}_{2})
-parameterization is an
(F,F_{2})
-parameterization, or alternatively that it is
free
, if it is isomorphic to the
(F^{\prime},F^{\prime}_{2})
-parameterization associated to
some

(E,E_{2})
-parameterization (note the lack of primes!)

Definition 61

(Restriction and Fixed Points.)
.

Let
\Gamma
be a
G
-poset and let
H\subset G
be a subgroup. Then
\Gamma
is an
H
-poset under the inclusion
H\subset G
.

Let
\Gamma^{H}
is the poset of elements fixed by
H
. If
H
is normal, this is naturally a
G/H
-poset.

The
restriction
of an
(E^{\prime},E^{\prime}_{2},F^{\prime},F^{\prime}_{2})
-parameterization of
\Gamma
from
G
to
H
is the corresponding representation of
\Gamma
thought of as an
H
-poset under
H\subset G
obtained by forgetting the maps
g_{xy}
for
g\notin H
.

The
H
-fixed points
of an
(E^{\prime},E^{\prime}_{2},F^{\prime},F^{\prime}_{2})
-parameterization
\mathcal{G}
of
\Gamma
are the
(E^{\prime},E^{\prime}_{2},F^{\prime},F^{\prime}_{2})
-parameterization
\mathcal{G}^{H}
of
\Gamma^{H}
obtained by taking
H
-fixed points of all assigned vector spaces and maps. If
H
was normal, this is naturally an
(E^{\prime},E^{\prime}_{2},F^{\prime},F^{\prime}_{2})
-parameterization of
\Gamma^{H}
as a
G/H
-poset.

Lemma 10
.

Freeness of all
(E^{\prime},E^{\prime}_{2},F^{\prime},F^{\prime}_{2})
-parameterizations is preserved by restriction and taking fixed points.
\blacksquare

Lemma 11
.

Given an embedding of
(F^{\prime},F^{\prime}_{2})
-parameterization
\mathcal{F}
into a
(E^{\prime},E^{\prime}_{2}
) parameterization
\mathcal{E}
, the
(F^{\prime},F^{\prime}_{2})
parameterization produced by Lemma
9
from
\mathcal{E}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{F}
.

Proof.

This follows because the condition that the embedding is adapted means that for every
x>y
objects of
\Gamma
, the map
\bar{\alpha}_{xy}
restricted to
V(x,y)
must send
V(x,y)
isometrically onto the perpendicular to the image of
V(y)
under the isometric embedding associated to the poset representation, the latter being simply the restriction of
\bar{\alpha}_{xy}
to
V(y)
.
∎

By (
43
), the vector spaces underlying an
F
-parameterization can be written like

V(x,y)=\oplus_{x\geq z,y\ngeq z}V^{*}(y).

where
V^{*}
are produced from the corresponding
\mathcal{E}
-representation as in (
43
)
with the maps
\tau_{xyz}
for
x>y>z
objects of
\Gamma
given by the natural inclusion of direct sums.

Definition 62
.

Given a representation
\{V(x)\}
of a
G
-poset
\Gamma
, one can associate a map

\mu(x):\Gamma\to\cup_{H\subset G}RO(H)

such that
\mu(x)\in RO(Stab_{x})
gives the class of
V(x)
. This map is called the
numerical invariant
of the representation, and will have the property that
\mu(gx)
is the image of
\mu(x)
under the map
RO(Stab_{x})\to RO(Stab_{gx})
induced by conjugation the isomorphism
Stab_{x}\to Stab_{gx}
given by conjugation by
g
.

Lemma 12
.

A pair of
E_{2}
-parameterizations
\{V(x)\},\{V^{\prime}(x)\}
with the same numerical invariants
\mu(x),\mu^{\prime}(x)
are isomorphic.

Proof.

First note that for each each
x
,
\oplus_{y\in Gx}V(y)
is a
G
-representation via the action of
\oplus_{y\in Gx}g_{y}
. Moreover, this
G
-representation is isomorphic to the induction of
V(x)
as a
Stab_{x}
-representation from
Stab_{x}
to
G
. This representation has the property, in particular, that for any other subgroup
H\subset G
,
\oplus_{y\in Hx}V(y)
is an
H
-representation.

Fix
x\in\Gamma
. Assume (by inductive hypothesis) that for every
G
-orbit
o
of
\Gamma
which contains some element that is strictly less than
x
in
\Gamma
, we have fixed a distinguished element
y\in o
(which may or may not be less than
x
); denote the set of distinguished elements as
Y\subset\Gamma
. Assume further that for each distinguished element
y\in Y
that there are
Stab_{y}
-equivariant isomorphisms
\gamma_{y}:V^{*}(y)\simeq(V^{\prime})^{*}(y)
for all
y\in\Gamma
such that
y<x
.

Using this
\gamma_{y}
, we can, for any other element
y^{\prime}\in Gy
, define a map

\gamma_{y^{\prime}}:V^{*}(y^{\prime})\to(V^{\prime})^{*}(y^{\prime}),g_{y}\gamma_{y^{\prime}}=\gamma_{y^{\prime}}g^{-1}_{y^{\prime}}\text{ where }y^{\prime}=gy\text{ for some }g\in G.

These maps satisfy the following properties:

•

They are well defined, i.e. the above formula defines a map that is independent of the choice of
g\in G
.

•

The direct sum map

\bigoplus_{y^{\prime}\in Gy}\gamma_{y^{\prime}}:\bigoplus_{y\in G^{\prime}}V^{*}(y^{\prime})\to\bigoplus_{y^{\prime}\in Gy}(V^{\prime})^{*}(y^{\prime})

is
G
-equivariant, and

•

For any
H
subgroup
H
of
G
, letting
o^{\prime}
be an
H
-orbit in
Gy
(which may not contain
y
),
the map

\bigoplus_{y^{\prime}\in o^{\prime}}\gamma_{y^{\prime}}:\bigoplus_{y^{\prime}\in o^{\prime}}V^{*}(y^{\prime})\to\bigoplus_{y^{\prime}\in o^{\prime}}(V^{\prime})^{*}(y^{\prime})

is an
H
-equivariant isomorphism.

For any
y<x
, writing

V(y)=\bigoplus_{z\in\Gamma,y>z}V^{*}(z)=\bigoplus_{o\in\{z\in\Gamma,y>z\}/Stab_{y}}\bigoplus_{z^{\prime}\in o}V^{*}(z)

(49)

and similarly for
V^{\prime}(y)
, we see that the corresponding direct sum of maps
\bigoplus_{z\in\Gamma,y>z}\gamma_{z}
defines a
Stab_{y}
-equivariant isomorphism
V(y)\to V^{\prime}(y)
.

We now turn to
x
. If we have already chosen a distinguished element
x^{\prime}\in Gx\cap Y
and found an isomorphism
\gamma_{x^{\prime}}
, then the above discussion defines
\gamma_{x}
and thus a
Stab_{x}
-equivariant isomorphism
V(x)\to V^{\prime}(x)
. If we have not, we will add
x
to
Y
and show that a map
\gamma_{x}
as above exists. Indeed, the decomposition (
49
), now applied to
V(x)
, and the properties listed above of the maps
\alpha_{y}
for
y<x
shows that
[V^{*}(x)]=[(V^{\prime})^{*}(x)]
in
RO(Stab_{x})
. But then a choice of
\alpha_{x}
exists; we pick any one and continue the induction.
∎

Remark 36
.

Lemma
12
is the fundamental reason we always use (semi-)free parameterizations throughout this paper; it is the minimal formalization of the statement that the parameterizations of the framings, at least when they are taken to be (semi-)free, are entirely homotopically auxiliary data (which is suggested in the discussion of Section
4.4
about parameterizatoins). If one does not impose a condition like semi-freeness such that some analog of Lemma
12
holds, it becomes exteremely difficult to work with the resulting homotopy types, and the
space of parameterizations
begins to play a very complicated role in all constructions. The author is entirely unsure, for example, of how to show that two parameterizations are deformation equivalent after sufficient stabilization; while Lemma
12
makes this completely trivial. Beyond that, statements like the existence of shift spaces (see Definition
63
below) completely fail to be true for parameterizations in the general case (one can easily find an counterexample where
\Gamma
has four objects), making it very difficult to add new objects to a flow category. The convenient language of (semi-)free parameterizations and the resulting (elementary) theory completely avoids all these technicalities hiding in the background.

Remark 37
.

The importance of (semi)-free parameterization is the fact that they admit shift spaces (discussed below) together with the fact that they are preserved under group restriction
and
under taking invariants; simple generalizations of canonical parameterizations to the equivariant setting (obtained e.g. by choosing orderings on irreducible representations of
G
) generally do not satisfy all of these properties at once.

Lemma 13
.

Given vector spaces
\{V(x)\}_{x\in\Gamma}
and isometries
g_{x}:V(x)\to V(gx)
satisfying (
45
), define the classes
V^{*}(x)\in RO(G_{x})
inductively as follows. If
x
is a minimum of
\Gamma
, set
V^{*}(x)=V(x)
. Otherwise,
If
V(y^{\prime})
is defined as
y^{\prime}
ranges over
y^{\prime}\in Gy
, write

\bigoplus_{y^{\prime}\in G_{x}y}V^{*}(y^{\prime})\in RO(G_{x})

for the corresponding virtual
G_{x}
-representation isomorphic to
Ind_{G_{xy}}^{G_{x}}Res^{G_{y}}_{G_{xy}}
.

If
V^{*}(y)
is defined for all
y<x
, define

V^{*}(x)=V^{0}(x)-\bigoplus_{y<x}V^{*}(y)\in RO(G_{x}).

(50)

Suppose that
V^{*}(x)
,
which is a-priori virtual, is represented by an actual
G_{x}
-representation, for every
x
. Then the vector spaces
\{V(x)\}
and operators
g_{x}
are the underlying vector spaces and operators of an equivariant
E
-parameterization of
\Gamma
.

Proof.

We inductively define the embeddings
\hat{\alpha}_{xy}
. We induct upwards on the poset
\Gamma/G
. For the minima of
\Gamma/G
there is nothing to do. Suppose we have defined an
E
-parameterization of the pre-image
\Gamma^{\prime}
of a downwards closed sub-poset
\Gamma^{\prime}/G
of
\Gamma/G
under
\Gamma\to\Gamma/G
, which has underlying vector spaces and operators the ones specified. Pick an element
[x]\in\Gamma/G
such that all elements less than this element are in
\Gamma^{\prime}/G
, and a representative
x\in[x]
. Then by the condition we can define a
G_{x}
-equivariant embedding of
\bigoplus_{y^{\prime}<x}/GV^{*}(y^{\prime})
into
V(x)
. Fix the representative of the class (
50
) to be the perpendicular of this image. Restricting these embeddings to the sums of the
V^{*}(y^{\prime})
contained in
V(y)
for
y<x
defines the embedding
\hat{\alpha}_{xy}
. One then sets
\hat{\alpha}_{gxgy}=g_{x}\hat{\alpha}_{xy}g^{-1}_{y}
.
∎

Definition 63

(Shift spaces.)
.

To an
E^{\prime}
-parameterization
\mathcal{E}
of a
G
-poset
\Gamma
can associated a canonical
G
-representation called the
shift space
of
\mathcal{E}
, characterized by the following property. Write
\Gamma^{\prime}=\Gamma\cup m
for the poset with
m
a new object that is strictly larger than all previous objects of
\Gamma
, and otherwise the same partial order on the objects of
\Gamma\subset\Gamma^{\prime}
; with the group action fixing
m
. The
shift space
of
\mathcal{E}
, when it exists, is the orthogonal
G
-representation assigned by an extension
\mathcal{E}_{m}
of
\mathcal{E}
up to
\Gamma_{m}
to thhe object
m
which is initial among such extensions
\mathcal{E}_{m}
in the category of
E^{\prime}
-parameterizations of
\Gamma^{\prime}
with morphisms
\{V(x)\}\to\{W(x)\}
given by object-wise injective natural transformations
f_{x}:V(x)\to W(x)
of functors commuting with the
G
action i.e. such that
f_{gx}g_{x}=g_{x}f_{x}
for all
g\in G
and
x\in\Gamma
. This
G
-representation is manifestly unique up to unique isomorphism in the category of orthogonal
G
-representations, but it may or may not exist.

Remark 38
.

In the construction of the Floer homotopy type (Definition
33
) of a virtually smooth flow category
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
, we use the shift space of the parameterization of the framing of the obstruction bundles and we desuspend the Floer homotopy space by this shift space to get ther Floer homotopy type. We refer to this shift space as the shift space of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
.

Remark 39
.

Shift spaces will exist for semi-free
E^{\prime}
-representations, and are computed in Lemma
14
and Lemma
16
below; whenever we refer to
the
shift space of a
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
representation we use the formulae in those lemmas as a preferred choice of shift space.

Lemma 14

(Shift spaces for free parameterizations.)
.

Suppose
\mathcal{E}=\{V(x)\}
is a free (equivariant)
\Gamma
-representation. Then the shift space of
\Gamma
exists and is given by

V_{\mathcal{E}}=\oplus_{x\in\Gamma}V^{*}(x).\blacksquare

(51)

Canonical and semi-free parameterizations.

Definition 64
.

Let
\Gamma
be a
G-
poset equipped with an
integral action
, i.e. a map
\bar{A}:\Gamma\to\{n\in\mathbb{Z}:n\geq-1\}
such that if
x>y
in
\Gamma
then
\bar{A}(x)>\bar{A}(y)
. To this data, we can associate the set
S_{\bar{A}}
as in Definition
17
.

A
canonical parameterization
of
\Gamma
(thought of as being equipped with
\bar{A}
) is an assignment of a natural number
f(n)
to each element of
S_{\bar{A}}
. This defines an
E^{\prime}
-parameterization of
\Gamma
via

x\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{f(\bar{A}(x))}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{f(\bar{A}(x)-1)}\oplus\ldots\oplus\mathbb{R}^{f(0)}.

Here, the
G
-action is everywhere
trivial
, and the maps associated to
x>y
in
\Gamma
are just natural inclusion into the right-most factors. We call this
E^{\prime}
-parameterization or its associated
F^{\prime}
-parameterization
canonical
; we call an (
E^{\prime}_{2}
or
F^{\prime}_{2}
) parameterization for which each element of the pair is canonical, canonical.

When a parameterization has a specified isomorphism with a canonical parameterization, we will also refer to it as canonical.

The proof of the following useful lemma is trivial:

Lemma 15
.

The direct sum
\mathcal{E}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{E}_{2}
of a pair of canonical
E^{\prime}
-parameterization
\mathcal{E}_{1}=\{V_{1}(x)\}
and
\mathcal{E}_{2}=\{V_{2}(x)\}
defined by functions
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
, respectively, is isomorphic to the canonical
E^{\prime}
-parameterization
\mathcal{E}_{12}
with defining function
f=f_{1}+f_{2}
, via the reordering maps

\left(\mathbb{R}^{f_{1}(\bar{A}(x))}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathbb{R}^{f_{1}(0)}\right)\oplus\left(\mathbb{R}^{f_{2}(\bar{A}(x))}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathbb{R}^{f_{2}(0)}\right)\to\left(\mathbb{R}^{f_{1}(\bar{A}(x))}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{f_{2}(\bar{A}(x))}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathbb{R}^{f_{1}(0)}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{f_{2}(0)}\right)

where grouping pairs of factors on the right hand side of course gives
\mathcal{E}_{12}
. Whenever we take a direct sum of a pair of canonical parameterizations, we will view it as a canonical parameterization via the isomorphism induced by the one specified above.
\blacksquare

Definition 65
.

The direct sum
\mathcal{E}_{f}\oplus\mathcal{E}_{c}
of a canonical
E^{\prime}
-parameterization
\mathcal{E}_{c}
with a free
E
-parameterization
\mathcal{E}_{f}
will be referred to as an
E^{s}
parameterization; the associated
F^{\prime}
-parameterization will be referred to as an
F^{s}
-parameterization, and an
F^{\prime}_{2}
parameterization for which each element of the pair is an
F^{s}
-parameterization will be called an
F^{s}_{2}
-parameterization. We will use ‘semi-free parameterization’ to refer to any of an
E^{s}-,F^{s}-
, or
F^{s}_{2}
-parameterization.

Lemma 16
.

Any semi-free representation
\mathcal{E}_{f}\oplus\mathcal{E}_{c}
(where
\mathcal{E}_{c}
is a canonical representation defined by the function
f
) has a shift space given by

V_{\mathcal{E}}=V_{\mathcal{E}_{f}}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{\sum_{i\in S_{\bar{A}}}f(i)}

with
V_{\mathcal{E}_{f}}
as in Lemma
14
and the
G
-action is trivial on the second factor above.
\blacksquare

Remark 40
.

Note that the factors
T\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\bar{A}(x,y)}
in the definition of a framing of a virtually smooth category can be viewed as the terms of a canonical
F^{\prime}
-parameterization, and thus if the flow category is freely parameterized then the terms
T\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\bar{A}(x,y)}\times V^{0}(x,y)
giving the targets of the framings of the thickenings
T(x,y)
can be viewed as the terms of an
F^{s}
-parameterization. Thus, even if the parameterization associated to a framing is free, in some sense the notion of a framing almost forces the appearance of semi-free parameterizations in the general discussion. However, as mentioned in Remark
28
, we cannot stick to the use of canonical parameterizations, as previous works do, because if the importance of being able to easily change the symmetry group
G
.

Definition 66

(Stabilization of canonical parameterizations.)
.

We can
stabilize
a canonical
E^{\prime}
-parameterization
\mathcal{E}_{1}
with defining function
f_{1}
another canonical
E^{\prime}
-parameterization
\mathcal{E}_{2}
with defining function
f_{2}
by specifying maps
\mathbb{R}^{f_{1}(n)}\to\mathbb{R}^{f_{2}(n)}
for each
n\in S_{\bar{A}}
which include the domain as the set where a chosen subset of the
f_{2}(n)
coordinates of size
f_{2}(n)-f_{1}(n)
are required to be zero. It is easy to see that
\mathcal{E}_{2}
is the direct sum of
\mathcal{E}_{1}
with the canonical
E^{\prime}
-parameterization with defining function
f_{2}-f_{1}
; the inclusions specified above fix the isomorphism with the direct sum. This notion defines the corresponding notion of stabilization of canonical
F^{\prime}
,
E^{\prime}_{2}
,and
F^{\prime}_{2}
parameterizations.

We will refer to a stabilization of an any semi-free parameterization
M
as one that is obtained by first stabilizing the free part of
M
and subsequently stabilizing the canonically parameterized part (of course, these stabilization operations can be performed in either order, up to canonical isomorphism).

Linear parameterization of stabilizations.

In this paper, we are largely able to avoid discussion of general kinds of stabilizations of flow categories. However, we do have to perform one particularly simple kind of stabilization, which we describe below.

Definition 67
.

A stabilization of a framed virtually smooth flow category
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}^{\prime})
by an
F^{\prime}
-parameterization
\mathcal{F}
of
\mathcal{C}
(which is required to be
G
-equivariant if
\mathcal{C}
is) with underlying vector spaces
\{V(x,y)\}
is the framed virtually smooth flow category
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{F}}
, which has the same objects as
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
, satisfies
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{F}}(x,y)=\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(x,y)_{V(x,y)}
(Definition
12
) and with the composition maps

(f_{\mathcal{F}},\bar{f}_{\mathcal{F}},\bar{f^{\prime}}_{\mathcal{F}}):\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{F}}(x,y)\times\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{F}}(y,z)\to\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{F}}(x,z)

induced by the maps
\tau_{xyz}
covering the compositions

(f,\bar{f},\bar{f^{\prime}}):\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(x,y)\times\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(y,z)\to\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(x,z).

If the underlying
F^{\prime}_{2}
-parameterization of the framing of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
was
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}=(\mathcal{F}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{1})
, then the underlying
F^{\prime}_{2}
-parameterization of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{F}}
is

\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}\boxplus\mathcal{F}:=(\mathcal{F}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{F}).

If
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}
was an
F^{\prime}_{2}
-parameterization arising from the
E^{\prime}_{2}
-parameterization
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}(\mathcal{E}_{0},\mathcal{E}_{1})
, and
\mathcal{F}
is an
F^{\prime}
-parameterization arising from the
E^{\prime}
-parameterization
\mathcal{E}
, then
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}\boxplus\mathcal{F}
is an
F_{2}
-parameterization arising from the
E_{2}
-parameterization

\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C}}\boxplus\mathcal{E}=(\mathcal{E}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{E}).

In this case we will denote the stabilized virtual smooothing
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{F}}(x,y)
by
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{E}}
as well.

5.3
Stabilization of embeddings and Floer homotopy types

We have now explained how to define spaces
\{\mathcal{C}\}
associated to extensions of embeddings of framed flow categories. Certain desuspensions of these spaces are then the associated floer homotopy types
|\mathcal{C}|
. In this section, we describe the sense in which the spectra
|\mathcal{C}|
do not change (up to
canonical
weak equivalance) upon
stabilizations
of the flow categories and their associated embeddings. This turns out to be very helpful when trying to “extend” embeddings from flow subcategories to larger flow categories, as is necessary when when defining continuation maps or any other operations on Floer homotopy types.

Because of the way that we set up Definitions
32
and
33
, we can use a very strict notion of a stabilization of an embedding of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
and consequently prove a strict invariance statement for the floer homotopy type. Imagine
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{1}
is a virtually smooth flow category with an extension of an embedding, and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{2}
is produced from
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{1}
by stabilizing the category and the associated embedding. In our setup we will establish a
canonical homeomorphism
of
\{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{2}\}
with a suspension of
\{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{1}\}
. The fact that this is a homeomorphism, rather than a homotopy equivalence defined up to contractible choice, allows us to simplify some of the coherence data we need to keep track of later when constructing the cyclotomic structure on symplectic cohomology. This is possible because when defining the Floer homotopy type we use
canonical
maps on the the
V^{0}(y)
factors in the definition of the
\tilde{e}_{x,i}
in (
37
). If instead we used maps which were
homotopy equivalent
to these canonical maps, some some of the other constructions (e.g. when constructing or extending embeddings, as in Proposition
3
and
4
below) would have been simpler, at the cost of a loss of the canonical nature of the homeomorphisms we establish below.

In any case, we proceed with the constructions.

Lemma 17

(Stabilizing embeddings by
E^{s}
-parameterizations.)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
be a flow category with an extension
\bar{\mathbf{E}}=\{e_{xy}\}
of an embedding
\mathbf{E}=\{\iota_{xy}\}
produced by applying Proposition
3
to
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
. Let
\mathcal{E}
be an
E
-parameterization of
\mathcal{C}
with associated
F
-parameterization
\mathcal{F}
. Then there is a canonical embedding
\mathbf{E}_{\mathcal{E}}=\{\iota_{xy}^{\mathcal{E}}\}
of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{F}}
which is the
stabilization
of the embedding
\mathbf{E}
by
\mathcal{E}
, as well a corresponding extension
\bar{\mathbf{E}}_{\mathcal{E}}=\{e_{xy}^{\mathcal{E}}\}
of
\mathbf{E}^{\mathcal{E}}
which we also refer to as the stabilization of
\bar{\mathbf{E}}
by
\mathcal{E}
. These are characterized by the property that, writing
V_{\mathcal{F}}(x,y)
for the vector space assigned by
\mathcal{F}
to
x>y
in
\mathcal{C}
, we have that

\iota_{xy}^{\mathcal{E}}=\iota_{xy}\times id_{V_{\mathcal{F}}(x,y)},e^{\mathcal{E}}_{xy}=e_{xy}\wedge id_{(V_{\mathcal{F}}(x,y))_{*}}.

Proof.

The only nontrivial condition to check is the disjointness condition for embeddings and for extensions of embeddings. Write
V_{\mathcal{E}}(x)
for the vector space associated by
\mathcal{E}
to
x\in\mathcal{C}
. To see that the disjointness condition still holds holds, we note that the embedding maps for
\mathcal{F}
give canonical isomorphisms

V_{\mathcal{F}}(x,y)\times V_{\mathcal{E}}(y)\to V_{\mathcal{E}}(x);

as such, the embedding maps maps in the disjointness condition for
\bar{\mathbf{E}}_{\mathcal{E}}
look like

(T_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}(x,y_{i})\times V_{\mathcal{F}}(x,y_{i}))\times(V^{0}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}(y_{i})\times V_{\mathcal{E}}(y_{i}))\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\bar{A}(x,y_{i})}\times V^{0}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}(x)\times V_{\mathcal{E}}(x).

(52)

thus the disjointness condition still holds because the new embeddings are just the products of the old embeddings with
V_{\mathcal{E}}(x)
, so they are still disjoint as
y_{i}
ranges over the relevant set of objects of
\mathcal{C}
.
∎

Lemma 18
.

Given a framed virtually smooth flow category
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
with an extension
\bar{\mathbf{E}}
of an embedding
\mathbf{E}
, and an
E^{s}
-parameterization
\mathcal{E}
of
\mathcal{C}
, writing
\{\mathcal{C}\}
and
|\mathcal{C}|
for the Floer homotopy space/type associated to this data and writing

\{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}\},|\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}|
for the Floer homotopy space/type associated to the extension
\bar{\mathbf{E}}_{\mathcal{E}}
of of the embedding
\mathbf{E}_{\mathcal{E}}
of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}
, we have an equivariant homeomorphism

\Sigma^{V^{\mathcal{E}}_{max}}\{\mathcal{C}\}\simeq\{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}\}

(53)

where
V^{\mathcal{E}}_{max}
the shift space assigned to
\mathcal{E}
(see Definition
63
). This homeomorphism is characterized by the following property: write
(\{V^{0}(x)_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}\},\{V^{1}(x)_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}\})
for the
E_{2}^{s}
-parameterization associated to the framing of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
, and
V^{1}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime},max}
for the shift space assigned to
\{V^{1}(x)_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}\}.
Then we manifestly have

V^{1}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{F}},max}=V^{1}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime},max}\oplus V^{\mathcal{E}}_{max},V^{0}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{F}}}(x)=V^{0}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}(x)\oplus V^{\mathcal{E}}(x),(V^{1}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{F}}(x)})^{\perp}=V^{1}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}(x)^{\perp}\oplus V^{\mathcal{E}}(x)^{\perp}

where
V^{\mathcal{E}}(x)^{\perp}
is the perpendicular to the image of
V^{\mathcal{E}}(x)
in
V^{\mathcal{E}}_{max}
. From this we see that there is a homeomorphism

\Sigma^{V^{\mathcal{E}}_{max}}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\bar{A}(x)+1}\times V^{0}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}(x)\times V^{1}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}(x)^{\perp})_{*}\simeq(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\bar{A}(x)+1}\times V^{0}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{F}}}(x)\times V^{1}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{F}}}(x)^{\perp})_{*}

The homeomorphism (
53
) is defined by requiring that it restricts to the composition of the above homeomorphism with the inclusion of its codomain into
\{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{F}}\}
on the image of its domain in
\Sigma^{V^{\mathcal{E}}_{max}}\{\mathcal{C}\}
.

Proof.

One simply needs to check that the definition of the maps
\tilde{e}_{x}
for
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{F}}
is the smash product of the same maps for
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
with the sphere
S^{V^{\mathcal{E}}_{max}}
. One can check by checking this for each
\tilde{e}_{x,i}
, and subsequently on on each open
U^{\prime\prime}_{xy}
where the definition of the map may be nontrivial. Now there are canonical isomorphisms

V^{\mathcal{E}}(x)\oplus V^{\mathcal{E}}(x)^{\perp}\simeq V_{\mathcal{E}}(y)\oplus V^{\mathcal{E}}(x,y)\oplus V^{\mathcal{E}}(x)^{\perp}\simeq V^{\mathcal{E}}(y)\oplus V^{\mathcal{E}}(y)^{\perp}

which commute with the isomorphisms of the left and right hand sides of this equation with
V^{\mathcal{E}}_{max}
described in statement of the lemma; the maps on each
U^{\prime\prime}_{xy}
for
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{F}}
simply built out of the maps on
U^{\prime\prime}_{xy}
by summing with the middle term of the above equation, analogously to (
52
), and the map is just the smash product with (the 1-point compactification) of the second map in the above equation. This verifies that the attaching maps indeed agree with the smash product of the previous attaching maps with the identity map on
V^{\mathcal{E}}_{max}
.
∎

5.4
Linear algebra of the Pontrjagin-Thom construction

In this section, we review certain elementary aspects of the Pontrjagin-Thom construction, as preparation for the construction of embeddings of flow categories in Section
5.5
below.

Traditionally, in the Pontrjagin-Thom construction, given a manifold
M^{n}
and a framing
TM\simeq\underline{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
, we embed
M
into
\mathbb{R}^{m}
and then subsequently into
\mathbb{R}^{m+N}
for some large
N>>0
by composing with the
canonical
embedding
\mathbb{R}^{m}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m+N}
, then then argue that the normal bundle of
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{m+N}
is canonically trivialized. In fact, the normal bundle is already canonically trivialized with
N=n
. For every
x\in M
, we have a map
A:T_{x}M\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
, and an isomorphism
B:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to T_{x}M
. Writing
\bar{A}:T_{x}M\to\mathbb{R}^{m}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{n}
for
\bar{A}=A\oplus 0
, we have that
\coker\bar{A}=\coker A\oplus\mathbb{R}^{n}
. But there is a canonical isomorphism
\mathbb{R}^{m}\simeq\dom AB\oplus\coker AB
; since
\coker AB=\coker A
and
\dom AB=\mathbb{R}^{n}
, as
A
and
B
vary continuously, so does the above isomorphism. The collections of these isomorphisms over
x\in M
thus define a trivialization of the normal bundle of
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
. .

An important aspect of the Pontrjagin-Thom construction is that, in a certain precise sense that is not always completely articulated, this trivialization of the normal bundle of
M
is compatible with stabilization. Let us contemplate replacing the embedding
\iota_{m+n}:M^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{m+n}
with the embedding
\iota_{m+N}:M^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{m+N}
,
N>n
, given by composing with the canonical inclusion
\mathbb{R}^{m+n}\to\mathbb{R}^{m+N}
into the first
m+n
coordinates. The normal bundle
\nu_{\iota_{m+N}}
is canonically isomorphic to
\nu_{\iota_{m+n}}\oplus\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{N-n}
, and we would like to say that there is a trivialization of
\nu_{\iota_{m+N}}
which is under this isomorphism simply the trivialization of
\nu_{\iota_{m+n}}
summed with the identity map on
\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{N-n}
.
However, this happens only if we keep track of the image of
\mathbb{R}^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m+n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m+N}
, and use
this
copy of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
to define the trivialization of
\nu_{\iota_{m+N}}
.

We could think of the relevant copy of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
as the ‘first
n
coordinates of
\mathbb{R}^{N}=0\oplus\mathbb{R}^{N}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}+N
. This uses the
ordering
on the natural numbers, and this convention is one of the usual ones in textbook discussions of the Pontrjagin-Thom construction. However, when one wishes to consider
equivariant
embeddings of
G
-manifolds, one will have to replace the vector spaces involved with various
G
-representations, and there is no longer a canonical ordering that can be used to track this data.

Remark 41
.

The issue above with the use of the
ordering
on the natural numbers, which does not immeidately generalize to the equivariant context, is closely related to the problems related to the ordering of coordinates when defining smash products of spectra
[
11
]
. Carefully tracking certain decompositions of vector spaces while performing Pontrjagin-Thom constructions, as we do below, is heuristically analogous to the solution to this ordering problem pursued in the construction of orthogonal spectra.

We now describe the abstract linear algebra setup of the Pontrjagin-Thom construction. Given

•

inner product spaces
V,X,Y
;

•

an inclusion
\bar{A}:V\to X
, and

•

an orthogonal decomposition
X\simeq Y\oplus V
such that
Y\supset\im A
,

there is a canonical isomorphism
\coker A\simeq Y
given by writing
A:V\to Y
for the factorization of
\bar{A}
, and identifying
\coker\bar{A}\simeq(\coker A)\oplus Y\simeq Y
. Indeed, we can canonically identify
\coker A\simeq(\im A)^{\perp}
, and we can write the above isomorphism as
(\im\bar{A})^{\perp}=(\im A)^{\perp}\oplus Y\simeq Y
, where the last map sends
(v,w)\to v+Aw
.

Thus, we can describe the above construction by stating that
the normal bundle to
\bar{A}
is
Y
-framed
. This latter isomorphism clearly values continuously with
A
so long as
\im A
continues to stay in
Y
. Moreover, if all inner product spaces and maps are upgraded to orthogonal
G
-representations and
G
-equivariant maps, the isomorphism
\coker A\simeq Y
becomes
G
-equivariant, as well.

In the next three paragraphs, we will discuss how this map behaves under several standard operations.

Renaming the framing.

Clearly, if we choose an isomorphism
Y^{\prime}\simeq Y
then the normal bundle to
\bar{A}
will by
Y^{\prime}
-framed by composing with the inverse of this isomorphism.

Behavior under stabilization of
X
.

If we have an (equivariant, isometric) inclusion
X\subset X^{\prime}
, then we can replace
\bar{A}
with the composed embedding
\bar{A}^{\prime}:V\to X^{\prime}
; we have that
(\im\bar{A})^{\perp}=(\im\bar{A})\oplus X^{\perp}
, and replacing
Y
with
Y^{\prime}=Y\oplus X^{\perp}
, we have that the decomposition
X^{\prime}\simeq Y^{\prime}\oplus V
still holds and thus there is an isomorphism
(\im\bar{A}^{\prime})^{\perp}\simeq Y^{\prime}
.
(Here we use
\oplus
to denote direct sum of subspaces rather than abstract direct sum; of course, one is canonically isomorphic to the other.) In fact, this isomorphism is just the direct sum of the original isomorphism
(\im\bar{A})^{\perp}\simeq Y
with the identity map
X^{\perp}
.

Products of embeddings.

Finally, suppose that we are given a pair of embeddings
\bar{i}_{1}:M_{1}^{n_{1}}\subset X_{1}
and
\bar{i}_{2}:M_{2}^{n_{2}}\subset X_{2}
, as well as a pair of decompositions
X_{i}=Y_{i}\oplus V_{i},i=1,
as in the previous paragraph, as well as trivializatoins
TM_{i}\simeq\underline{V_{i}}
. An isometry
P_{12}:X_{1}\oplus X_{2}\to X
defines an embedding
\bar{i}_{12}:M_{1}\times M_{2}\to X
by product and composition, and a decomposition
\bar{P}_{12}:\eta\bar{i}_{12}\simeq\eta d\bar{i}_{1}\oplus\eta d\bar{i}_{2}
by applying
P_{12}^{-1}
to the orthogonal of the image of
d\bar{i}_{12}
. Define
Y=P_{12}(Y_{1}\oplus Y_{2})
. An isomorphism
P^{\prime}_{12}:V\to V_{1}\oplus V_{2}
then defines a trivialization
T(M_{1}\times M_{2})\simeq\underline{V}
by precomposition. The resulting isomorphism
\eta\bar{i}_{12}\simeq Y
then satisfies the condition that when compositing with
P^{\prime}_{12}
and with
\bar{P}^{-1}_{12}
it gives the sum of the trivializations of
\eta\bar{i}_{1}
and
\eta\bar{i}_{2}
.

Remark 42
.

There is another kind of natural kind of stabilization operation that can be performed on in this linear algebra setup, where one replaces stabilizing the domain
V
of
X
by a stabilized domain vector space
V\oplus W
for some vector space
W
. In the setting of Pontrjagin-Thom constructions, this operation would arise if we wish to understand the interaction of the Pontrjagin-Thom construction on manifold
M
, thought of a smooth derived manifold with a zero dimensional obstruction bundle, with the procedure of stabilizing the manifold (and the embedding) by a framed vector bundle. However, we are able to systematically avoid the discussion of this latter operation in this paper, except in the case of codimension zero embeddings, where the situation is rather simple.

5.5
Embeddings of flow categories.

Proposition 3
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}^{\prime})
be a finite virtually smooth framed
E^{\prime}_{2}
-parameterized
G
-equivariant flow category equipped with a very convenient metric. Then
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
can be stabilized by an
E
-parameterization
\mathcal{E}_{stab}
to
\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}
such that a restriction
\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime\prime}
of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}
admits an embedding.

This proposition is an analog of the equivariant Pontrjagin-Thom construction for compatible families of manifolds with corners. Moreover, the proof of this proposition uses a certain
equivariant double induction
strategy which is used repeatedly in the rest of the paper.

Proof of Proposition
3
.

This follows via an
equivariant double induction
. We explain the strategy in abstract for future reference.

Abstract description of equivariant double induction.

The outer loop of the induction is on downwards-closed
G
-subposets
\mathcal{P}\subset\mathcal{C}
. The base case of this induction is taken to be the empty poset.

For each step of the outer loop, one takes a
minimal element

x
of the poset
\mathcal{C}\setminus\mathcal{P}
, and writes
\mathcal{P}_{x}=\mathcal{P}\cup\{x\}
,
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}=\mathcal{P}\cup Gx
. Write also
\mathcal{P}_{\bar{x}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{x}
for the subset of elements
y
such that
x\geq y
. After completing the inner loop, one performs an operation (generally involving
transport of structure
by a natural
G/Stab(x)
-action) such that the inductive hypothesis is satisfied for
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
, and then continues the induction.

The inner loop of the induction is on upwards-closed
G_{x}:=Stab(x)
-subposets of
\mathcal{Q}\subset\mathcal{P}_{x}
. The base case is taken to be
\mathcal{Q}=\{x\}
. At each step, one chooses a maximal element
z\in\mathcal{P}_{x}\setminus\mathcal{Q}
, and writes
\mathcal{Q}_{z}=\mathcal{Q}\cup\{z\}
and
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}=\mathcal{Q}\cup G_{x}z
. If
z\notin\mathcal{P}_{\bar{x}}
one does nothing; otherwise, one performs an operation on the full subcategory of the current state of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}
and associated data over the objects of
\mathcal{Q}_{z}
; then, usually using transport of structure under a
G_{x}/G_{xz}
action (where
G_{xz}=Stab(x,z)\subset G
) one achieves the inductive hypothesis for
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}\supset\mathcal{Q}
. Because something is only done for
z\in\mathcal{P}_{\bar{x}}
, we may think of this as an induction over
z\in\mathcal{P}_{\bar{x}}
, or more appropriately over
\mathcal{P}_{\bar{x}}/G_{x}
.

Both inductions terminate because of finiteness of the number of objects of
\mathcal{C}
.

Application of inductive strategy to embeddings

.

Write
(\{V^{0}(x)\},\{V^{1}(x)\})
for the vector spaces the
E^{\prime}
-parameterization of the framing of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
. With notation as above, during the induction we will define
E
-parameterizations
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{P}}
such that if
\mathcal{P}_{2}\subset\mathcal{P}
is a downwards-closed
G
-sub-poset of
\mathcal{P}
then
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{P}}|_{\mathcal{P}_{2}}=\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{P}_{2}}
. We will write
\bar{V}_{\mathcal{P}}(x)
for the
G_{x}
-representation that
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{P}}
assigns to
x
for any
\mathcal{P}\ni x
as in the induction. Similarly, for any
y>x
in such a
\mathcal{P}
, we will write
\delta_{xy}:\bar{V}(y)\to\bar{V}(x)
for the corresponding embedding defined by
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{P}}
.

The inductive assumption to be verified for
\mathcal{P}
is that we have defined, for every pair of elements
x>y
in
\mathcal{P}
, smooth embeddings

\iota_{xy}:T(x,y)\to\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\bar{A}(x,y)}\times im\delta_{xy}^{\perp}

Moreover, these maps are required to satisfy the following properties A, B, C, D defined below.
Write
\bar{f}:T(x,y)\times T(y,z)\to T(x,z)
for the map associated to the composition
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(x,y)\times\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(y,z)\to\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(x,z)
for objects
x>y>z
of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(x,y)
.
Property A is compatibility of the embeddings
\iota_{xy}
with composition: we must have that for
x>y>z
objects of
\mathcal{P}
, we have that

\iota_{xz}\circ\bar{f}(a,b)=\iota_{xy}(a)+\delta_{xy}\iota_{yz}(b).

(54)

(where we make sense of the sum after rearranging the
\mathbb{R}_{+}
factors to be adjacent). Property B is the equivariance condition:

(id\times g_{x})\iota_{xz}=\iota_{xz}g_{xz}.

(55)

Property
C
is that the maps to
\mathbb{R}^{\bar{A}(x,y)}_{+}
induced by composing the
\iota_{xy}
with the projection have full rank differential, and near
T(x,y)_{j}
the corresponding component of the map is the distance from
T(x,y)_{j}
.

Finally, one requires that the analog of the disjointness condition on embeddings is satisfied for
\mathcal{P}
: for every
x\in\mathcal{P}
and any
a<\bar{A}(x)
, we have that the closures of the sets
Im\iota_{xy}\times V^{0}(y)
in
V^{0}(x)
are pairwise disjoint, as
y
runs over
\{y\in\mathcal{Q}:\bar{A}(y)=a\}
.

Assume that we have satisfied the inductive assumption for
\mathcal{P}
. As in the framework for the double induction, choose a minimum
x
of
\mathcal{C}\setminus\mathcal{P}
and write
\mathcal{P}_{x}
,
\mathcal{P}_{\bar{x}}
, and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
as above. The inner loop of the double induction runs over upwards-closed
G_{x}
-subposets
\mathcal{Q}\subset\mathcal{P}_{x}
. For each
\mathcal{Q}
, we define an
E
-parameterization
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q}}
of
\mathcal{C}|_{\mathcal{P}_{\bar{x}}}
by stabilizing
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{P}}
by a
G_{x}
-representation
V^{*}(x)
at
x
(Lemma
7
) which depends on
\mathcal{Q}
(although we suppress this dependence from the notation). We will initially set
V^{*}(x)
to be the zero vector space; at each step of the inner loop we will enlarge
V^{*}(x)
as a
G_{x}
-representation.

The inductive assumptions on the inner loop are that that for all
x>y
in
\mathcal{Q}\cup\mathcal{P}
we have chosen maps
\iota_{xy}
which satisfy property
B
and
C
above as well as property
A
for all
x>y>z
in
\mathcal{Q}\cup\mathcal{P}
, together with property D: that for
a<\bar{A}(x)
, the images of
\iota_{xy}\times V^{0}(y)
in
V^{0}(x)
are pairwise disjoint, as
y
runs over
\{y\in\mathcal{Q}:\bar{A}(y)=a\}
.

To proceed with the induction on the inner loop, we choose a
y\in\mathcal{C}
such that

•

the element
y
is a maximum of
\mathcal{P}_{\bar{x}}\setminus\mathcal{Q}
, and

•

\bar{A}(y)=\max_{y\in\mathcal{P}_{\bar{x}}\setminus\mathcal{Q}}\bar{A}(y).

We write
\mathcal{Q}_{y}=\mathcal{Q}\cup\{y\}
,
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}=\mathcal{Q}\cup Gy
.

Condition A for
\mathcal{Q}
lets us define a map

\iota_{xy}|_{\partial T(x,y)}:\partial T(x,y)\to\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\bar{A}_{x,y}}\times(\im\delta_{xy})^{\perp}

which automatically satisfies the equivariance condition. We would like the image of
\iota_{xy}|_{\partial T(x,y)}\times V^{0}(y)
in
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\bar{A}(x,y)}\times V^{0}(x)
to be disjoint from the images of all
\iota_{xy^{\prime}}\times V^{0}(y^{\prime})
as
y^{\prime}
runs over
\{y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Q}:\bar{A}(y)=\bar{A}(y^{\prime})\}
. If there are no such other
y^{\prime}
then this condition is vacuous. If there are such
y^{\prime}
then it turns out to be satisfied due to the inductive assumptions: if the images of these two sets for
y
and
y^{\prime}
overlap, then there must be a
z\in\mathcal{Q}
such that
x>z>y
,
z>y^{\prime}
(by considering the projections of these images to
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\bar{A}(x,a^{\prime})}\times V^{0}(x)
over various
a^{\prime}
with
\bar{A}(x)>a^{\prime}>a
, and using condition
D
for
\mathcal{Q}
). But then by condition
D
for
\mathcal{P}
and condition
A
for
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{Q}
these images will not overlap because the corresponding images of
\iota_{zy}\times V^{0}(y)
and
\iota_{zy^{\prime}}\times V^{0}(y^{\prime})
in
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\bar{A}(z,y)}\times V^{0}(z)
will not overlap.

We then enlarge
V^{*}(x)
sufficiently such that there is a
G_{xy}
-equivariant extension
\iota_{xy}
of
\iota_{xy}|_{\partial T(x,y)}
satisfying condition
C
, which satisfies the corresponding analog
D
of condition
D
, namely that

•

the
G_{x}
-orbit of the image of
\iota_{xy}\times V^{0}(y)
is disjoint from the images of
pr_{x}^{*}\iota_{xy^{\prime}}
as
y^{\prime}
, runs over the minima of
\mathcal{Q}
; and

•

Choosing representatives
\{g_{1},\ldots,g_{r}\}
of
G_{x}/G_{xy}
, the sets
g_{i}(\text{Im }\iota_{xy}\times V^{0}(y))=g_{i}(\text{Im}\iota_{xy})\times V^{0}(g_{i}y)
are disjoint as
i=1,\ldots,r
.

Such an extension exists by Lemma
5
; we extend
\iota_{xy}
such that the projection of the image of
\iota_{xy}|_{T(x,y)\setminus\partial T(x,y)}
to the
G_{xy}
-representation
V^{*}(x)
we are enlarging
V^{*}(x)
by does not intersect
0
, and then enlarge
V^{*}(x)
by
Ind_{G_{xy}}^{G_{x}}V^{*}(x)
after choosing this embedding.

For all
y^{\prime}\in G_{x}y^{\prime}
, we then define
\iota_{xy^{\prime}}
by requiring that the equivariance condition
B
holds; that these can be chosen to satisfy condition
D
is possible by the second part of condition
D^{\prime}
above. This completes the inductive step for the inner loop. After completing the inner loop (so
\mathcal{Q}=\mathcal{P}_{\bar{x}}
) we set
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}
to be the stabilization of
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{P}}
by the free representation which stabilizes by the final value of
\tilde{V}(x)
at
x
(as in Lemma
7
). Similarly, to complete the inductive step for the outer loop, for all
y\in\mathcal{P}_{\bar{x}}
and all
g\in G
, one defines
\iota_{gx,gy}
such that the equivariance condition holds.

Note that we have now constructed an
\mathcal{F}
-parameterization
\mathcal{F}^{new}
with underlying vector spaces
W(x,z)=\im\delta_{xz}^{\perp}
embedded in the the
E
-parameterization
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C}}
with underlying vector spaces
\{\bar{V}(x)\}
(Lemma
9
). We set
\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}=\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C}}}
(Definition
67
).

We now think of the maps
\iota_{xy}
as maps

\iota_{xy}:T_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}(x,y)\to\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\bar{A}(x,y)}\times V^{0}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}}(x,y).

This is possible because
V^{0}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}}(x,y)
is canonically identified with
(im(\hat{\alpha}^{0}_{xy})_{new})^{\perp}
, which contains

(im\delta_{xy})^{\perp}
. Because the metric on the underlying virtual smoothing is very convenient, we have that at every point
p\in im\iota_{xy}
, projection of
(d\iota_{xy})_{p}(\eta^{-1}_{xy}(0\oplus V^{0}(x,y)))
to
V^{0}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}}(x,y)_{p}
is full rank (since the projection of
(d\iota_{xy})(\eta_{xy}^{-1}(T\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\bar{A}(x,y)}\oplus 0))
to
T\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\bar{A}(x,y)}
is full rank). We write
\nu\iota_{xy}
for the union of the normals to the images of
(d\iota_{xy})_{p}(\eta^{-1}_{xy}(0\oplus V^{0}(x,y)))
over
p\in im\iota_{xy}
.

The
discussion of Section
5.4
then gives a family of isometric isomorphisms of bundles over
T_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}(x,y)
,

\kappa_{xy}:\underline{W^{0}(x,y)}\to\nu_{v}\iota_{xy}.

Using the Riemannian metrics on
V(x)
, by choosing a sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
, we can find diffeomorphisms
\phi_{xy}
from the
\epsilon
-neighboroods of the zero section of
\nu\iota_{xy}
to neighborhoods of
im\iota_{xy}
. We define
T_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime\prime}}(x,y)
to be the
\epsilon
-neighborhood of the zero section of
\underline{W^{0}(x,y)}
, and we define the final embedding
\iota_{xy}:T_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime\prime}}(x,y)\to V^{0}(x)
to the composition
\phi_{xy}\circ\kappa_{xy}
. The discussion about products in Section
5.4
shows precisely that these maps define an (equivariant) embedding of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime\prime}
.
∎

Lemma 19
.

Assume that
\mathcal{C}
is proper. After shrinking
\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime\prime}
, any embedding of
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime\prime})
produced as in Proposition
3
admits an extension.

Proof.

We take the shrinking to be some
\epsilon
-neighborhood of the zero-sets of the defining sections. After this,
this follows from an equivariant double induction, as in Proposition
3
, of the
G
-equivariant Tietze extension theorem. We omit the details; the only
crucial
fact is that the properness of the category is what allows us to use the Tietze exentsion theorem, which gives equivariant extensions of continuous maps from
closed
sets, which we take to be closed neighborhoods of various zero sets of Kuranishi sections.
∎

There are also several relative variants of Proposition
3
which are needed for the construction of continuation maps and for the geometric setup that allows us to construct the cyclotomic structure on symplectic cohomology. These are proven identically to Proposition
3
, but with a slightly modification of conditions
D
and
D^{\prime}
. We explain these statements and modifications below.

Proposition 4
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a
G
-equivariant virtually smooth framed flow category with with the framing parameterized by an
E_{2}^{s}
-parameterization. Suppose that the objects of
\mathcal{C}
can be partitioned into two disjoint
G
-invariant subsets
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}
such that

•

If
x\in\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
y\in\mathcal{C}_{2}
then either
x>y
or they are incomparable;

•

We have that
\max_{y\in\mathcal{C}_{2}}\bar{A}(y)<\min_{x\in\mathcal{C}_{1}}\bar{A}(x).

Write
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}
for the full flow subcategories of
\mathcal{C}
on the relevant objects, and suppose that (with the framings and embeddings of framings restricted from
\mathcal{C}
) there are embeddings and extensions of embeddings
\bar{\mathbf{E}}_{i}
and
\mathbf{E}_{i}
for
\mathcal{C}_{i}
for
i=1,2
, respectively. Then there exists an
E^{s}
-parameterization
\mathcal{E}
, an embedding
\mathbf{E}
of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{E}}
and an extension
\bar{E}
of
\mathbf{E}
such that the restriction of
\bar{\mathbf{E}}
and
\mathbf{E}
to
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{E},i}\subset\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{E}}
, the full subcategories on the objects of
\mathcal{C}_{i}
, agrees with the stabililized embedding
\mathbf{E}^{\mathcal{E}|_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{E},i}}}
and the stabilized extension
\bar{\mathbf{E}}^{\mathcal{E}|_{\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{E},i}}}
for
i=1,2
, respectively.

Proof.

This follows identically to the proofs of Proposition
3
and Lemma
19
, with a few key modifications. Write
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}
for the
E^{s}
-parameterization underlying the parameeterization of the framing of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
.

When defining
\mathbf{E}
, we first note that we can start the induction with
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{C}_{2}
and
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{P}}=\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}|_{\mathcal{P}}
. Note that all
E
-parameterizations are built by inductively stabilizing the zero
E
-parameterization upwards along the poset
\mathcal{C}
. Then, whenever we begin the outer loop on some
x\in\mathcal{C}_{1}
, before the first run of the inner loop we set
V^{*}(x)
to be a copy of the vector space that we would stabilize by to build
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}
from
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}|_{\mathcal{P}}
. With this choice, there will be canonical copy of
V^{0}(x,y)
for any
y\in\mathcal{C}_{1}
in
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{P};\{x\}}
, the stabilization of
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{P}}
by this vector space
V^{*}(x)
at
x
. The condition in the lemma statement on
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}
then means that we can start the second loop at
\mathcal{Q}=\mathcal{P}_{\bar{x}}\cap\mathcal{C}_{1}
, since we just choose the embeddings to be the composition of the original embeddings to
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\bar{A}(x,y)}\times V^{0}(x,y)
with the inclusions of the latter into
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\bar{A}(x,y)}\times V^{0}(x,y)\times V^{0}_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{P};\{x\}}}(x)
arising from this initial choice of
V^{*}(x)
. We then continue identically with the rest of the induction.

After the double induction has terminated, we use now stabilize the category and get trivializations of normal bundles as before; however we will choose
\epsilon
in a point-dependent way. Two conditions characterize our choice of
\epsilon
:

•

There is no restriction on the size
\epsilon
of the valid neighborhood for
x>y
in
\mathcal{C}_{1}
. In fact we choose
\epsilon=\infty
for those pairs. With this choice,
the nondisjointness condition for embeddings is still satisfied
for all
x\in\mathcal{C}_{1}
and all
a
such that
a>\max_{y\in\mathcal{C}_{2}}\bar{A}(y)
.

•

With this choice, for
x\in\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
y\in\mathcal{C}_{2}
we can simply shrink
\epsilon
as we move away from the boundary such that the nondisjointness condition is still satisfied.

Finally, we note that for
x>y
in
\mathcal{C}_{1}
, the embedding of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{E}}
produced by the above method
agrees
with the stabilized embedding, via direct linear algebra computation.

To produce
\bar{\mathbf{E}}
, we argue as before but choose the closed sets to which we apply the Tietze extension theorem to be the unions of the boundaries of
\partial(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\bar{A}(x,y)}\times V^{0}(x,y))_{*}
with closed neighborhoods of the zero locus.

∎

Remark 43
.

Analogs of Proposition
4
without the constraint of
\bar{A}
of that proposition can be produced by a
restratification
process, where one stabilizes
\mathcal{C}
, relabels the boundary strata so that the conditions of Proposition
4
hold, and proceeds onwards with the proof. We do not pursue this (technically useful but not immediately necessary) elaboration in this work. The constraint on
\bar{A}
can be seen as a partial ‘cofibrancy’ condition, of the form that ‘different boundary components are labeled by different strata’. A strong version of this condition would be that
the map
\bar{A}:Ob(\mathcal{C})\to\mathbb{Z}
is injective
, which arises automatically for the vitually-smooth flow categories produced by the method of Section
7
for
action-nondegenerate Hamiltonians
, i.e Hamiltonians for which all actions are distinct.

Remark 44
.

Given an embedded virtually smooth flow category
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{E}}
(where
\mathcal{E}
is the
E^{s}
-parameterization arising in Proposition
3
), one can use the linearizations of the embedding maps to define framings of the thickenings of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{E}}
. These framings will not agree with the original framings of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
, nor will they agree with the framings of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{E}}
induced by taking the framing of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
and then subsequently stabilizing it by
\mathcal{E}
. However, the final Floer homotopy type is still controlled by the original framings of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
(see Remark
32
), sufficiently so that in the geometric case we can prove a comparison with integral Floer homology in Section
10.2
(for which of course getting the framings correct is necessary, as the framings control the signs of the corresponding maps in a certain spectral sequence). More generally, it should be possible to prove that after a further sufficient stabilization, the framings produced by using the differentials of the embedding maps are deformation-equivalent in a suitable sense to the framings produced by taking the original framings of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
and stabilizing.

Proposition 5
.

Let
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
be a virtual smoothing of a
G
-equivariant flow category
\mathcal{C}
which has a semi-freely parameterized embedded framing. Suppose that for some normal subgroup
H\subset G
,
(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})^{H}
(thought of as a
G/H
-equivariant virtual smoothing of
\mathcal{C}^{H}
, with its induced framing and parameterization) has an (
G/H
-equivariant) embedding
\mathbf{E}_{H}
and an extension
\overline{\mathbf{E}}_{H}
. Then there is an
E^{s}
-parameterization
\mathcal{E}
such that
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{E}}
admits an embedding
\mathbf{E}
with extension
\overline{\mathbf{E}}
such that their restrictions to
(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{E}})^{H}=((\mathcal{C}^{\prime})^{H})_{\mathcal{E}^{H}}
are
\mathbf{E}^{\mathcal{E}^{H}}
and
\bar{\mathbf{E}}^{\mathcal{E}^{H}}
respectively.

Proof.

This follows identically to the proofs of Proposition
3
and Lemma
19
, with a few key modifications. As in the proof of Proposition
4
, before running the inner loop, at each step of the outer loop we initialize
V^{*}(x)
to be the stabilization which builds up
\mathcal{E}|_{(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})^{H}}
from zero, so that there is a copy of
V^{0}_{(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})^{H}}(x,y)
in
V_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{P};\{x\}}}(x)
. Using this we require that
\iota_{xy}
restrict to compositions of the embeddings
\iota_{xy}^{H}
of
(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})^{H}
with the inclusion
V^{0}_{(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})^{H}}(x,y)\subset V_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{P};\{x\}}}(x)
; this is possible by the inductive conditions and because
\iota_{xy}^{H}
is already
G_{xy}/H_{xy}
-equivariant. After the induction terminates, the one concludes similarly to the proof of Proposition
4
, choosing
\epsilon(x)
for
x\in T_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}(x,y)^{H}
to be infinity and having it decrease rapidly as one exits this locus, and similarly requiring that choosing the closed subsets to which the apply the Tietze extension theorem to be unions of closed neighborhoods of the zero-sections with
\mathbb{R}^{c}_{+}\times V^{0}(x,y)^{H_{xy}}
.
∎

Remark 45
.

Without the normality condition on
H
and the
G/H
-equivariance of the embedding and extension of
(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})^{H}
, it is not completely clear to the author if the corresponding analog of the proposition above holds, primarily because there may be a condition for extending a general map
M^{H}\to V^{H}
to a map
M\to V
for
M
a
G
-manifold and
V
a
G

G
-representation.

5.6
Restratfications.

Definition 68
.

A
restratification
of a flow category
\mathcal{C}_{1}
with an integral action
\bar{A}_{1}
is a flow category
\mathcal{C}_{2}
with an integral action
\bar{A}_{2}
such that
Ob(\mathcal{C}_{1})=Ob(\mathcal{C}_{2})
, such that the gradings (if defined) of the two flow categories agree, such that there are embeddings
\rho_{xy}:[\bar{A}_{1}(x,y)]\subset[\bar{A}_{2}(x,y)]
for each
(x,y)\in\mathcal{C}_{2}
and such that
\mathcal{C}_{2}(x,y)
is
\mathcal{C}_{1}(x,y)
as a space, with the stratification on
\mathcal{C}_{2}(x,y)
determined by

\begin{gathered}\mathcal{C}_{2}(x,y)([\bar{A}_{2}(x,y)]\setminus j)=\emptyset\text{ if }j\notin Im\rho_{xy},\\
\text{ and otherwise }\mathcal{C}_{2}(x,y)([\bar{A}_{2}(x,y)]\setminus j)=\mathcal{C}_{1}(x,y)([\bar{A}_{1}(x,y)]\setminus\rho_{xy}^{-1}(j)).\end{gathered}

with such that the composition maps in
\mathcal{C}_{2}
agreeing with the composition maps in
\mathcal{C}_{1}
if we think of these categories as enriched in spaces.

Definition 69
.

Suppose
\mathcal{C}_{1}
has an integral action. We can specify a restratification with the following construction.

Given a flow category
\mathcal{C}_{1}
, we can define a restratification
\mathcal{C}_{2}
associated to an order-preserving inclusion of sets

\rho:S_{\bar{A}}\to\{0,\ldots,\ell\}=:S_{\bar{A}_{2}}

for some integer
\ell>\max_{x\in\mathcal{C}}\bar{A}(x)-1
. This is defined by setting

\bar{A}_{2}(x)=\rho(\bar{A}_{1}(x))-1,

We call the integer
\ell-|S_{\bar{A}}|
the
defect
of the restratification.

Lemma 20
.

If
\mathcal{C}_{1}
has a virtual smoothing
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}
is a restratification of
\mathcal{C}_{1}
, then there is associated virtual smoothing
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{2}
of
\mathcal{C}_{2}
for which, writing
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{i}(x,y)=(T_{i}(x,y),V_{i}(x,y),\sigma_{i}(x,y))
, we have

T_{2}(x,y)=(-1,1)^{[\bar{A}_{2}(x,y)]\setminus\rho_{xy}([\bar{A}_{1}(x,y)])}\times T_{1}(x,y)

V_{2}(x,y)=\underline{\mathbb{R}^{[\bar{A}_{2}(x,y)\setminus\rho_{xy}([\bar{A}_{1}(x,y)])}}\oplus\pi_{T_{1}(x,y)}^{*}V_{1}(x,y),

\sigma_{2}(x,y)(a,b)=(id,\sigma_{1}(b))

where the stratification on
T_{2}(x,y)
is

T_{2}(x,y)([\bar{A}_{2}(x,y)]\setminus j)=(-1,1)^{[\bar{A}_{2}(x,y)]\setminus\rho_{xy}([\bar{A}_{1}(x,y)])}\times T_{1}(x,y)[\bar{A}_{1}(x,y)]\setminus\rho_{xy}^{-1}(j))

if
j\in Im\rho_{xy}
, and otherwise
T_{2}(x,y)([\bar{A}_{2}(x,y)]\setminus j)=\emptyset
. The composition is defined using product of the composition on
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{1}
and maps which use the embeddings

\rho_{xyz}:([\bar{A}_{2}(x,y)]\setminus Im\rho_{x,y})\cup([\bar{A}_{2}(y,z)]\setminus Im\rho_{y,z})\to([\bar{A}_{2}(x,z)]\setminus Im\rho_{x,z})

associated to the maps of sets defining composition in
\mathcal{C}_{2}
to compose the
(-1,1)^{[\bar{A}_{2}(x_{i},x_{j})]\setminus\rho_{xy}([\bar{A}_{1}(x_{i},x_{j})])}
factors and the
\mathbb{R}^{[\bar{A}_{2}(x_{i},x_{j})]\setminus\rho_{xy}([\bar{A}_{1}(x_{i},x_{j})])}
factors.

A framing of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{1}
defines a framing of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{2}
, as specified under (
58
) and (
59
) below. Similarly, an embedding of the framing of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{1}
, together with a choice of integer
M
, the
restratification embedding shift
, satisfying

0\leq M\leq\rho(0)

(56)

defines an embedding of the framing of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{2}
. If the parameterization of the framing of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{1}
was
E^{s}_{2}
and the restratification was simple then the same holds for
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{2}
. Finally, embeddings of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{1}
extend to embeddings of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{2}
canonically, as do extensions of embeddings.

Proof.

It is clear that the above definition defines a virtual smoothing of
\mathcal{C}
. We need to specify the framings. We set

V^{0}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}}(x,y)=V^{0}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}(x,y),\;\;V^{1}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}}(x,y)=\mathbb{R}^{[\bar{A}_{2}(x,y)\setminus\rho_{xy}([\bar{A}_{1}(x,y)])}\oplus V^{1}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}(x,y).

(57)

We set the trivialization

\eta^{2}_{xy}:TT_{2}(x,y)=T(-1,1)^{[\bar{A}_{2}(x,y)]\setminus\rho_{xy}([\bar{A}_{1}(x,y)])}\times TT_{1}(x,y)\to\underline{T\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{\bar{A}_{2}(x,y)}\times V^{0}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}}(x,y)

(58)

(where we use the natural reordering isomorphism on the right) to be
\eta^{2}_{xy}=id\times\eta_{xy}
, where we use that
T(0,1)=\underline{\mathbb{R}}
is canonically trivialized.

We set the trivialization

(\eta^{\prime})^{2}_{xy}:V_{2}(x,y)=\underline{\mathbb{R}^{[\bar{A}_{2}(x,y)\setminus\rho_{xy}([\bar{A}_{1}(x,y)])}}\oplus\pi_{T_{1}(x,y)}^{*}V_{1}(x,y)\to\mathbb{R}^{[\bar{A}_{2}(x,y)\setminus\rho_{xy}([\bar{A}_{1}(x,y)])}\oplus V^{1}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}(x,y)=V^{1}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}}(x,y)

(59)

to be the
(\eta^{\prime})^{2}_{xy}=id\times\eta_{xy}

We need to specify the parameterizations, and make sure that this new framing is embedded. We set

V^{0}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}}(x)=V^{0}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}(x),\;\;V^{1}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}}(x)=\mathbb{R}^{\bar{A}_{2}(x)-\bar{A}_{1}(x)-M}\times V^{1}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}(x).

The condition (
56
) ensures precisely that
\bar{A}_{2}(x)-\bar{A}_{1}(x)-M>0
for all
x
.
We set the embeddings

(\bar{\alpha}^{2})^{0}_{xy}:V^{0}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}}(x,y)\oplus V^{0}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}}(y)\to V^{0}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}}(x)

to be
\bar{\alpha}^{2}_{xy}=\bar{\alpha}_{xy}
, and

(\bar{\alpha}^{2})^{1}_{xy}:V^{1}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}}(x,y)\oplus V^{1}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}}(y)=\mathbb{R}^{[\bar{A}_{2}(x,y)\setminus\rho_{xy}([\bar{A}_{1}(x,y)])}\times V^{1}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}(x,y)\times\mathbb{R}^{\bar{A}_{2}(y)-\bar{A}_{1}(y)-M}\times V^{1}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}(y)\to\mathbb{R}^{\bar{A}_{2}(x)-\bar{A}_{1}(x)-M}\times V^{1}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}}(x)

by noting that

\bar{A}_{2}(x,y)-\bar{A}_{1}(x,y)=(\bar{A}_{2}(x)-\bar{A}_{1}(x)+M)-(\bar{A}_{2}(y)-\bar{A}_{1}(y)-M)

and simply moving switching the third factor of the domain with the second and then applying
\bar{\alpha}^{1}_{xy}
to the latter two factors.
Writing
(\mathcal{F}_{0}^{\prime},\mathcal{F}_{1}^{\prime})
for the
F^{\prime}_{2}
-parameterization of the framing of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{2}
and
(\mathcal{F}_{0},\mathcal{F}_{1})
for the
F^{\prime}_{2}
parameterization of the framing of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{1}
, we see that
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{0}=\mathcal{F}_{0}
. If
\mathcal{F}_{1}
was an
F^{s}
parameterization then
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{1}
is as well: the free parts are the same, while the canonical part of
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{1}
(with defining function
f^{\prime}_{1}
, and the defining function of the canonical part of
\mathcal{F}_{1}
being
f_{1}
) by setting
f^{\prime}_{1}(\rho(n))=f_{1}(n)
and otherwise
f^{\prime}_{1}(n)=1
for
n\in S_{\bar{A}_{2}}\setminus\im\rho
.

We extend embeddings of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{1}
to embeddings of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{2}
by setting

\iota_{xy}:(-1,1)^{[\bar{A}_{2}(x,y)]\setminus\rho_{xy}([\bar{A}_{1}(x,y)])}\times T_{1}(x,y)\to\mathbb{R}_{+}^{[\bar{A}_{2}(x,y)]\setminus\rho_{xy}([\bar{A}_{1}(x,y)])}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{[\bar{A}_{1}(x,y)]}\times V^{0}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{1}}(x,y)=\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\bar{A}_{2}(x,y)}\times V^{0}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{1}}(x,y)

as
\iota_{xy}(a,p)=(a+(2,\ldots,2),\iota_{xy}(p))
where the
\iota_{xy}
inside the parentheses is the original embedding map for
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{1}
, and
(2,\ldots,2)
is the vector containing only twos. We will choose, once and for all, a function

\xi:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{*}

(60)

which extends continuously to
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}
as a pointed map, and such that
\xi^{-1}(\infty)=0
(and thus
\xi
descends to a homeomorphism
\bar{\xi}=(\mathbb{R}_{+})_{*}/\{0,\infty\}\to\mathbb{R}_{*}
).
Namely, we set

\xi|_{(1,\infty)}(p)=p-2,\xi(0)=\infty,\xi|_{0,1}(p)=-1/p.

We set the new extension of the embedding
e^{2}_{xy}
be

e^{2}_{xy}=((\xi,\xi,\ldots,\xi)\wedge e_{xy})

(61)

where we have used the decomposition (
57
) of
V^{1}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}}(x,y)
as well as (
30
).
∎

Lemma 21
.

Given a restratification
\mathcal{C}_{2}
of a flow category
\mathcal{C}_{1}
with associated embeddings
\rho^{1}_{xy}:[\bar{A}_{1}(x,y)]\to[\bar{A}_{2}(x,y)]
and a restratification
\mathcal{C}_{3}
of
\mathcal{C}_{2}
with associated embeddings
\rho^{2}_{xy}:[\bar{A}_{1}(x,y)]\to[\bar{A}_{2}(x,y)]
,
\mathcal{C}_{3}
is also a restratification of
\mathcal{C}_{1}
with associated embeddings
\rho^{12}_{xy}=\rho^{2}_{xy}\circ\rho^{1}_{xy}
. Moreover, the virtual smoothing of
\mathcal{C}_{3}
associated to
\rho^{12}_{xy}
by Lemma
20
and the virtual smoothing of
\mathcal{C}_{3}
built by first applying Lemma
20
to
\rho^{1}_{xy}
and subsequently to
\rho^{2}_{xy}
, are canonically isomorphic, and there is an isomorpism of the parameterizations of the framings and of the embeddings of the framings which takes one framing to the other, and takes the embeddings and extensions of embeddings from one to the other.

Proof.

(Sketch) We do not spell out all the details of this compositionality result. However, it follows straightforwardly from the canonical identification of sets

[\bar{A}_{3}(x,y)]\setminus\rho^{12}_{xy}([\bar{A}_{1}(x,y)])=([\bar{A}_{3}(x,y)]\setminus\rho^{2}_{xy}([\bar{A}_{2}(x,y)]))\cup([\bar{A}_{2}(x,y)]\setminus\rho_{xy}([\bar{A}_{1}(x,y)])).

∎

Proposition 6
.

The effect of a restratification on the floer homotopy space is

\{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{2}\}\simeq\Sigma^{\mathbb{R}^{S_{\bar{A}_{2}}\setminus\rho(S_{\bar{A}_{1}})}}\{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{1}\}\simeq\Sigma^{\ell}\{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{1}\}

(62)

|\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{2}|\simeq F_{\mathbb{R}^{\ell+M}}\Sigma^{\ell}|\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{1}|.

Proof.

Write

S^{\prime}_{<a}=\{a^{\prime}\in S_{\bar{A}_{2}}\setminus\rho(S_{\bar{A}_{1}}):a^{\prime}<a\},S^{\prime}_{>a}=\{a^{\prime}\in S_{\bar{A}_{2}}\setminus\rho(S_{\bar{A}_{1}}):a^{\prime}>a\}.

Then for
a\in S_{\mathfrak{A}_{1}}
, under a restratification, the term corresponding to
z
in the wedge summand (
35
) changes by taking smash product with

(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{S^{\prime}_{<\bar{A}_{2}(z)}}\times\mathbb{R}^{S^{\prime}_{>\bar{A}_{2}(z)}})_{*}

(63)

with the second factor in the product coming from the change in
V^{1}(x)^{\perp}
. For
z\in M(\mathcal{C})
the boundary of the
\mathbb{R}_{+}
factors is collapsed to the basepoint; thus via
\bar{\xi}
(see (
60
)) we can identify the effect of those factors as simply a smash product with
(\mathbb{R}^{S^{\prime}_{<\bar{A}_{2}(z)}})_{*}
; thus we simply suspend the cells of
\{\mathcal{C}_{1}\}
corresponding to
z\in M(\mathcal{C})
by
\mathbb{R}^{S_{\bar{A}_{2}}\setminus\rho(S_{\bar{A}_{1}})}
, as desired.

For the other cells, we can use
\bar{\xi}
to convert the
\mathbb{R}_{+}
factors in (
63
) corresponding to
\{s\in S_{\bar{A}_{2}}\setminus:s<\min\rho(S_{\bar{A}_{1}})\}
, as before. For the other factors, we see that we are exactly using
\xi
in
(
61
)
to define the action of the extension of the embedding, which is used to define take the quotient in (
35
); thus, identifying those factors with
\mathbb{R}
factors via
\xi
as well is consistent with the quotient in (
35
). This establishes (
62
). The rest of the claim follows from computing the change in the shift space under the assignment (
57
).
∎

6
Review of (Hamiltonian) Floer Homology

Hamiltonian Floer Homology, like other Floer theoretic invariants, arises formally from the study of the gradient flow of a closed
1
-form on an infinite-dimensional manifold. Specifically, let
(M^{2n},\omega)
be a symplectic manifold, such that
\omega
has a chosen primitive
1
-form
\lambda
. To a
t
-dependent Hamiltonian function

H=H_{t}:M\times S^{1}_{t}\to\mathbb{R}

Hamilton’s equations

dH=-i_{X_{H}}\omega

(64)

associate a
t
-dependent Hamiltonian vector field
X_{H}=X_{H_{t}}
with associated time
t
flows given by
\phi^{t}_{H}
. The
1
-periodic trajectories of the flow of
X_{H}
are fixed points of
\phi^{1}_{H}
, or equivalently the solutions to

x:S^{1}\to M,\frac{dx}{dt}=X_{H_{t}}.

(65)

Thinking of the periodic trajectories as elements of the free loop space
LM
, which behaves much like an infinite-dimensional manifold, one notices that (
65
) are formally the equations for the critical points of the symplectic action functional

\mathcal{A}_{H}:LM\to\mathbb{R},\mathcal{A}_{H}(x)=\int_{x}\lambda-x^{*}Hdt.

(66)

Formally, the differential of the symplectic action functional takes the form

d\mathcal{A}_{H}:TLM\to\mathbb{R},d\mathcal{A}_{H}(x,v)=\int_{0}^{1}\omega(\dot{x},v)-dH(v)dt

(67)

where
x:S^{1}\to M
is a point of the free loop space
LM
and
v\in C^{\infty}(x^{*}TM)
is a tangent vector field along
x
, i.e. an element of
T_{x}LM
. Picking a
S^{1}_{t}
-dependent family
J_{t}
of almost complex structures on
M
which are all compatible with
\omega
defines a
t
-dependent metric on
M
, and thus a metric on
LM
via

\langle v,w\rangle=\int_{0}^{1}\omega_{x(t)}(v(t),J_{t}w(t))dt

(68)

where
x\in LM
and
v,w\in T_{x}LM
. The downwards gradient flow trajectories of the symplectic action functional with respect to this metric are thus formally given by solutions to Floer’s equations

u:Z\to M,\partial_{s}u+J_{t}(\partial_{t}u-X_{H_{t}})=\partial_{s}u+J_{t}\partial_{t}u-\nabla H_{t}=0

(69)

a
where
Z=\mathbb{R}_{s}\times S^{1}_{t}
is thought of a holomorphic cylinder with complex structure
i
such that
i\partial_{s}=\partial_{t}
.

Let
Fix(H)
denote the solutions to (
65
); these are in canonical bijection with their starting points
x(0)
. The Hamiltonian
H_{t}
is
nondegenerate
if
(d\phi^{1}_{H})_{x(0)}
has no eigenvalues equal to
1
for all
x\in Fix(H)
. Formally, the Hessian of
\mathcal{A}_{H}
is given by

Hess(\mathcal{A}_{H})(x;v,w)=\int_{0}^{1}\langle J_{t}\nabla_{t}v+(\nabla_{v(t)}J_{t})\partial_{t}u-\nabla_{v}\nabla H_{t},w(t)\rangle dt

(70)

where
x\in LM
and
v,w\in T_{x}LM
. Just as in the context of finite-dimensional Morse theory, where Hessians of functions are self-adjoint with respect to the metric, the operator

\xi\mapsto J_{t}\nabla_{t}v+(\nabla_{v(t)}J_{t})\partial_{t}u-\nabla_{v}\nabla H_{t}

(71)

is a formally self-adjoint operator with respect to the inner product (
68
).

Remark 46
.

The formal self-adjointness of the operator (
71
) is not obvious, but can be established with the same computation that establishes the self-adjointness of the Hessian
v\mapsto\nabla_{v}\nabla f
for
f
a Morse function on a finite-dimensional manifold. Namely, it follows from the torsion-freeness of the Levi-Civita connection, together with the identity

\begin{gathered}g(\nabla_{X}\nabla f,Y)-g(X,\nabla_{Y}\nabla f)=Xg(\nabla f,Y)-g(\nabla f,\nabla_{X}Y)-Yg(\nabla f,X)+g(\nabla f,\nabla_{Y}X)\\
=X(Y(f))-Y(X(f))-[X,Y]f=0.\end{gathered}

for
g
a metric and
X,Y
vector fields.

An immediate observation from the formula (
71
) is that if
H
is nondegenerate then
\mathcal{A}_{H}
behaves formally like a Morse function. Making this assumption forces
Fix(H)
to be finite if
M
is compact (possibly with boundary) or if
H
satisfies certain standard conditions near infinity which will be spelled out later. For any pair
x_{\pm}\in Fix(H)
, one then defines moduli spaces
\mathcal{M}(x_{-},x_{+})
as the sets of solutions to (
69
) satisfying the asymptotic conditions

\lim_{s\to\pm\infty}u(s,t)=x_{\pm}(t).

(72)

Under standard conditions described below, the Gromov-Floer compactifications
\overline{\mathcal{M}}(x_{-},x_{+})
of
\mathcal{M}(x_{-},x_{+})
are compact Hausdorff spaces, and come with gluing maps

\overline{\mathcal{M}}(x_{0},x_{1})\times\ldots\times\overline{\mathcal{M}}(x_{k-1},x_{k})\to\overline{\mathcal{M}}(x_{0},x_{k})

for any
(x_{0},\ldots,x_{k})\in Fix(H)^{k+1}
. The specializations of the gluing maps to
k=2
define the compositions of the
pre-flow category

\mathcal{C}(H,J)
associated to
(H,J)
, which is a-priori just a topologically-enriched category, which has objects
Fix(H)
and morphisms
\mathcal{C}(H,J)(x_{-},x_{+})=\overline{\mathcal{M}}(x_{-},x_{+})
.

Remark 47
.

This is not yet a flow category in the sense of Definition
14
because we need to pick a convention for stratifying the spaces
\overline{\mathcal{M}}(x_{-},x_{+})
. When the Floer datum
(H,J)
is regular, it is conventional to stratify by the difference of the Conley-Zehnder indices of the endpoints; however, this does not make sense for irregular Floer data since the index of a curve
u\in\overline{\mathcal{M}}(x_{-},x_{+})
may be negative.

Conditions on symplectic manifolds and Floer data.

Definition 70
.

A
Liouville domain
is a compact exact symplectic manifold with boundary
(M,\omega,\lambda)
such that the Liouville vector field
X
defined by
i_{X}\omega=\lambda
points outward along
\partial M
.

Under this condition
\lambda|_{\partial M}
a contact form on
\partial M
. Moreover,
The flow of the vector field
X
defines a collar
[\epsilon,1]_{t}\times\partial M
around
\partial M
sending
1
to
\partial M
and such that

\lambda|_{[\epsilon,1]_{t}\times\partial M}=e^{t}\lambda|_{\partial M},

X|_{[\epsilon,1]_{t}\times\partial M}=\partial/\partial t.

A symplectic manifold with with contact boundary is a sympectic manifold with boundary such that an open neighborhood of its boundary is Liouville.

A
symplectically atoroidal
manifold
M
is a symplectic manifold
(M,\omega)
such that
\int_{T^{2}}u^{*}\omega=0
for every differentiable map
u:T^{2}\to M
. Note that Liouville domains are necessarily symplectically atoroidal.

An
admissible
symplectic manifold
M
is a symplectic manifold that is symplectically atoroidal with contact boundary.

Definition 71
.

Let
M
be an admissible symplectic manifold.
A pair
(H,J)
, where
H
is an
S^{1}
-dependent Hamiltonian on
M
, and
J
is an
S^{1}
-dependent almost complex structure taming
\omega
, is a
Floer datum
. In the case when
M
has contact boundary, we say that
(H,J)
is a
convex linear Floer datum
if
(after possibly shrinking the collar

•

H|_{[\epsilon,1]_{t}\times\partial M}=ct
for constant
c>0
, called the
slope
of
H
, and

•

On the image of the collar,
J
preserves
\ker\lambda
and sends
X
to the Reeb vector field of
\lambda|_{\partial M}
.

A Floer datum is
nondegenerate
if
H^{\#k}
is nondegenerate for all
k
, and (if
M
is Liouville none of Reeb orbits of
\lambda|_{\partial M}
has period an integral multiple of the slope of
H
.

An
admissible
Floer datum
(H,J)
is a nondegenerate Floer datum which is convex linear if
M
is Liouville.

Definition 72
.

Given a pair of admissible Floer data
(H_{\pm},J_{\pm})
such that
H_{\pm}
has slope
c_{\pm}
, an
admissible continuation datum
(from
(H_{+},J_{+})
to
(H_{-},J_{-})
) is a family
(H_{s},J_{s})
of admissible Floer data for
s\in\mathbb{R}
such that

•

The functions
H^{s}:\mathbb{R}\times S\times S^{1}\to\mathbb{R}
and
J^{s}:\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\times M\to\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\times End(TM)
are smooth, and

•

(H^{s},J^{s})=(H_{\pm},J_{\pm})
for
\pm s>>0
, and finally,

•

(if
M
is has contact boundary) slopes
c_{s}
of
H_{s}
are non-increasing, i.e.
c^{\prime}_{s}\leq 0
.

It is a standard result
[
4
]
that the Gromov-Floer bordifications of moduli spaces of Floer trajectories with Hamiltonian
H
and complex structure
J
are compact when
(H,J)
an admissible Floer datum on an admissible symplectic manifold, as are the Gromov-Floer bordifications of continuation maps defined using
(H_{s},J_{s})
when
(H_{s},J_{s})
are an admissible continuation datum.

Remark 48
.

In the case where
M
is not exact, the action functional (
66
) is not defined in general, but is only defined up to a constant. To make sense of the action functional, one chooses basepoints on each component of
LM
and integrates the expression under the integral sign for
d\mathcal{A}_{H}
(
67
) over a map from
S^{1}\times I
with one end sent to the basepoint loop and the other to the given loop
\gamma
. This gives a well-defined value for
\mathcal{A}_{H}(\gamma)
due to the condition of being symplectically atoroidal. The rest of the discussion in this paper makes sense with this convention.

Definition 73
