∎

Compatible smoothings.

Lemma 36
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be an
E^{s}
-parameterization such that there is a smooth structure on
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H_{s},J_{s})_{\mathcal{E}}
. There is an
E^{s}
-parameterization
\mathcal{E}_{k}
such that there is a smooth structure on
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H_{s}^{\#k},J_{s}^{\#k})_{\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}
such that the corresponding smooth structure on
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H_{s}^{\#k},J_{s}^{\#k})_{\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}^{C_{k}}
agrees (possibly after restriction) with that induced on

\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H_{s},J_{s})_{\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}
from that chosen on
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H_{s},J_{s})_{\mathcal{E}}
(also possibly both under restriction).

Proof.

This follows from a very slight modification of the proof of Proposition
15
. First one notes that the subspaces

(T_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H_{s}^{\#k},J_{s}^{\#k})})_{\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q}}}(x,y)^{C_{k}}\subset(T_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H_{s}^{\#k},J_{s}^{\#k})})_{\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q}}}(x,y)

are nice submanifolds. One chooses a compatible system of elegant metrics on the thickenings (such that the maps on thickenings associated to composition are isometries); then, using Lemma
, one sees that one has for every
x,y
, a system of tubular neighborhoods of the inclusions above which one can use to deform the standard microbundle lift to one which restricts to the smooth microbundle lift coming from the chosen elegant metrics
TT_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H_{s}^{\#k},J_{s}^{\#k})})_{\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q}}}(x,y)^{C_{k}}
, mapping the latter to
T_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H_{s}^{\#k},J_{s}^{\#k})})_{\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{P},\mathcal{Q}}}(x,y)^{C_{k}}
. One then runs the induction of Proposition
15
using these nice submanifolds with their elegant metrics in all propositions invoked.
∎

7.10
Composition of continuation maps

In this section, we explain how to generalize the construction of Section
7.8
to produce virtually smooth flow categories witnessing the existence of homotopies between compositions of continuation maps. Specifically, suppose we are given

•

four choices of admissible Floer data
((H_{1},J_{1}),(H_{2},J_{2}),(H_{3},J_{3}),(H_{4},J_{4}))
;

•

four choices of admissible continuation data
(H_{ab},J_{ab})
from
(H_{b},J_{b})
to
(H_{a},J_{a})
, where
(a,b)\in\{(1,2),(1,3),(2,4),(3,4)\}
, and

•

A
(admissible) continuation homotopy datum

(H_{h},J_{h})
defined as follows. Note that there is a diffeomorphism
\phi:\overline{Conf}_{2}\simeq\mathbb{R}_{+}
: the boundary point corresponds to a curve with two components, each with a marked point, and as we move away from the boundary, the two components merge into one and the two marked points move closer together. Let us write
\overline{Conf}_{2,h,0}
and
\overline{Conf}_{2,h,1}
for two copies of
\phi^{-1}([0,1/2])
. We can defined structure of a
\langle 1\rangle
-manifold on

\overline{Conf}_{2,h}=\overline{Conf}_{2,h,0}\cup_{\phi^{-1}(1/2)}\overline{Conf}_{2,h,1}

by requiring that the boundary of
\overline{Conf}_{2,h}
is the union of the boundaries of
\overline{Conf}_{2,h,0}
and of
\overline{Conf}_{2,h,1}
, and more generally that there is a diffeomorphism

\tilde{\tau}:\overline{Conf}_{2,h}\to[0,1],\tilde{\tau}(x)|_{\overline{Conf}_{2,h,0}}=\phi(x),\tilde{\tau}(x)|_{\overline{Conf}_{2,h,1}}=1-\phi(x).

Over
\overline{Conf}_{2,h}
lie the universal curves
\overline{Conf}_{2,h}^{Z},\overline{Conf}_{2,h}^{\bar{Z}},
and so forth, which are the union of the corresponding universal curves lying over
\overline{Conf}_{2,h,0}
and
\overline{Conf}_{2,h,1}
.

The chosen continuation data
(H_{ab},J_{ab})
in fact define smooth maps

H_{h}:\partial\overline{Conf}_{2,h}^{\bar{\mathbb{R}}}\times M\to\mathbb{R},

J_{h}:\partial\overline{Conf}_{2,h}^{\bar{\mathbb{R}}}\to\mathcal{J}(M)

where
\mathcal{J}(M)
is the space of almost complex structures on
M
. These maps are defined by requiring that on
\partial(\overline{Conf}_{2,h}^{\bar{\mathbb{R}}})_{\tilde{\tau}^{-1}(0)}
,
H_{h}
is
H_{12}
on the first component and
H_{24}
on the second component, under the diffeomorphisms sending the corresponding component of the universal curve to
Z
with the marked coordinate sent to
s=0
. Similarly, on
\partial(\overline{Conf}_{2,h}^{\bar{\mathbb{R}}})_{\tilde{\tau}^{-1}(1)}
, we require that
H_{h}
agrees with
H_{13}
on the first component and
H_{34}
on the second component under the corresponding diffemorphisms with
Z
. Finally we define
J^{h}
in the same way, replacing
H^{h}
with
H^{h}
and
H_{ab}
with
H^{ab}
in the discussion above.

The data of the continuation homotopy datum
(H_{h},J_{h})

is the data of a smooth extension of the maps defined above to the domains

\overline{Conf}_{2,h}^{\bar{Z}}\times M
.

We can symbolically describe this data by the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{4}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{12}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{13}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{24}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{43}}

where the double arrow corresponds to
H_{h}
.

From this data, one can produce a pre-flow category
\mathcal{C}(H_{h},J_{h})
with objects the disjoint union of the objects of
\mathcal{C}(H_{a},J_{a})
for
a=1,2,3,4
, and such that the full subcategories on the objects of
\mathcal{C}(H_{a},J_{a})
and
\mathcal{C}(H_{b},J_{b})
for
(a,b)\in\{(1,2),(1,3),(2,4),(3,4)\}
agree with
\mathcal{C}(H_{ab},J_{ab})
, by setting, for
x_{-}\in Fix(H_{1})
and
x_{+}\in Fix(H_{4})
,

\mathcal{C}(H_{h},J_{h})(x_{-},x_{+})=\overline{\{(u,a),u:(\overline{Conf}_{2,h}^{Z})_{a}\to M,a\in\overline{Conf}_{2,h}:\partial_{s}u+J_{h}(a,s)\partial_{t}u=\nabla H_{h}^{(}a),\lim_{s\to\pm\infty}u(s,t)=x_{\pm}(s),\}}

where for
a\in\tilde{\tau}^{-1}(\{0,1\})
, the condition is imposed on each component of the universal curve, and two boundary conditions are imposed at the two opposite ends of the two components of the map from the universal curve in the natural way; and the overline denotes the Gromov-Floer compactification of this parameterized moduli space.

In this section, we explain how to adapt the construction of Section
7.8
to produce a flow category lift of
\mathcal{C}(H_{h},J_{h})
, and a virtual smoothing of this flow category lift, in a way that is compatible with given virtual smoothings of
\mathcal{C}(H_{ab},H_{ab})
and also with
k
-iteration operations which relate
(H_{a},J_{a})
to
(H^{\#k}_{a},J^{\#k}_{a})

We will follow the general strategy of
[
85
]
to produce this virtual smoothing. We will explain the geometric setup for this virtual smoothing in some detail; however, because the proofs of the required properties are almost identical to those given in Section
7.8
, we will only write out a summary of the argument, with few comments indicating small modifications that may need to be made from proofs given earlier.

Integralization data for continuation homotopies.

Note that continuation homotopy data
(H_{h},J_{h})
define a map

\mathcal{A}_{h}:\overline{Conf}_{2,h}^{\bar{\mathbb{R}}}\times LM\to\mathbb{R}.

using the modified actions from
\bar{\mathfrak{A}}_{12}
and
\bar{\mathfrak{A}}_{24}
over
\tilde{\tau}^{-1}(0)
and the modified actions from
\bar{\mathfrak{A}}_{13}
and
\bar{\mathfrak{A}}_{34}
over
\tilde{\tau}^{-1}(1)
.

Definition 86
.

An
integralization datum
for
\mathcal{C}(H_{h},J_{h})
is a tuple
(\mathcal{A}_{h},\mathfrak{A}_{h},\{A^{1}_{c},A^{2}_{c}\})
such that

(A)

There exists a function
c:\overline{Conf}_{2,h}^{\bar{\mathbb{R}}}\to\mathbb{R}
which takes constant values near the nodes and which has negative nonzero derivative at all
s
coordinates for which
\partial_{s}H_{h}
is nonzero, such that

(B)

The quantity
\mathcal{A}_{h}
is a function

\mathcal{A}_{h}:\overline{Conf}_{2,h}^{\bar{\mathbb{R}}}\times LM\to\mathbb{R}

given by

\mathcal{A}_{h}:=D\mathcal{A}_{H_{h}}+c(s)

for some constant
D>0
; in addition,

(C)

For every pair of objects
x>y
of
\mathcal{C}(H_{h},J_{h})
with
x\in\mathcal{C}(H_{1},J_{1})
and
y\in\mathcal{C}(H_{4},J_{4})
,
for every
(u,a)\in\mathcal{C}(H_{h},J_{h})(x,y)
, and every
s\in(\overline{Conf}_{2,h}^{\mathbb{R}})_{a}
, we have that

\frac{d}{ds}\mathcal{A}_{h}(u_{s})<0\text{ for all }s.

(112)

(D)

The function
\mathcal{A}_{h}
defines four associated functions

\mathcal{A}_{h,ab}:\mathbb{R}\times LM\to\mathbb{R}

for
(a,b)\in\{(1,2),(1,3),(2,4),(1,4)\}
, by restricting
\mathcal{A}_{h}
in the components of the universal curves over
(\overline{Conf}_{2,h}^{Z})_{0}
and
(\overline{Conf}_{2,h}^{Z})_{0}
, so
\mathcal{A}_{h,ab}
is related to
\mathcal{A}_{H_{ab}}
by an affine-linear reparameterization of its codoomain. We require that for each
(a,b)
as above, the tuple
(\mathcal{A}_{h,ab},\mathfrak{A}_{h,ab},\{A^{j_{ab}}_{c}\})
is an integralization datum for
\mathcal{C}(H_{ab},J_{ab})
where

\begin{gathered}j_{12}=j_{13}=1,j_{24}=j_{34}=2,\\
\mathfrak{A}_{12}=\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}_{1}\cup\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}_{2},\mathfrak{A}_{13}=\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}_{1}\cup\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}_{2}\cup\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}_{3},\\
\mathfrak{A}_{24}=\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}_{2}\cup\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}_{3}\cup\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}_{4},\mathfrak{A}_{34}=\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}_{3}\cup\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}_{4}.\end{gathered}

(113)

Lemma 37
.

Suppose that we are given integralization data
\bar{\mathfrak{A}}_{ab}=(\mathcal{A}_{ab},\mathfrak{A}_{ab},\{\rho^{x}\})
for
\mathcal{C}(H_{ab},J_{ab})
with
(a,b)\in\{(1,2),(1,3),(2,4),(3,4)\}
, such that the induced pairs of integralization data on
\mathcal{C}(H_{i},J_{i})
agree for each of
i=1,2,3,4
. Write

\mathcal{A}_{ab}=D\mathcal{A}_{H_{a}b}+c_{ab},\lim_{s\to-\infty}c_{ab}=C_{a},\lim_{s\to+\infty}=C_{b}

and

\mathfrak{A}_{ab}=(\mathfrak{A}_{a}+C_{a})\sqcup(\mathfrak{A}_{b}+C_{b})

for the corresponding decompositions of marked actions. Suppose further that the following conditions are satisfied:

C_{a}-C_{b}>\left(\max_{x\in\mathcal{C}(H_{c},J_{c})}D\mathcal{A}_{H_{c}}(x)+C_{c}\right)-\left(\min_{x\in\mathcal{C}(H_{a},J_{a})}D\mathcal{A}_{H_{a}}(x)+C_{a}\right)

(114)

C_{a}-C_{c}>\left(\max_{x\in\mathcal{C}(H_{b},J_{b})}D\mathcal{A}_{H_{b}}(x)+C_{b}\right)-\left(\min_{x\in\mathcal{C}(H_{a},J_{a})}D\mathcal{A}_{H_{a}}(x)+C_{a}\right)

(115)

C_{a}-C_{d}>\sup_{\tau\in(0,1)}\int_{(\overline{Conf}_{2,h}^{Z})_{\tilde{\tau}^{-1}(\tau)}}\max_{(x,t)\in M\times S^{1}}|\partial_{s}H_{h}(s,x,t)|ds.

(116)

Then there exists an integralization datum
\bar{\mathfrak{A}}_{h}
such that the associated integralization data for each
\mathcal{C}(H_{ab},J_{ab})
are
\bar{\mathfrak{A}}_{ab}
.

Proof.

The first two conditions above mean that the extension of
\mathfrak{A}_{ab}
by the new marked actions
\mathfrak{A}_{c}+C_{b}
to the marked actions is still an integralization datum from
\mathcal{C}(H_{ab},J_{ab})
, and similarly with
b
swapped with
c
everywhere. The only challenge is thus to define the function
c
(Definition
86
(A)); this can be defined by the gluing construction for points projecting to points near the boundary of
\overline{Conf}_{2,h}
, and the last condition in the lemma allows us to extend
c
to the remaining locus.
∎

Lemma 38
.

Suppose we are given
\bar{\mathfrak{A}}_{h}=(D\mathcal{A}_{H_{h}}+c,\mathfrak{A}_{h},\ \{A^{1}_{c},A^{2}_{c}\})
for
\mathcal{C}(H_{h},J_{h})
. Write
C_{a},C_{b},C_{c},C_{d}
and so forth as in the statement of Lemma
37
, and suppose that

•

for each
(\alpha,\beta)
in
((a,b),(a,c),(b,c),(b,d))
, the data
(C_{\alpha},C_{\beta},A^{j_{ab}}_{c})
satisfy the condition required in Lemma
33
with respect to the integralization datum
\bar{\mathfrak{A}}_{ab}
associated by
\mathfrak{A}_{h}
to
\mathcal{C}(H_{ab},J_{ab})
, and

•

The condition

C_{a}-C_{d}>DC_{H_{a}}+DC_{H_{d}}+max_{\tau\in(0,1)}\int_{(\overline{Conf}_{2,h}^{Z})_{\tilde{\tau}^{-1}(\tau)}}\max_{(x,t)\in M\times S^{1}}|\partial_{s}H(s,x,t)|ds,

is satisfied
where
C_{H_{a}}
and
C_{H_{d}}
are the constants associated by Lemma
31
to
H_{a}
and
H_{d}
, respectively.

Then there exists a set
\mathfrak{A}^{k}_{h,new}\subset\mathbb{R}\setminus k\mathfrak{A}_{h}
and such that

\bar{\mathfrak{A}}_{h}^{k}=(D\mathcal{A}_{H_{h}^{\#k}}+kc,k\mathfrak{A}_{h}\cup\mathfrak{A}^{k}_{h,new},\{kA^{1}_{c},kA^{2}_{c}\})

is an integralization datum for
\mathcal{C}(H_{h}^{\#k},J_{h}^{\#k})
.

Proof.

The argument is essentially identical to that of Lemma
33
.
∎

Stratifying the pre-flow category
\mathcal{C}(H_{h},J_{h})
using integralization data

We let
\bar{\mathfrak{A}}
be an integralization datum for
\mathcal{C}(H_{h},J_{h})
; we will keep the notation as in Definition
86
. We set
\underline{\mathfrak{A}}=\mathfrak{A}\cup\{A^{1}_{c},A^{2}_{c}\}
. As before we have
\underline{\mathfrak{A}}(x)=\{A\in\underline{\mathfrak{A}}:A<\mathcal{A}(x)\}
, where
\mathcal{A}
is the augmented action underlying
\bar{\mathfrak{A}}
. The integral action for
\mathcal{C}(H_{h},J_{h})
is

\bar{A}(x)=\#\underline{\mathfrak{A}}(x)-1

as usual; the definition of the integral action is the same as before but with the new notation.

Parameter spaces and perturbation data for continuation homotopy.

Let us assume that
x\in Fix(H_{1})
and
y\in Fix(H_{4})
.
We now define analogs of the spaces
\overline{Conf}^{c}_{\mathfrak{A}(x,y)}
defined previously which are appropriate to this new setting. Let
(\overline{Conf}_{\mathfrak{A}(x,y)})^{\mathbb{R}^{2}}
be the fiber product of
(\overline{Conf}_{\mathfrak{A}(x,y)})^{\mathbb{R}}
over
(\overline{Conf}_{\mathfrak{A}(x,y)})
with itself. Similarly, to before, we
we define a open subset
\overline{Conf}^{o}_{\underline{\mathfrak{A}}(x,y)}\subset(\overline{Conf}_{\mathfrak{A}(x,y)})^{\mathbb{R}^{2}}
, and then an alternative compactification
\overline{Conf}^{c}_{\underline{\mathfrak{A}}(x,y)}
of this set. The set
(\overline{Conf}_{\underline{\mathfrak{A}}(x,y)})^{\mathbb{R}^{2}}
is an element
a
of
(\overline{Conf}_{\mathfrak{A}(x,y)})
together with two additional marked
s
coordinates, which we will call
s_{A_{c}^{1}}
and
s_{A_{c}^{2}}
, on the universal curve over
a
. The subset
\overline{Conf}^{o}_{\underline{\mathfrak{A}}(x,y)}
consists of those elements such that
(a,s_{A^{1}_{c}},s_{A^{2}_{c}})
satisfy the following properties:

(A)

The element
a
lies in a stratum where there is a component of the universal curve containing marked points labeled by actions from both
\mathfrak{A}_{1}
and from
\mathfrak{A}_{2}\cup\mathfrak{A}_{3}
(this component must be unique if it exists)and
s_{A^{1}_{c}}
is on that component;

(B)

Similarly there is a (unique) component of the universal curve containing marked points from
\mathfrak{A}_{2}\cup\mathfrak{A}_{3}
and from
\mathfrak{A}_{4}
, and
s_{A^{2}_{c}}
also is on that component;

(C)

Finally, we require that
s_{A^{1}_{c}}
lies to the left of
s_{A^{2}_{c}}
.

We now define a stratification of
\overline{Conf}^{o}_{\underline{\mathfrak{A}}(x,y)}
making it into a
\langle\underline{\mathfrak{A}}(x,y)^{\prime}\rangle
-manifold. Recall as before that we identify
\underline{\mathfrak{A}}^{\prime}(x,y)
with the regions in between the elements of
\underline{\mathfrak{A}}(x,y)
; given an element
\tilde{a}\in\overline{Conf}^{o}_{\underline{\mathfrak{A}}(x,y)}
we put it in the stratum
S(\tilde{a})=\underline{\mathfrak{A}}^{\prime}(x,y)\setminus S^{c}(\tilde{a})
, where
S^{c}(\tilde{a})
contains the elements corresponding to the regions where there are break-points in between marked coordinates labeled by elements of
\mathfrak{A}(x,y)
. (See Figure
9
.)

We now modify the space
\overline{Conf}^{o}_{\underline{\mathfrak{A}}(x,y)}
by “doubling” it in a way analogous to the fashion that
\overline{Conf}_{2,h}
is produced from
\overline{Conf}_{2}
. Namely, by forgetting all the marked points except for the markings
s_{A_{c}^{1}}
and
s_{A_{c}^{2}}
and collapsing all the remaining unsable components, we get a map

\pi_{h}:\overline{Conf}^{o}_{\underline{\mathfrak{A}}(x,y)}\to\overline{Conf}_{2}.

As before we will define two halves

\overline{Conf}^{h,0}_{\underline{\mathfrak{A}}(x,y)}=\pi_{h}^{-1}(\tilde{\tau}^{-1}([0,1/2]))

\overline{Conf}^{h,1}_{\underline{\mathfrak{A}}(x,y)}=\pi_{h}^{-1}(\tilde{\tau}^{-1}([0,1/2])),

\overline{Conf}^{h,\partial}_{\underline{\mathfrak{A}}(x,y)}=\pi_{h}^{-1}(\tilde{\tau}^{-1}(1/2))

and define, as a topological
\langle k\rangle
-manifold,

\overline{Conf}^{h}_{\underline{\mathfrak{A}}(x,y)}=\overline{Conf}^{h,0}_{\underline{\mathfrak{A}}(x,y)}\cup_{\overline{Conf}^{h,\partial}_{\underline{\mathfrak{A}}(x,y)}}\overline{Conf}^{h,1}_{\underline{\mathfrak{A}}(x,y)}

We note now that
\overline{Conf}^{h,\partial}_{\underline{\mathfrak{A}}(x,y)}
is in fact a codimension
1

\langle k\rangle
-submanifold, i.e. there are local charts to
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{[k]\setminus S}
covering an open neighborhood of
\overline{Conf}^{h,\partial}_{\underline{\mathfrak{A}}(x,y)}
such that the latter takes the form

\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{[k]\setminus S}_{+}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{[k]\setminus S}_{+}.

In order to put a smooth structure on
\overline{Conf}^{h}_{\underline{\mathfrak{A}}(x,y)}
we must choose collar neighborhoods

\phi_{i}:(1/2-\epsilon,1/2]\times\overline{Conf}^{h,i}_{\underline{\mathfrak{A}}(x,y)},\;i=0,1,

(117)

sending
\{1/2\}\times\overline{Conf}^{h,\partial}_{\underline{\mathfrak{A}}(x,y)}
to
\overline{Conf}^{h,\partial}_{\underline{\mathfrak{A}}(x,y)}
. We will choose
\phi_{1}=\phi_{2}
such that
\pi_{1}\phi_{i}^{-1}=\tilde{\tau}\pi_{h}
on the image of
\phi_{i}
, where
\pi_{1}
is projection to the first factor. To define
\phi_{i}
, we simply think of a point
a
in
\overline{Conf}^{h,\partial}_{\underline{\mathfrak{A}}(x,y)},
as taking the curve
\pi_{h}(a)\in(\overline{Conf}_{2}^{Z})_{1/2}
, which has two marked
s
-coordinates, which we identify with
s_{A_{1}}
and
s_{A_{2}}
, and adding on some additional marked coordinates; fixing the locations of these new marked coordinates and moving the originally chosen marked coordinates
s_{A_{c}^{1}}
,
s_{A_{c}}^{2}
coordinates apart defines
\phi_{i}
. The two collars
\phi_{i}
glue together to a smooth map

\phi:(1/2-\epsilon,1/2+\epsilon)\times\overline{Conf}^{h,\partial}_{\underline{\mathfrak{A}}(x,y)}\to\overline{Conf}^{h}_{\underline{\mathfrak{A}}(x,y)}

(118)

which is a diffeomorphism onto its image by definition.

Now, for
x>y

\mathcal{C}(H_{t},J_{t})
with
x\in Fix(H_{1})
and
y\in Fix(H_{4})
, we define

{\overline{M}}(x,y)=\overline{Conf}^{h}_{\underline{\mathfrak{A}}(x,y)}.

Otherwise we define the spaces
{\overline{M}}(x,y)
as before. We define the universal curves over over
\overline{M}(x,y)
to be the pullback of the corresponding universal curves from the first factor. One has maps
\iota_{xzy}
covered by maps
\iota^{\prime}_{xzy}
identifying universal curves as in (
82
), with the clarification that if
x\in Fix(H_{1})
,
z\in Fix(H_{2})
and
y\in Fix(H_{4})
then one includes
{\overline{M}}(x,z)\times{\overline{M}}(z,y)
into
{\overline{M}}(x,y)
via the inclusion into
\overline{Conf}^{h,0}(x,y)
; and otherwise if
x\in Fix(H_{1})
,
z\in Fix(H_{3})
and
y\in Fix(H_{4})
one includes
{\overline{M}}(x,z)\times{\overline{M}}(z,y)
into
{\overline{M}}(x,y)
via
\overline{Conf}^{h,1}(x,y)
. Systems of perturbation data and their compatibility are defined as earlier but with the new notation.

We have maps
\pi_{h}:{\overline{M}}(x,y)^{Z}\to\overline{Conf}_{2,h}^{\bar{\mathbb{R}}}
which come from forgetting all the markings except for
s_{A_{c}^{1}}
and
s_{A_{c}^{2}}
, collapsing the components that are now unstable to points, and subsequently quotienting by the
S^{1}
action. Given a point on
{\overline{M}}(x,y)^{Z}
we can write
\tilde{s}
for its image under
\pi_{h}
.

We define

T(x_{-},x_{+})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{c|r}(u,a,v):v\in V^{1}(x_{-},x_{+}),&\partial_{s}u(s,t)+J(\tilde{s})_{t}\partial_{t}u(s,t)-\nabla H^{h}(\tilde{s},u(s,t))=\lambda_{V}(((s,t),a),v),\\
a\in{\overline{M}}(x,y),u:{\overline{M}}(x,y)^{Z}_{a}\to M&\lim_{s\to-\infty}u_{1}(s,\cdot)=x_{-},\lim_{s\to\infty}u_{r}(s,\cdot)=x_{+}\\
u=u_{1}\cup\ldots\cup u_{r}&\lim_{s\to+\infty}u_{j}(s,\cdot)=\lim_{s\to-\infty}u_{j+1}(s,\cdot),j=1,\ldots,r-1\end{array}\right\}.

(119)

We define the thickenings, obstruction bundles, obstruction sections, as in the section on the continuation maps but now with the new notation.

Properties.

The proofs of the next two propositions are essentially identical to the proofs of the corresponding propositions in Section
7.8
, and are thus omitted.

Proposition 22
.

Suppose that we have compatible regular systems of perturbation data
\mathfrak{P}_{i}
over
\mathcal{C}(H_{i},J_{i})
, and compatible regular systems of perturbation data
\mathfrak{P}_{ab}
over
\mathcal{C}(H_{ab},J_{ab})
for
(a,b)\in\{(1,2),(2,4),(1,3),(3,4)\}
such that the restriction of
\mathfrak{P}_{ab}
to
\mathcal{C}(H_{a},J_{a})
is a stabilization of
\mathfrak{P}_{a}
by an
E^{s}
-parameterization equipped with trivial perturbation data, and similarly for its restriction to
\mathcal{C}(H_{b},J_{b})
; and such that there is an integralization datum
\mathfrak{A}
for
\mathcal{C}(H_{h},J_{h})
with underlying integralization data for
\mathcal{C}(H_{ab},J_{ab})
agreeing with those underlying
\mathfrak{P}_{ab}
. Then there exists a regular perturbation datum
\mathfrak{P}_{h}
with underlying integralization datum
\mathfrak{A}_{h}
such that its restriction to
\mathcal{C}(H_{ab},J_{ab})
is a stabilization of
\mathfrak{P}_{ab}
by an
E^{s}
-parameterization equipped with trivial perturbation data.
\blacksquare

Proposition 23
.

The category
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H_{h},J_{h})
defined using
\mathfrak{P}
produced by Proposition
22
, is topologically smooth.

Moreover, suppose we have
E^{s}
-parameterizations
\mathcal{E}_{a}
over
\mathcal{C}(H_{a},J_{a})
and
\mathcal{E}_{ab}
over
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H_{ab},J_{ab})
, such that
(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H_{ab},J_{ab})_{\mathcal{E}_{ab}})|_{\mathcal{C}(H_{a},J_{a}}
is a semi-free stabilization (by some
E
-parameterization) of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H_{a},J_{a})_{\mathcal{E}_{a}}
(and similarly with
(H_{a},J_{a},\mathcal{E}_{a})
replaced with
(H_{b},J_{b},\mathcal{E}_{b})
); and moreover such that there are smooth structures on
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H_{ab},J_{ab})_{\mathcal{E}_{ab}}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H_{a},J_{a})_{\mathcal{E}_{a}}
with the previously mentioned isomorphisms being smooth. Then there is an
E^{s}
-parameterization
\mathcal{E}
such that
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H_{h},J_{h})_{\mathcal{E}}
has a smooth structure, and its restriction to
\mathcal{C}(H_{ab},J_{ab})
is a stabilization by some
E^{s}
-parameterization of
(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H_{ab},J_{ab})_{\mathcal{E}_{ab}})
with the corresponding smooth structure.

Similarly, using arguments which can be copied verbatim from the previous sections, we can choose perturbation data in a manner that is compatible with the
C_{k}
-symmetries on continuation homotopy data.

Proposition 24
.

The analog of Proposition
21
and Lemma
36
hold with
(H_{s},J_{s})
replaced with
(H_{h},J_{h})
, with the condition involving Lemma
33
replaced with the corresponding condition involving Lemma
38
.
\blacksquare

8
Index theory

In order to produce the framings of flow categories defined using Floer theory that are needed for the constructions of Section
4
, and to verify that the same method can be used to produce the
equivariant framings
of Section
4.7
, we will make extensive use of family index theory. As such, in this section, we will review the aspects of equivariant family index theory that we will need to subsequently produce framings in Section
8.6
.

8.1
Review of
K
theory

There is an extraordinary cohomology theory called
KO
theory which defines a contravariant functor

X\mapsto KO^{*}(X)

from the homotopy category
hTop
to the category of graded algebras. On spaces
X
with the homotopy type of a finite
CW
complex, the degree-zero part of this functor has an elementary description in terms of
virtual vector bundles
. Namely, let
X
be a paracompact Hausdorff space, and let
Vect(X)
denote the set of isomorphism classes of finite-dimensional real vector bundles over
X
. Then, if
X
has the homotopy type of a finite
CW
complex, we have

KO^{0}(X)=\{(V_{1},V_{2})\in Vect(X)\times Vect(X)\}/((V_{1},V_{2})\sim(V_{1}\oplus W,V_{2}\oplus W)\text{ for }W\in Vect(X))

(120)

where we take the quotient by the minimal equivalence relation generated by the relation above. One writes the element of
KO^{0}(X)
represented by
(V_{1},V_{2})
as
[V_{1}]-[V_{2}]
.

Recall now the
Atiyah-Janich theorem
:

Theorem 9

(Atiyah
[
18
]
, Jänich (thesis))
.

Let
X
be a finite
CW
complex. Then

KO^{0}(X)=[X,\Fred\mathcal{H}]

(121)

where on the right,
\Fred\mathcal{H}
is the space of Fredholm operators acting on a separable Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, equipped with the operator norm topology.

The ideas behind the proof of this result are used repeatedly when studying moduli spaces arising in Floer homology. As such, we will recall the method of proof. We first recall the fundamental theorem of Kupier:

Theorem 10

(Kupier
[
63
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{R}}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}}
be real and complex infinite-dimensional separable Hilbert spaces, respectively. Let
GL(\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{R}})
and
GL(\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}})
be the spaces of real- and complex-linear bounded automorphisms with bounded inverses, respectively. Then, writing

O(\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{R}})\subset GL(\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{R}}),U(\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}})\subset GL(\mathcal{H}_{\mathbb{C}})

(122)

for inclusions of real and complex-linear isometries, we have that every space in (
122
) above is contractible.

Proof sketch of Thm
9
, see
[
18
]
.

We write
\underline{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
for the trivial
\mathbb{R}^{n}
bundle over
X
. Similarly, write
\underline{\mathcal{H}}
for the trivial Hilbert bundle
\mathcal{H}\times X
over
X
.

Every virtual vector bundle
[V]-[W]
is equivalent to virtual vector bundle
[V^{\prime}]-[\underline{\mathbb{R}^{n}}]
for some
n
. Embed
V^{\prime}
into the Hilbert bundle
\mathcal{H}\times X
as a subbundle; using Kupier’s theorem, one can then produce a bundle isomorphism

\bar{\mathcal{F}}:\underline{\mathcal{H}}/V\to\underline{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}},

where
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{H}
is a complement of a fixed embedding
W\subset\mathcal{H}
. One defines the family of Fredholm operators associated to
[V]-[W]
to be

\mathcal{F}(x)=\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}/V_{x}\xrightarrow{\bar{\mathcal{F}}}\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{H}.

One then shows that the homotopy class of this family is independent of the choices made via Kupier’s theorem, and that this class is independent of the representative
(V,W)
of the original class via an explicit homotopy of Fredholm opreators which adds an
x
-independent trivial vector space to the kernel and the cokernel of each operator
\mathcal{F}_{x}
.

Let
\mathcal{F}:X\to Fred\mathcal{H}
be a map, and write
\mathcal{F}_{x}:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}
for the operator
\mathcal{F}(x)
.
Then, for every
x\in X
, there exists a finite dimensional subspace
W_{x}\subset\mathcal{H}
such that
W_{x}
is transverse to
Im(\mathcal{F}_{x})
, i.e. that
W_{x}+Im(\mathcal{F}_{x})=\mathcal{H}
. But then
W_{x}
is also transverse to
Im(\mathcal{F}_{y})
for all
y
in some open neighborhood
U_{x}
of
x
. Since
X
is compact, there exists a finite collection of points
x_{1},\ldots,x_{r}\in X
such that
W:=W_{x_{1}}+\ldots+W_{x_{r}}
is transverse to
Im(\mathcal{F}_{y})
for all
y\in X
. As such, there is a continuous family of Fredholm operators

\mathcal{F}^{W}:X\to Hom(\mathcal{H}\oplus W,\mathcal{H})

\mathcal{F}^{W}_{x}(v,w)=\mathcal{F}_{x}(v)+w

which are all surjective. Therefore, the dimensions of
\ker\mathcal{F}^{W}_{x}
are independent of
X
and are given by the index
Ind\mathcal{F}^{W}_{x}
for any
x
. As such, there is a vector bundle
\ker\mathcal{F}^{W}
over
X
with the fiber
\ker\mathcal{F}^{W}_{x}
given by the kernel of the corresponding operator. We set the element of
KO^{0}(X)
associated to
\mathcal{F}
to be
[\ker\mathcal{F}^{W}_{x}]-[\underline{W}]
.

Given any two choices
W_{1},W_{2}
as above with
W_{1}\subset W_{2}
, one sees that

\ker\mathcal{F}^{W_{2}}=\ker\mathcal{F}^{W_{1}}\oplus\underline{W_{2}/W_{1}}.

As such, the assignment above gives a well defined element of
KO^{0}(X)
for any continuous map
\mathcal{F}
. To show that this assignment does not change upon a homotopy of maps
\mathcal{F}^{t}
, one uses the same argument to build a vector bundle
\ker\mathcal{F}^{W^{\prime},t}
over
X\times[0,1]
for some sufficiently large finite-dimensional
W^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{H}
associated to such a homotopy of maps, and then uses the fact that this implies that
\ker\mathcal{F}^{W^{\prime},t}|_{X\times\{0\}}\simeq\ker\mathcal{F}^{W^{\prime},t}|_{X\times 1}
.
∎

There is another space,
\mathbb{Z}\times BO
, such that for spaces with the homotopy type of a finite
CW
complex we have

KO^{0}(X)\simeq[X,\mathbb{Z}\times BO].

(123)

We briefly review the construction of this space in Appendix
11.1
. More generally, one defines
KO^{0}(X)=[X,\mathbb{Z}\times BO]
for
any space
with the homotopy type of a CW complex; this set turns out
not
to agree with the set of virtual vector bundles on
X
for infinite CW complexes
X

[
14
]
. Nonetheless, Theorem
9
still establishes a homotopy equivalence
\mathbb{Z}\times BO\simeq\Fred\mathcal{H}
intertwining the isomorphisms (
121
) and (
123
), as we show in Lemma
63
.

8.2
Index theory for paths of self-adjoint operators

The Atiyah-Janich theorem is only the first of a richer series of idenfications which identify classifying spaces of the cohomology theory
KO^{*}
, which were computed by Bott
[
25
]
, with spaces of operators on
\mathcal{H}
. In particular, we have

Theorem 11

(Atiyah-Singer
[
19
]
)
.

For
X
compact Hausdorff, we have

KO^{1}(X)=[X,U/O]=[X,\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{H})]

where

U/O=\colim_{n}U(n)/O(n)

(124)

and
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{H})
is the connected component of the space
\mathcal{A}^{all}(\mathcal{H})
of real bounded self-adjoint operators on a separable Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
containing operators with infinitely-many positive and infinitely-many negative eigenvalues. The other two components are
\mathcal{A}^{+}(\mathcal{H})
and
\mathcal{A}^{-}(\mathcal{H})
, consisting of operators with finitely-many negative and finitely-many positive eigenvalues, respectively; each of
\mathcal{A}^{\pm}(\mathcal{H})
is contractible.

As such, since
\Omega U/O\simeq\mathbb{Z}\times BO
by Bott periodicity, one expects that there is a natural map

\Omega\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{H})\to\Fred\mathcal{H}.

(125)

Such a map indeed exists, and figures heavily in Floer-theoretic constructions. It is helpful to recall there the following theorems:

Theorem 12

(Corollary of
[
79
]
)
.

Any Banach manifold has the homotopy type of a CW complex.

In fact, an equivariant version of the above is known:

Theorem 13
.

Any smooth
G
-Banach manifold has the homotopy type of a CW complex.

Proof.

This follows from
[
16
]
,

[
15
]
, and
[
17
]
.
∎

The following is standard:

Theorem 14
.

The spaces
Fred(\mathcal{H}),\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{H})
are smooth Banach manifolds for a real or complex Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. If
\mathcal{H}
is a
G
-representation for a compact Lie group
G
, then all of these spaces are smooth
G
-Banach manifolds.

Proof.

The space of
B(\mathcal{H})
of bounded operators on
H
is a Banach space under the operator norm;
Fred(\mathcal{H})
is an open subset of the latter
[
68
]
. and
\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{H})
is manifestly a Banach submanifold of
Fred(\mathcal{H})
. The smoothness of the
G
-action in the operator norm topology is obvious.
∎

Fix two operators
A_{\pm}\in\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{H})
, and constants
C,D>0
. Let the model for
\Omega\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{H})
be

\Omega_{A_{-},A_{+}}\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{H})=\{A_{t}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{H}):|A_{t}-A_{\pm}|\leq Ce^{\mp Dt}\text{ as }t\to\pm\infty,A_{t}\text{ is smooth }.\}.

(126)

There are natural separable Hilbert spaces

\mathcal{H}^{\prime}=L^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}_{s},\mathcal{H}),\;\mathcal{H}^{\prime\prime}=L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{s},\mathcal{H})

(127)

of
\mathcal{H}
-valued functions on
\mathcal{R}
. There is a natural continuous map

\begin{gathered}\Omega_{A_{-},A_{+}}\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{H})\to Fred(\mathcal{H}^{\prime},\mathcal{H}^{\prime\prime})\\
A_{t}\mapsto(\partial_{s}+A_{s})\in Fred(L^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}_{s},\mathcal{H}),L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{s},\mathcal{H})).\end{gathered}

(128)

The operators in the image of this map are Fredholm by Robbin-Salamon
[
86
]
(see also
[
84
]
).

Remark 57
.

Unfortunately, the author does not know any proof that the above map is a homotopy equivalence, nor that it in some sense agrees with the Bott periodicity map
\Omega U/O\simeq Z\times BO
under some naturally defined equivalences

\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{H})\simeq U/O,\Fred\mathcal{H}\simeq Fred(\mathcal{H^{\prime}},\mathcal{H}^{\prime\prime})\simeq\mathbb{Z}\times BO

associated to Theorems
9
and
11
. First of all
\mathcal{H^{\prime}}\neq\mathcal{H}
, so the operators
A_{s}
are not
bounded
operators, and
[
19
]
does not actually apply; secondly, even if we have
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}=\mathcal{H}
, the comparison with the Bott periodicity map proven in
[
19
]
is somewhat different. It seems to the author that in the literature (e.g.
[
27
]
) some comparison of the above kind is implicitly used.

Below, while we will not provide a comparison with a more classically defined Bott-periodicity map, we will explain how to extend
[
19
]
to the Floer theoretic setting (where the operators are unbounded) and subsequently how the map (
128
) can be used to produce compatible (and
equivariant
!) framings of Floer theoretic moduli spaces.

8.3
Equivariant Operators and Classifying Spaces

Now let
\mathcal{H}
be a separable Hilbert space equipped with an orthogonal
G
-action. Suppose that
\mathcal{H}
is the
completion of a complete
G
-universe
: it contains an infinite number of pairwise orthogonal copies of the regular
G
-representation whose sum is dense in
\mathcal{H}
.

Using
\mathcal{H}
, we can perform a number of constructions analogous to those of Section
8
. Namely, there is a functor
KO^{0}_{G}
on
hG-Top
which sends spaces
X
with the homotopy type of a finite
G
-CW complex to the set of isomorphism classes of
virtual
G
-vector bundles on
X
, which are defined like in (
120
) with
Vect
replaces with
Vect_{G}
, the set of isomorphism classes of finite-dimensional
G
-equivariant vector bundles. Similarly, the space of Fredholm operators
\Fred\mathcal{H}
is now a
G
-space, with the
G
-action given by
gA=gAg^{-1}
. The
G
-fixed points of
\Fred H
are then the
G
-equivariant Fredholm operators. By replacing the definition of
W
with
W:=Span(G(W_{x_{1}}\oplus\ldots\oplus W_{x_{r}}))
in the proof of Theorem
9
, one gets a functorial isomorphism

[X,\Fred\mathcal{H}]_{G}\to KO_{G}^{0}(X)

on all finite
G
-CW complexes.
Similarly, there are classifying spaces
BO\in hG-Top
for
KO_{G}^{0}
(see Appendix
11.1
).

8.4
Polarization classes

As we have seen earlier, the Hessians of the symplectic action functional formally define a family of formally-self-adjoint operators (
71
) parameterized by points on
LM
, which are unbounded operators on the Hilbert spaces

L^{2}(x^{*}TM)=(\mathcal{T}^{0,2}LM)_{x}

(129)

which are densely defined on the subspaces

W^{1,2}(x^{*}TM)=(\mathcal{T}^{1,2}LM)_{x}\subset(\mathcal{T}^{0,2}LM)_{x}.

(130)

These spaces piece together into Hilbert bundles
\mathcal{T}^{L^{2}}LM
over
LM
, or at least over the homotopy equivalent subspace
L^{1,2}M\subset LM
of
W^{1,2}
loops in
M
, and the operators give a continuous bundle map

Hess(\mathcal{A}_{H}):\mathcal{T}^{1,2}LM\to\mathcal{T}^{0,2}LM.

(131)

This is closely tied to the discussion of Section
8
, where we recalled that the classifying space for
KO^{1}
is the nontrivial component of the space of bounded self-adjoint operators on a separable Hilbert space.

By Kupier’s theorem on the contractibility of the space of invertible bounded linear endomorphisms
GL(\mathcal{H})
of a separable Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, it is tempting to produce a class in
KO^{1}(LM)
from the family of Hessians (
131
) by trivializing the bundle
\mathcal{T}^{0,2}LM
, and applying
11
. However, this cannot be done directly as
LM
is noncompact and the operators (
71
) are unbounded on
\mathcal{T}^{0,2}LM
, the idea is essentially correct, and can be carried forward with a few minor technical modifications.

The first challenge is the non-compactness of
LM
; this, however, can be fixed using Palais’ theorem, as described in Appendix
11.1
.

The second challenge is to write (
71
) as a family of bounded operators. First, write

\mathcal{H}=L^{2}(S^{1},\mathbb{C}^{n}),\mathcal{H}_{1}=W^{1,2}(S^{1},\mathbb{C}^{n}).

(132)

Then the inclusion

\iota:\mathcal{H}_{1}\subset\mathcal{H}

(133)

is a bounded map of Hilbert spaces, and in fact a compact operator. There is also a bounded operator without kernel

i\partial_{t}+1:\mathcal{H}_{1}\to\mathcal{H}

(134)

As such,
(i\partial_{t}+1)
has an adjoint operator
(i\partial_{t}+1)^{T}
, and the composition
\mathcal{K}=\iota(i\partial_{t}+1)
acts via

\mathcal{K}:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{K}(e_{j}e^{2\pi ikt})=\frac{-2\pi k+1}{1+(2\pi k)^{2}}e_{j}e^{2\pi ikt}

(135)

where
e_{j}
is the
j
-th unit coordinate vector of
\mathbb{C}^{n}
, and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. The operator
\mathcal{K}
is a compact self-adjoint operator without kernel, has image equal to
\mathcal{H}_{1}
, and has
mild spectrum
in the sense of
[
62
, Definition 33.1.1]
. The closure of its positive and negative eigenvectors in
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{H}_{1}
will be denoted by
\mathcal{H}^{\pm}
and
\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\pm}
, respectively.

There are spaces of operators

\begin{gathered}B_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H}_{1})=\{A\in B(\mathcal{H}):A(\mathcal{H}_{1})\subset\mathcal{H}_{1},A^{*}(\mathcal{H}_{1})\subset\mathcal{H}_{1}\},\|A\|_{B(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H}_{1})}=\max(\|A\|_{\mathcal{H}},\|A\|_{\mathcal{H}_{1}},\|A^{T}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{1}});\\
B_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H}_{1})=\{A\in B_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H}_{1}):A\text{ is }\mathbb{C}\text{-linear}\}\\
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{H};\mathcal{H}_{1})=\{O\in B_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H}_{1}):O^{T}O=1\}\\
\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H};\mathcal{H}_{1})=\{U\in B_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H}_{1}):U^{*}U=1\}\end{gathered}

(136)

with the latter two spaces naturally having the strucutre of Banach-Lie Groups (this follows from standard spectral theory of unitary operators
[
68
]
).
There are further spaces of bounded Fredholm operators

\begin{gathered}\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{H};\mathcal{H}_{1})=\{A\in B_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H})|A\text{ Fredholm of index }0,\langle A\psi,\phi\rangle_{\mathbb{C}}=\langle\psi,A\phi\rangle\}\\
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{H};\mathcal{H}_{1})=\{A\in B_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H})|A\text{ Fredholm of index }0,\langle A\psi,\phi\rangle=\langle\psi,A\phi\rangle\}\end{gathered}

(137)

where
B_{\mathbb{C}}
,
B_{\mathbb{R}}
, denote
\mathbb{C}
- and
\mathbb{R}
-linear bounded maps of Banach spaces, respectively. These spaces, which are all Banach manifolds, contain subspaces

\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{C}}_{*}(\mathcal{H};\mathcal{H}_{1})\subset\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{H};\mathcal{H}_{1}),\;\mathcal{S}_{*}(\mathcal{H};\mathcal{H}_{1})\subset\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{H};\mathcal{H}_{1})

(138)

consisting of those operators
A
such that there exists
U\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H};\mathcal{H}_{1})
or
O\in\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{H};\mathcal{H}_{1})
respectively, sending
\mathcal{H}^{\pm}
and
\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\pm}
to the closures of the spans of the non-negative (for
+
) and negative (for
-
) eigenvectors of
A
(which are all guaranteed to lie in
\mathcal{H}_{1}
) in
\mathcal{H}
and in
\mathcal{H}_{1}
, respectively.

For every
x\in LM
, (after possibly restricting the bundle
x^{*}TM
to a homotopy-equivalent subspace
LM^{\prime}\subset LM
consisting of sufficiently regular loops, which we will denote by
LM
for the purposes of the rest of this proof) can be differentiably trivialized as a finite-dimensional Hermitian bundle, and a choice of trivialization defines a trivialization of
(x^{\prime})^{*}TM
for all
x^{\prime}\in LM
near
x
. As such, the bundle
\mathcal{T}^{0,2}LM\to LM
has structure group
U(\mathcal{H})
, and the inclusion

\bar{\iota}:\mathcal{T}^{1,2}LM\to\mathcal{T}^{0,2}LM

(139)

defines a restriction of structure group of
\mathcal{T}^{0,2}LM
from
\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H})
to
\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H};\mathcal{H}_{1})
. The corresponding
\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H};\mathcal{H}_{1})
-principal bundle has associated Hilbert bundles
\mathcal{T}^{0,2}LM
as well as the bundle
\mathcal{T}^{1,2}LM
; the fiberwise-norms induced on these bundles from their identification with associated bundles of this principal bundle are equivalent to their usual fiber-wise inner products induced from
TM
.The operator
Hess(\mathcal{A}_{H})
(
71
) can be thought of as a
C^{1}
section of the associated bundle
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{T}^{0,2}LM,\mathcal{T}^{1,2}LM)
corresponding to the action of
\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H}:\mathcal{H}_{1})
on
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{H};\mathcal{H}_{1})
.

In fact, it is canonically homotopic as
C^{1}
sections to an operator which lies in
\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{C}}_{*}(\mathcal{T}^{0,2}LM,\mathcal{T}^{1,2}LM)\subset\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{T}^{0,2}LM,\mathcal{T}^{1,2}LM)
, the bundle associated to the
\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H}:\mathcal{H}_{1})
-action on
\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{C}}_{*}(\mathcal{H};\mathcal{H}_{1})
. To see this, we first note that there is a canonical homotopy of the operators (
71
) through self-adjoint Fredholm operators to the operator
J_{t}\nabla_{t}
, which in turn under unitary trivializations of
x^{*}TM
become operators of the form
i\partial_{t}+A^{x}(t)
where
A^{x}(t)
is a family of symmetric matrices. Deforming the connection on
M
to a unitary connection induces a further deformation of operators to one such that the matrices
A^{x}(t)
are all Hermitian. As such,the eigenvectors of this operator can be written as
e^{2\pi ikt}v_{j}(t)
for some finite collection of functions
v_{j}
,
j=1,\ldots 2n
, corresponding to the eigenvectors of the operator
u_{x}
associated to the value at
t=1
of the solution to the equation
i\partial_{t}U(t)+A^{x}(t)U(t)=0
with
U(0)=0
for
U\in U(N)
(see
[
62
, Proposition 33.2.2]
). In particular, we see that these operators all lie in
\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{C}}_{*}(\mathcal{H}_{1};\mathcal{H})
, and so
J_{t}\nabla_{t}
defines a section of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathbb{C}}_{*}(\mathcal{T}^{0,2}LM,\mathcal{T}^{1,2}LM)
.

Chapter 33 of
[
62
]
proves:

Proposition 25

(Proposition 33.1.6,
[
62
]
)
.

The group
\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H};\mathcal{H}_{1})
is contractible, and the space
S^{\mathbb{C}}_{*}(\mathcal{H};\mathcal{H}_{1})
admits a map to
U
which is a homotopy equivalence.

Applying this proposition to the operators
J_{t}\nabla_{t}
thus defines an element of
[LX,U]
, which defines an associated element of
[LX,U/O]=KO^{1}(LX)
.

Proposition 26
.

The group
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{H};\mathcal{H}_{1})
is contractible, and the space
S_{*}(\mathcal{H};\mathcal{H}_{1})
admits a map to
U/O
which is a homotopy equivalence.

Proof.

The proof of this proposition follows directly analogously to the proof of Proposition 33.1.6 of
[
62
]
except for one key detail, where one uses the geometry of the Lagrangian Grassmannian. We will start off assuming that
\mathcal{H}
has no complex structure, and complexify it shortly. The contractibility of
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{H};\mathcal{H}_{1})
is proven in the same way as the contractibility of
\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{H};\mathcal{H}_{1})
, via the Eilenberg swindle. Let
\beta:\mathbb{R}\to[-1,1]
be a monotone function with
\beta(\lambda)=1
for
\lambda>\Lambda_{+}
and
\beta(\lambda)=-1
for
\lambda<\Lambda_{-}
. The map
A\mapsto\beta(A)
defines a continuous map from
S(\mathcal{H};\mathcal{H}_{1})
to

S^{f}(\mathcal{H};\mathcal{H}_{1})=\{A\in B(\mathcal{H};\mathcal{H}_{1})\cap S(\mathcal{H};\mathcal{H}_{1})|A^{2}-1\text{ has finite rank},\|A\|=1.\}

Its image is

S^{f}_{*}(\mathcal{H};\mathcal{H}_{1})\subset S^{f}(\mathcal{H};\mathcal{H}_{1}),

the subspace of operators for which there are unitary isomorphisms
\ker(A\mp 1)\to\mathcal{H}^{\pm}
which restrict to bounded isomorphisms
\ker(A\mp 1)\cap\mathcal{H}^{\pm}_{1}\to\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\pm}
. The fibers of this map can be identified with the spaces of pairs of operators
(A^{+},A^{-})
with

A^{\pm}:\ker(A\mp 1)\cap\mathcal{H}^{\pm}_{1}\to\ker(A\mp 1)

a symmetric Fredholm operator of index zero with spectrum bounded from one side. Thus this map is a homotopy equivalence (by arguing analogously to
[
19
, Lemma 3.7]
using an inductive argument and the Dold-Thom quasifibration recognition lemma as in
[
62
]
).

Let

U^{f}(\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathbb{C};\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathbb{C})\subset U(\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathbb{C};\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathbb{C})

be the set of elements of such that
u-1
is of finite rank.
Let

U^{f}_{2}(\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathbb{C};\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathbb{C})\subset U(\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathbb{C};\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathbb{C})

consisting of elements
u
such that
u^{2}-1
is of finite rank. Let

Lag_{f}(\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathbb{C};\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathbb{C})=U^{f}(\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathbb{C};\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathbb{C})/U^{f}(\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathbb{C};\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathbb{C})

(140)

where the quotient is taken by the subgroup of elements which preserve (but might not fix) the subspace

i\mathcal{H}\subset\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathbb{C}.

In other words, (
140
) parameterizes set of images of the Lagrangian subspace
\mathcal{H}\subset\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathbb{C}
under elements of
U^{f}_{2}(\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathbb{C};\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathbb{C})
, topologized with the quotient topology. Elements of
Lag_{f}(\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathbb{C};\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathbb{C}))
are subspaces
\Lambda
of
\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathbb{C}
on which the symplectic form
(v,w)\mapsto Re\langle v,iw\rangle
restricts to zero, and which have the property that
\Lambda\cap i\mathcal{H}
is finite codimension in both
\Lambda
and in
i\mathcal{H}
. The perpendicular to
\Lambda\cap i\mathcal{H}
in
\Lambda
, which we will denote by
(\Lambda\cap i\mathcal{H})^{\perp}
, is a finite dimensional subspace of
\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathbb{C}
on which the symplectic form restricts to zero, and which intersects with
i\mathcal{H}
in
0
. As such, projection to
\mathcal{H}
is full rank, and, writing
\pi((\Lambda\cap i\mathcal{H})^{\perp})
for its image, we have that
(\Lambda\cap i\mathcal{H})^{\perp}
is the image of
\pi((\Lambda\cap i\mathcal{H})^{\perp})
under a unique symmetric unitary automorphism
U
of
\mathbb{C}\pi((\Lambda\cap i\mathcal{H})^{\perp})
with eigenvalues
e^{i\pi/2\lambda_{j}}
satisfying
1>\lambda_{j}>-1
for all
j
.

Remark 58
.

The last claim may not be completely obvious but follows from elementary symplectic linear algebra (the product of symmetric matrices is symmetric, an element of
U(n)
preserving
\mathbb{R}^{n}
lies in
O(n)
, and a Lagrangian subspace that is a graph over
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is the graph of a unique real symmetric matrix). The eigenvalues of
U
are the ‘Lagrangian angles’ of the Lagrangian
(\Lambda\cap i\mathcal{H})^{\perp}\subset\mathbb{C}\pi((\Lambda\cap i\mathcal{H})^{\perp})
.

This implies that the continuous map

\begin{gathered}S^{f}_{*}(\mathcal{H};\mathcal{H}_{1})\to Lag_{f}(\mathcal{H}\otimes\mathbb{C};\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathbb{C})\\
A\mapsto[\exp((i\pi/2)A)]\end{gathered}

(141)

is a surjection.
The fibers of this map correspond to orbits of the decomposition
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}^{+}\oplus\mathcal{H}^{-}
under
O(\mathcal{H};\mathcal{H}_{1})
, which are contractible. Thus, arguing as in
[
19
]
, this map is a homotopy equivalence. (Note that the statement that the has the homotopy type of a CW complex can be proven from Lemma
53
and standard results about spectral theory of symmetric operators by filtering the domain by the rank of the matrix.)

Let
e_{i}
be an orthonormal basis of eigenvectors of
K
, arranged in decreasing order of the absolute value of the eigenvalue. Let
E_{n}
be the span of the first
n
eigenvectors, and let
Lag_{n}\subset\Lambda\cap i\mathcal{H}
be the subset for which
(\Lambda\cap i\mathcal{H})^{\perp}
is contained in
\mathbb{C}E_{n}
. Then
Lag_{n}
is manifestly the Lagrangian Grassmannian of subspaces in
\mathbb{C}E_{n}
, and an approximation argument
[
80
]

shows that
\cup_{n}Lag_{n}
is homotopy equivalent to
\Lambda\cap i\mathcal{H}
. We have thus proven the proposition.
∎

Proposition 27

(Equivariant analog of Proposition
26
)
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{H}_{1}
contains a dense complete
G
-universe. Then the map in
hTop
constructed in the previous proposition admits a canonical lift to
G-Top
which is also a
G
-homotopy-equivalence.

(Sketch.).

The equivariant contractibility of
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H}_{1})
follows from
[
58
]
. All maps defined in the previous section are equivariant so it suffices to show that they are
G
-homotopy equivalences. The arguments of
[
19
]
showing the maps defined above with contractible fibers are weak homotopy equivalences can be adapted to the equivariant case using the equivariant quasifibration results of
[
96
]
.
∎

With this background in mind, we now prove Theorem
4
.

Proof of Theorem
4
.

Note that
S_{*}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H}_{1})
has a canonical base point, namely the operator
A_{0}
which acts by
\pm 1
on
\mathcal{H}^{\pm}
. We will show that the map
LM\to S_{*}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H}_{1})
,
u\mapsto A_{u}
produced by Proposition
26
from the operators
J_{t}\nabla^{\prime}_{t}
(where
\nabla^{\prime}_{t}
is
G
-equivariant Hermitian connection on
M
) can be equivariantly contracted to the base point. If
SpecA_{u}\cap\{0\}=0
for all
u
, the proof would be obvious: over any compact subset of
LM
, a homotopy would be given by
\beta(C(1+t)A_{u})
for some sufficiently large
C
, which would land in the equivariantly contractible fiber of (
141
). Thus
u\mapsto A_{u}
would give the trivial map on equivariant homotopy groups and thus by Theorem
8.2
would be equivariantly homotopic to the constant map. The hypothesis allows us to canonically ‘split’ the zero eigenspaces of
A_{u}
in this homotopy. First, note that the
t
-dependent complex structure
J_{t}
can be deformed to
t
-independent one since
\pi_{1}(BU(n))=0
; second, note that we can deform
\nabla^{\prime}_{t}
equivariantly to a connection
\nabla^{\prime\prime}_{t}
which preserves the splitting
TM=\xi\oplus i\xi
. The matrices
U(t)
arising in the characterization of the spectrum of
J\nabla^{\prime\prime}_{t}
(see reference to
[
62
, Proposition 33.2.2]
above) will then actually be orthogonal matrices, and the spectrum of
J\nabla^{\prime\prime}_{t}
will be symmetric under flipping the sign of the eigenvalue with an even number of eigenvectors in any symmetric interval around zero. Moreover, given a sphere
S^{V}
mapping euivariantly to
LM
, choosing
\nabla^{\prime\prime}_{t}
generically among equivariant connections preserving the splitting above, the same characterization shows that if we plot the spectrum of
J\nabla^{\prime\prime}_{t}
as a subset
\mathcal{S}
of
S^{V}\times\mathbb{R}
it will be a union of local sections of the latter bundle over
S^{V}
. Thus there will be an
L
such that
S^{V}\times((-\infty,-L)\cup(L,\infty))
never intersects any component of
\mathcal{S}
which intersects the zero section. Thus by a suitable deformation
\beta_{t}
one can deform
J\nabla^{\prime\prime}_{t}
over
S^{V}
to a family of operators for which the spectrum is either
1,0
or
-1
, with the multiplicity of the zero eigenspace being even and and constant. By choosing sections of the
1
and
-1
eigenspaces (which are infinite-dimensional bundles over
S^{V}
, and thus have sections!) and deforming those eigenvectors to have eigenvalue zero, one can deform the operator to one for which the bundle of zero eigenspaces is trivial over
S^{V}
. A global orthormal basis of sections
e_{1},\ldots,e_{r},e^{\prime}_{1},e^{\prime}_{r}
of the zero eigenspace and then deforming
e_{1},\ldots,e_{r}
to have positive eigenvalues and
e^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,e^{\prime}_{r}
to have negative eigenvalues then gives a deformation of the family of operators to the basepoint.
∎

Remark 59
.

There is a more direct construction of this class
\rho\in KO^{1}(LM)
which has been claimed
[
27
]
to be isomorphic to the class defined by (
128
) under the isomorphism of Theorem
11
. Part of the purpose of this section is is to explain how to make sense of this claim, given that the Hessians of the Floer action functional are
unbounded
self-adjoint operators, while Theorem
11
is about
bounded
self-adjoint operators. (We do not give a complete argument for the claim in
[
27
]
.) In this paper, we will use Propositions
26
and
27
to define the polarization class, in order to establish the existence of equivariant framings, and in order to keep the discussion as unambiguous as possible. Note that
[
29
]
also gives an argument for the non-equivariant analog of Theorem
4
; an equivariant version of the argument of
[
29
]
was not obvious to the author.

8.5
Floer homology and loop space structures on spaces of operators

We now explain how the maps (
128
) of Section
8.2
arise in the context of Floer homology. Indeed, given a pair
x_{\pm}\in Fix(H)
such that
Hess(\mathcal{A}_{H})_{x_{\pm}}
has no zero eigenvalues, and a smooth path of loops

u:\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}\to M,\lim_{s\to\pm\infty}u(s,t)=x_{\pm}(t)

(142)

(where we require that in a chart near
x_{\pm}
, the above convergence is exponential in the
C^{0}
norm), the linearization of the floer equation
is

\xi\mapsto D_{u}\xi=\nabla_{s}\xi+J_{t}\nabla_{t}\xi+(\nabla_{\xi}J_{t})\partial_{t}u-\nabla_{\xi}\nabla H_{t}.

(143)

Thinking
u(s,t)=x_{s}(t)
as a path in
LM
, we have bundles of Hilbert spaces
x_{s}^{*}\mathcal{T}^{1,2}LM
and
x_{s}^{*}\mathcal{T}^{0,2}LM
over
\mathbb{R}
. Choosing a smooth trivialization
\rho
of the
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H}_{1})
-principal bundle underlying
\mathcal{T}^{0,2}LM
gives trivializations of Banach bundles

\begin{gathered}\rho_{s}:x_{s}^{*}\mathcal{T}^{1,2}LM\to\mathcal{H}_{1}\times\mathbb{R},\\
\rho_{s}:x_{s}^{*}\mathcal{T}^{0,2}LM\to\mathcal{H}\times\mathbb{R},\end{gathered}

(144)

(where the latter is a trivialization of Hilbert bundles)
denoted by the same symbol since the latter is the completion of the former. In this trivialization, the linearized Floer operator becomes

\partial_{s}+\rho_{s}Hess(\mathcal{A}_{H})\rho_{s}^{-1}+h_{s}:L^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}_{s},\mathcal{H})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{s},\mathcal{H}_{1})\to L^{0,2}(\mathbb{R}_{s},\mathcal{H})

(145)

where
h_{s}
is continuous family of bounded operators on
\mathcal{H}_{1}
. Explicitly,
h_{s}
is defined by

\partial_{s}+h_{s}=\rho_{s}\circ\nabla_{s}\circ\rho_{s}^{-1};

(146)

so these operators are bounded and skew-adjoint on
\mathcal{H}
, and since
\partial_{s}u\to 0
exponentially as
s\to\pm\infty
, we have that
\lim_{s\to\pm\infty}h_{s}=0
in operator norm. Thus, in particular the operators

A_{x_{\pm}}:=\lim_{s\to\pm\infty}\rho_{s}Hess(\mathcal{A}_{H})\rho_{s}^{-1}+h_{s}=\lim_{s\to\pm\infty}\rho_{s}Hess(\mathcal{A}_{H})\rho_{s}^{-1}

are
1-ASAFOE
operators in the sense of
[
62
, Section 14.1]
. Write

A_{s}=\rho_{s}Hess(\mathcal{A}_{H})\rho_{s}^{-1}.

(147)

The operator (
145
) Fredholm according to Robbin-Salamon
[
86
]
(see
[
84
]
for a further relaxation of the conditions required for Fredholmness).

Suppose now that the class
[\rho]
defined by Proposition
26
is zero.

Definition 87
.

A trivialization of the polarization map is a homotopy

\kappa(A_{\gamma},t):LM\to S_{*}(\mathcal{H};\mathcal{H}_{1}),t\in[0,1]

(148)

from the family
A_{\gamma}
,
\gamma\in LM
to a constant family. When the original family
A_{\gamma}
is
C_{p^{k}}
-equivariant this homotopy must also be
C_{p^{k}}
-equivariant, and we will refer to
\kappa
then as a
C_{p^{k}}
-equivariant trivializtion of the polarization map (the group may be dropped from the notation when it is implicit).

One can use this homotopy to write a deformation of Fredholm operators from (
145
) to an operator that does not depend on
u
, but only on the endpoints
A_{x_{\pm}}
. Let us explicitly write out such a deformation.
Write
A^{1}=\kappa(A,1)
for the relevant
\gamma
-independent operator. Write
A_{s}=A^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}(s)}
where
s^{\prime}(s)\in(0,1)
is the orientation-preserving diffeomorphism from
\mathbb{R}
to
(0,1)
given by

s^{\prime}(s)=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{\pi}\tan^{-1}(s).

(149)

Find a smooth family of functions with parameter
t

\tilde{r}_{t}:[0,1]\to[0,1]\times[0,1],\tilde{r}_{t}=(\tilde{s}_{t},\tilde{t}_{t})

(150)

such that

•

\tilde{r}_{0}(s^{\prime})=(s^{\prime},0)

•

\tilde{r}_{t}(0)=(0,0),\tilde{r}_{t}(1)=(1,0)

for all

t\in[0,1]
; and

•

the path
\tilde{r}_{1}(s^{\prime})
lies entirely on
\partial([0,1]\times[0,1])\setminus(0,1)\times\{0\}
, and satisfies

•

\tilde{r}_{1}([0,1/5])\subset\{0\}\times[0,1]
,

•

\tilde{r}_{1}^{-1}(0,1)=[1/5,2/5],
,

•

\tilde{r}_{1}^{-1}((0,1)\times\{1\})=(2/5,3/5)
, and

•

\tilde{r}_{1}(s^{\prime})=(1,0)+\tilde{r}_{1}(1-s^{\prime})
for
s^{\prime}\in[3/5,1]
.

Then there is a homotopy of operators

D_{u,t}:L^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}_{s},\mathcal{H})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{s},\mathcal{H}_{1})\to L^{0,2}(\mathbb{R}_{s},\mathcal{H}).

where

D_{u,t}=\partial_{s}+\kappa(A^{\prime}_{\tilde{s}_{t}(s^{\prime})},\tilde{t}_{t}(s^{\prime}))+(1-t)h_{s}.

(151)

The space of choices of functions
\tilde{r}_{t}
satisfying the above conditions is contractible, and the homotopy of families operators
h
is well defined up to contractible choice so the above homotopy
D_{u,t}
is unique up to contractible choice. Moreover, with little bit of effort, one can make the operator
D_{u,1}
be independent of
u
for any family of
u
in a compact CW subcomplex of the space of paths in
LM
from
x_{-}
to
x_{+}
. Indeed, the operator
D_{u,1}
defined above only depends on
A_{x_{\pm}}
, and we will write

D_{A_{x_{-}},A_{x_{+}}}=\partial_{s}+A^{x_{-},x_{+}}_{s}=D_{u}^{1}.

(152)

Note that there is a constant
D
only dependent on
\kappa
, such that
A^{x_{-},x_{+}}_{s}=A_{x_{\pm}}
for
\pm s>D
; and there is a constant
C<D
such that
A^{x_{-},x_{+}}(s)=A_{0}
for
|s|<C
.
Because of this, we will be able to use the homotopies
A^{t}_{x}
to find homotopies of the operators
D_{u,t}
over all
u
arising in a flow category, in a way compatible with gluing, and thus to produce framings of a virtually smooth flow category.

For later reference, we record a compatiblity condition between trivializations of the polarization map:

Definition 88
.

Fix a prime number
p
, and write
\phi
for the map sending a loop to its
p
-fold cover.

Write
\mathcal{H}^{k}
for
\mathcal{H}
thought of a
C_{p^{k}}
-representation, and similarly for
\mathcal{H}_{1}^{k}
. Then there is an isomorphism of
C_{p^{k}}\simeq C_{p^{k+1}}/C_{p}
-representations

\Gamma_{k}:(\mathcal{H}^{k+1})^{C_{p}}\simeq\mathcal{H}^{k}

(153)

induced by the pullback of functions along the
p
-fold cover map
S^{1}\to S^{1}
.
Suppose that we have a
C_{p^{k}}
-equivariant map

A^{k}_{\gamma}:LM\to S_{*}(\mathcal{H}^{k};\mathcal{H}^{k}_{1})

and a similar map
A^{k+1}_{\gamma}
with
k
replaced by
k+1
everywhere.

We say that these are
cyclotomically compatible
if
(A^{k+1}_{\phi(\gamma)})^{C_{p}}=\Gamma_{k}A_{\gamma}\Gamma_{k}^{-1}
. Similarly, given a
C_{p^{k}}
-equivariant trivializations
\rho_{k}
of the
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{H}^{k},\mathcal{H}^{k}_{1})
principal bundle underlying
\mathcal{T}^{0,2}LM
, and similarly
\rho_{k+1}
which is like
\rho_{k}
with
k
replaced by
k+1
everywhere, we say that these are cyclotomically compatible if

(\rho_{k+1})_{\phi(\gamma)}^{C_{k}}=\Gamma_{k}(\rho_{k})_{\gamma}.

When one has cyclotomically compatible trivializations of these principal bundles, the polarization maps associated to Floer data for
(H^{\#p^{k}},J^{\#p^{k}})
and
(H^{\#p^{k+1}},J^{\#p^{k+1}})
will be cyclotomiclly compatible as well.

A pair of
(C_{p^{k}},C_{p^{k+1}})
-equivariant trivializations of polarization maps which are homotopies through cyclotomically compatible pairs of such maps is said to be a cyclotomically compatible pair of trivializations of polarization maps.

The following lemma is straightforward to prove using an inductive argument and the results of the previous sectoin:

Lemma 39
.

Suppose that the polarization class
[\rho]\in KO^{1}_{C_{p^{k+1}}}(LM)
vanishes. Then, given a trivialization of the polarization map for
(H^{\#k},J^{\#k})
, there exists a cyclotomically compatible trivialization of the polarization map for
(H^{\#k},J^{\#k+1}
.

8.6
Compatible Framings

We now explain how to produce a compatible system of framings for the smooth
C_{p^{k}}
-flow categories produced in Section
7
. We first explain how to produce a framing for the virtual smoothing
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H,J)
defined using perturbation data
\mathcal{P}=(\bar{\mathfrak{A}},\mathcal{F}^{1},\{\lambda_{V_{\mathcal{F}^{1}}}(x,y)\})
as in Proposition
16
. These framings will depend on a choice of
C_{p^{k}}
-equivariant trivialization of the polarization map (Definition
87
).

First, choose once and for all a system of trivializations

T\overline{Conf}_{k+1}\simeq\underline{T\mathbb{R}^{k}_{+}}

which satisfy the condition that the restriction of this trivialization to
\overline{Conf}_{k_{1}+1}+\overline{Conf}_{k_{2}+1}
for
k_{1}+1+k_{2}+1=k+1
agrees with the product of the trivializations composed with the inclusion

\underline{T\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}}_{+}}\boxplus\underline{T\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}}_{+}}\simeq\underline{T\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}}_{+}\oplus 0\oplus T\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}}_{+}}\subset\underline{T\mathbb{R}^{k}_{+}}.

This defines a system of trivializations of the parameter spaces

T{\overline{M}}(x,y)\simeq\underline{T\mathbb{R}^{\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}(x,y)_{+}}}.

(154)

Thus there are partial trivializations

TT(x,y)=\underline{T\mathbb{R}^{\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}(x,y)_{+}}}\oplus\ker D_{u}^{V}

(155)

where
\ker D_{u}^{V}
is the bundle of kernels of the operators (
91
) (or the kernels of the corresponding direct sum of operators when the domain of
u
has multiple components).

Now, recall that the obstruction bundles of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H,J)
are framed with the corresponding parameterization given by

\mathcal{F}^{1}_{T}=\mathcal{F}^{1}_{can}\oplus\mathcal{F}^{1}

with
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{can}
the canonical
F^{\prime}
-parameterization
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{can}
corresponding to the
E^{\prime}
-parameterization
\mathcal{E}^{1}_{can}
(
80
).

Step 0: Collars.

Our first step is to choose a
compatible system of collars
on
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H,J)
.

There is a functor
T
from
DerMan^{\prime}_{sm,no-stab}
to
Man^{\prime}
sending a derived
\langle k\rangle
-manifold to its thickening. This is a monoidal functor; given a virtually smooth flow category
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
, write
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{T}
for the corresponding smooth flow category. The collaring operator
Coll
of Definition
79
defines a monoidal functor
Man^{\prime}\to Man^{\prime}
; thus, given a smooth flow category
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{T}
there is an associated smooth flow category
Coll(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{T})
obtained by applying
Coll
to all morphism objects. The latter is
G
-equivariant if the former is.

Definition 89
.

A
compatible system of collars
on a virtually smooth flow category
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is an (equivariant) isomorphism
Coll(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{T})\to\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{T}
such that the maps on morphism objects preserve strata, i.e. the induced map
S_{Coll(\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{T})(x,y)}\to S_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{T}(x,y)}
is the identity for all
x>y
in
\mathcal{C}
.

Lemma 40
.

A virtually smooth flow category
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
admits a compatible system of collars. If
H\subset G
is a normal subgroup then a (
G/H)
-equivariant) compatible system of collars on
(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})^{H}
extends to an (
G
-equivariant) compatible system of collars on
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

One follows an equivariant double induction; for each individual moduli space this is the content of
[
12
, Proposition 4.1]
.
∎

Now, every sequence
x_{0}>x_{1}>\ldots x_{r}
in
\mathcal{C}
has an associated stratum
S_{x_{0}>\ldots>x_{r}}
in
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(x_{0},x_{r})
, and by the assumption that
H
is
C_{k}
-action nondegenerate, the stratum
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{T}(x_{0},x_{r})(S_{x_{0}>\ldots>x_{r}})
consists of perturbed Floer trajectories from
x_{0}
to
x_{r}
which break on a set of Hamiltonian orbits containing a set of the form
\{g_{i}x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{r-1}
for some
g_{i}\in G_{x_{0}x_{r}}
. We will write
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{T}(x_{0},x_{r})[x_{0},\ldots,x_{r}]\subset\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{T}(x_{0},x_{r})(S_{x_{0}>\ldots>x_{r}})
for the corresponding connected component.

We will write
T(x,y)
for the thickening of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(x,y)
, and we will write
T(x,y)^{o}=T(x,y)\setminus\partial T(x,y)
. We will write

T(x,y)^{\square}\text{ for the image of }T(x,y)\text{ under }T(x,y)\subset Coll(T(x,y))\to T(x,y).

Step 1: Deforming operators.

We now build compatible deformations of Fredholm operators over the spaces
T(x,y)^{o}\times[0,1]_{t}
.

Let
\pi:T(x,y)\to{\overline{M}}(x,y)
be the projection; write

T(x,y)^{\mathbb{R}}=\pi^{*}{\overline{M}}(x,y)^{\mathbb{R}}.

There is a map

u_{s}:T(x,y)^{\mathbb{R}}\to LM

which sends
s
-coordinates on the domain of a perturbed fer trajectory to the corresponding loops. There is also a section
A^{u}_{s}
of the bundle
u_{s}^{*}\mathcal{S}_{*}(\mathcal{T}^{0,2}LM,\mathcal{T}^{1,2}LM)
.

We follow the strategy of Section
8.5
.

Let us imagine that the polarization class
\rho\in KO^{1}_{G}(LM)
vanishes for
G=C_{k}
for some
k
or
G=S_{1}
.
We fix, once and for all, a
G
-trivialization of the
\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H}_{1})
-principal bundle associated to
\mathcal{T}^{0,2}LM
, which induces trivializations

\begin{gathered}\rho_{s}:u_{s}^{*}\mathcal{T}^{1,2}LM\to\mathcal{H}_{1}\times T^{C}(x,y),\\
\rho_{s}:u_{s}^{*}\mathcal{T}^{0,2}LM\to\mathcal{H}\times T^{C}(x,y).\end{gathered}

(156)

We choose the
G
-homotopy
\kappa
(
148
) with the condition that that the base point base point
A^{0}\in S_{*}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H}_{1})
has no zero eigenvalues.

We wish to find a free stabilization of the perturbation data
\mathfrak{P}
by trivial perturbation data such that stabilized perturbation data
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}=(\bar{\mathfrak{A}},\mathcal{F}^{\prime},\lambda_{V^{1}(x,y)})
(so
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}=\{V^{1}(x,y)\}
is a free stabilization of
\mathcal{F}
by
\mathfrak{F}_{f}
) define the
\tau=0
case of a
1
-parameter family of
compatible thickening-dependent regular perturbation data

\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}_{\tau}=(\bar{\mathfrak{A}},\mathcal{F}^{\prime},\{\tilde{\lambda}^{\tau}_{V^{1}(x,y)}\})
for
\tau\in[0,1]
. Here, the maps
\tilde{\lambda}^{\tau}_{V^{1}(x,y)}
are differentiable bundle maps from
\underline{V^{1}(x,y)}
to
\mathcal{H}_{1}
over
T(x,y)^{\mathbb{R}}
which are zero near the ends of every fiber of the universal curve over
T(x,y)
, and restrict to one another under the isomorphisms
T(x,y)^{\mathbb{R}}(T(x,y)[x,z,y])\simeq T(x,z)^{\mathbb{R}}\times T(z,y)\cup T(x,z)\times T(z,y)^{\mathbb{R}}
; the continuity condition in
\tau
is that as
\tau
varies these define differentiable bundle maps over
T(x,y)^{\mathbb{R}}\times[0,1]_{\tau}
. The perturbation datum
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
defines the thickening-dependent perturbation datum
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}_{0}
via

\tilde{\lambda}^{0}_{V^{1}(x,y)}(u,a,v;s)=\rho_{s}\circ\lambda_{V^{1}(x,y)}(a,v)|_{u_{s}}.

To specify the conditions that
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}_{\tau}
must satisfy we specify the following operators.
First, recall that for
x,y\in\mathcal{C}
we have the canonical operators
D_{A_{x_{-}},A_{x_{+}}}
defined in (
152
). Now the gluing construction allows us to define, for every
x_{-}=x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{r-1},x_{+}\in\mathcal{C}
, a ‘canonical family of operators’
D_{A_{x_{0}},A_{x_{1}},\ldots,A_{x_{r}}}=:D_{x_{0},\ldots,x_{r}}
over
[0,1]^{r-1}
. We outline this below; first, however we specify a related gluing construction. Define the families of operators
A^{x,b}_{s}
by setting
A^{x,b}_{s}=A^{x,y}_{s}
for
s\leq-C
(where
y
is arbitrary), and
A^{x_{-},b}_{s}=A^{0}
for
s>-C
. Define the operator

D_{x}:L^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}_{s},\mathcal{H})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{s},\mathcal{H}_{1})\to L^{0,2}(\mathbb{R}_{s},\mathcal{H}),D_{x}\xi=\partial_{s}\xi+A^{x,b}_{s}\xi.

(157)

The operator
D_{y}
can be
canonically glued
to the operator
D_{x,y}
in the following sense. Set, for
\tau_{2}>D
,

A^{x_{-},x_{+}}_{s,\tau_{2}}=\begin{cases}A^{x_{-},x_{+}}_{s}&\text{ if }s<\tau_{2}\\
A^{x_{+},b}_{s-(D+\tau_{2})}&\text{ if }s\geq\tau_{2}\end{cases}

(158)

and for
0<\tau_{2}\leq D
, we set

A^{x_{-},x_{+}}_{s,\tau_{2}}=\begin{cases}A^{x_{-},x_{+}}_{s}&\text{ if }s<\tau_{2}\\
A^{x_{+},b}_{s-(2\tau_{2})}&\text{ if }s\geq\tau_{2}.\end{cases}

(159)

Thus for
\tau_{2}>D
,
\tau_{2}-D
measures the distance between the regions where the family of operators is staying at
A_{0}
is occurring; and for
0>\tau_{2}>D
the two homotopies
\kappa(A_{x_{+}},t)
going in opposite directions are cancelling themselves out. We can then set, for
\tau_{2}\in[0,\infty)
,

\begin{gathered}D_{x,y,\tau_{2}}:L^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}_{s},\mathcal{H})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{s},\mathcal{H}_{1})\to L^{0,2}(\mathbb{R}_{s},\mathcal{H}).\\
D^{\mathfrak{P^{\prime}}}_{x}(\xi,v)=\partial_{s}\xi+A^{x_{-},x_{+}}_{s,\tau_{2}}\xi.\end{gathered}

(160)

As
\tau_{2}\to\infty
, this operator becomes the ‘gluing’ of
D_{x,y}
and
D_{y}
.

Choosing once and for all an orientation-reversing diffeomorphism
[0,\infty)_{\tau_{2}}\to(0,1]_{\tau_{2}^{\prime}}
(we choose to work with the map
\tau_{2}=1/\tau^{\prime}_{2}-1
) this defines a family of operators
D_{x,y,\tau_{2}^{\prime}}
over
(0,1]
. We set
D_{x,y,0}=D_{x,y}\oplus D_{y}
.

By performing a similar construction with the
A^{x_{i},x_{i+1}}_{s}
, one defines operators

\begin{gathered}D_{x_{0},\ldots,x_{r};\tau^{\prime}_{2}}:L^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}_{s},\mathcal{H})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{s},\mathcal{H}_{1})\to L^{0,2}(\mathbb{R}_{s},\mathcal{H}),\tau^{\prime}_{2}\in(0,1]^{[r-2]}\\
D^{\mathfrak{P^{\prime}}}_{x}(\xi,v)=\partial_{s}\xi+A^{x_{0},\ldots,x_{r}}_{s;\tau_{2}}.\end{gathered}

(161)

Here
\tau_{2}
, after applying the diffeomorphism
(0,1]^{[r-1]}\to[0,\infty)^{[r-1]}
given by applying the previously chosen diffeomorphism coordinate-wise, measures the sizes of the regions where homotopies from
A_{x_{i}}
to
A^{0}
to
A_{x_{i+1}}
are occurring. Thinking of
[0,1]^{[r-1]}\subset\mathbb{R}^{[r-1]}_{+}
as a stratified spaces, note that

[0,1]^{[r-1]}([r-1]\setminus\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\})=[0,1]^{\{0,\ldots,a_{1}-1\}}\times[0,1]^{\{a_{1}+1,\ldots,a_{2}-1\}}\times\ldots\times[0,1]^{\{a_{\ell-1}+1,\ldots,a_{\ell}\}}.

We set, for
\tau^{\prime}_{2}
in
[0,1]^{[r-1]}([r-1]\setminus\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{\ell}\})
,

D_{x_{0},\ldots,x_{r};\tau^{\prime}_{2}}=D_{x_{0},\ldots,x_{a_{1}}}\oplus D_{x_{a_{1}},\ldots,x_{a_{2}}}\oplus\ldots\oplus D_{x_{a_{\ell}},\ldots,x_{r}}.

We now define operators

D_{\tilde{u},1}:L^{1,2}(T(x,y)^{\mathbb{R}}_{\tilde{u}},\mathfrak{H})\cap L^{2}({\overline{M}}(x,y)^{\mathbb{R}}_{a},\mathfrak{H}_{1})\to L^{0,2}(T(x,y)^{\mathbb{R}}_{\tilde{u}},\mathfrak{H})

where we write
\tilde{u}=(u,a,v)
for an element in
T(x,y)
, as follows.
We recall two facts. First, recall that the fibers of the universal curve
T(x,y)^{\mathbb{R}}_{\tilde{u}}
are disjoint ordered copies of
\mathbb{R}
, with an
s
-coordinate well defined up to translation; if
u
lies in the interior of a stratum corresponding to the set
S_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(x,y)}\setminus S^{\prime}
, then the fiber consists of
S^{\prime}
+1 copies of
\mathbb{R}
. In a moment, we we will choose trivializations

\phi^{\mathbb{R}}_{S}:(T(x,y)(S)^{o})^{\mathbb{R}}\to\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{\sqcup|S_{\mathcal{C}(x,y)}\setminus S|+1}.

Second, recall that the compatible system of collars defines maps

\Gamma:T(x_{0},x_{r})[x_{0},\ldots,x_{r}]\times[0,1]^{[r-1]}\to T(x_{0},x_{r})

for every
x_{0}>x_{1}>\ldots>x_{r}
in
\mathcal{C}
(where we can think of
[0,1]^{[r-1]}
as
[0,1]^{\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{r-1}\}}
).
These maps are diffeomorphisms onto subsets
U(x_{0},\ldots,x_{r})\subset T(x_{0},x_{r})
; giving these the induced stratification, we can write
\partial U(x_{0},\ldots,x_{r})
for the image of
T(x_{0},x_{r})[x_{0},\ldots,x_{r}]\times([0,1]^{[r-1]}\cap\mathbb{R}^{[r-1]}_{+})
the map defined by the collar. We write
\epsilon_{x_{0},\ldots,x_{r}}:U(x_{0},\ldots,x_{r})\to[0,1]^{[r-1]}
for the map given by the composition of
\Gamma^{-1}
with the projection to the second factor.
We choose the trivializations
\phi^{\mathbb{R}}_{S}
such that if we set

A^{\tilde{u}}_{s;1}=A^{x_{0},\ldots,x_{r};\epsilon_{x_{0},\ldots,x_{r}}(\tilde{u})}_{\phi_{S(x_{0},\ldots,x_{r}}(\tilde{u};s)}\text{ for }\tilde{u}\in U(x_{0},\ldots,x_{r})

then there is a continuous extension of
A^{\tilde{u}}:T(x,y)^{\mathbb{R}}\to S_{*}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{H}_{1})
to
T(x,y)^{\bar{\mathbb{R}}}
.
Using these maps, we define
D_{\tilde{u},1}
as

D_{\tilde{u},1}=\partial_{s}+A^{\tilde{u}}_{s;1}\text{ for }u\in T(x,y)^{o},

and more generally, writing

T(x_{0},x_{r})[x_{0},\ldots,x_{r}]^{o}\simeq T(x_{0},x_{1})^{o}\times\ldots\times T(x_{r-1},x_{r})^{o},\tilde{u}\leftrightarrow(\tilde{u_{1}},\ldots,\tilde{u_{r}}),

we have

D_{\tilde{u},1}=D_{\tilde{u}_{1},1}\oplus\ldots\oplus D_{\tilde{u}_{r},1}.

Given
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}_{\tau}
we then introduce

D^{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}_{\tilde{u},1}:(L^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}_{s},\mathcal{H})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{s},\mathcal{H}_{1}))^{\oplus r}\oplus V^{1}(x_{0},x_{r})\to L^{0,2}(\mathbb{R}_{s},\mathcal{H})\text{ for }\tilde{u}\in T(x_{0},x_{r})[x_{0},\ldots,x_{r}]^{o}

D^{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}_{\tilde{u},1}((\xi)_{j=1}^{r},v)=\oplus_{j}D_{\tilde{u}_{j},1}\xi_{j}+\tilde{\lambda}^{0}_{V^{1}(x,y)}(u,a,v;s)

Lemma 41
.

Suppose that the operators
D^{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}_{\tilde{u},1}
are all surjective. Then, topologizing the space of kernels
\ker D^{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}_{\tilde{u},1}=:\ker D^{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}_{\tilde{u};x,y,1}
in the Hausdorff topology on sections of the bundle
\underline{\mathcal{H}\oplus V^{1}(x_{0},x_{r})}
over
T(x,y)^{\mathbb{R}}
we have that the bundle of kernels of
D^{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}_{\tilde{u},1}
forms a topological vector bundle over
T(x_{0},x_{r})
for every
x_{0},x_{r}
.

Proof.

This follows from the compatibility conditions for the
A^{\tilde{u}_{s}}
and standard exponential decay estimates for solutions to operators of the form
D^{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}_{\tilde{u},1}
, which are simply of the form
\partial_{s}+A
at the ends of
\mathbb{R}
.
∎

We wish to interpolate between these bundles and the corresponding bundles associated to stabilizing
\mathfrak{P}
to
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
as perturbation data for
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
. Note that, writing
A^{\tilde{u}}_{s,0}=\rho_{s}A^{\tilde{u}}_{s}\rho_{s}^{-1}
we can identify the latter bundle with the bundle of fibers of the operators

D_{\tilde{u},0}^{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}:(L^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}_{s},\mathcal{H})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{s},\mathcal{H}_{1}))^{\oplus r}\oplus V^{1}(x_{0},x_{r})\to L^{0,2}(\mathbb{R}_{s},\mathcal{H})\text{ for }\tilde{u}\in T(x_{0},x_{r})[x_{0},\ldots,x_{r}]^{o}

D^{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}_{\tilde{u},0}((\xi)_{j=1}^{r},v)=\oplus_{j}(\partial_{s}\xi_{j}+A^{\tilde{u}}_{s;0}\xi_{j}+h^{u}_{s}\xi)+\tilde{\lambda}^{0}_{V^{1}(x,y)}(u,a,v;s).

We will find families of operators
A^{\tilde{u}}_{s,\tau}:T(x,y)^{\bar{\mathbb{R}}}\to S_{*}(\mathcal{H};\mathcal{H}_{1})
which restrict over
T(x,y)[x,z,y]^{\bar{\mathbb{R}}}
continuous in
\tau\in[0,1]
to the corresponding operators

A^{\tilde{u}_{1}}_{s,\tau}\sqcup A^{\tilde{u}_{2}}_{s,\tau}
and are equivariant with respect to the
C_{k}
action, such that, defining

D^{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}_{\tilde{u},\tau}((\xi)_{j=1}^{r},v)=\oplus_{j}(\partial_{s}\xi_{j}+A^{\tilde{u}}_{s;\tau}\xi_{j}+(1-\tau)h^{u}_{s}\xi)+\tilde{\lambda}^{0}_{V^{1}(x,y)}(u,a,v;s).

(where this has the same domain and codomain as
D^{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}_{\tilde{u},1}
) the spaces of kernels of these operators, topologized as in Lemma
41
, define topological vector bundles over
T(x,y)\times[0,1]_{\tau}
.

.

Remark 60
.

Note that we do not require any compatiblity conditions between
\tilde{\lambda}_{xy,\tau_{2}}
and
\tilde{\lambda}_{yz,\tau_{2}}
; this makes these choices straightforward. We essentially only need the
\tilde{\lambda}_{xy,\tau_{2}}
to be able to make sense of the numerical conditions in the lemma above, and to have gluing isomorphisms
\ker D^{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}_{x}\simeq\ker D^{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}_{x,y}\oplus\ker D^{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}_{y}

only well defined in the homotopy category, and satisfying the relevant associativity conditions in the homotopy category
, which standard from linear gluing theory. A crucial point of the entire construction is that the complications arising from the non-associativity of gluing are systematically avoided at every step.

We have established:

Proposition 28
.

Let
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H,J)
be the virtual smoothing constructed as in Section
7
. Then if the
S^{1}
-equivariant polarization class vanishes then a free stabilization of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H,J)
admits an equivariant embedded framing.

Remark 61
.

The reader may note that the whole construction of this paper never asks for any results about linear or nonlinear associativity of gluing. The statement that ‘the obstruction to moduli spaces being framed is controlled by the polarization classes restricted to the the interiors of the moduli spaces of Floer trajectories’ is simply Proposition 12 of
[
27
]
; however, that paper does not actually explain how to perform an inductive sequence of stabilizations of the category in order to frame the relevant flow categories (see diagram (28) therein, where rigorously translating this statement together with the triviality of the polarization class into a statement about the existence of compatible trivializations of tangent bundles involves a careful analysis of the (non)-associativity of linear Fredholm gluing, which is not entirely trivial to perform). The construction above makes this clear, and moreover makes it clear that this procedure can be done equivariantly. Below we will prove construct framings of additional flow categories satisfying various kinds of compatibility conditions using this strategy. In later works
[
65
,
34
]
, the notion of an ‘isomorphism of virtual bundles’ is used in the discussion of framings, but no definition is given and no definition is known to this author that makes the construction of framings completely elementary. Similarly, in this paper, we keep track of the distinction between

F_{V}\Sigma^{V}X\text{ and }X

which are canonically weakly equivalent but in fact denote distinct orthogonal spectra.

8.7
Generalization of framing constructions: continuation maps and cyclotomic compatibility.

Behavior of framings of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H,J)
under stabilization

Lemma 44
.

Suppose that

Recall that we can realize

\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H,J)_{\mathcal{E}^{f}}

V^{i}_{\mathcal{E}}(x,y)=V^{i}(x,y)\oplus V_{\mathcal{F}}(x,y),i=0,1

(175)

V^{i}_{\mathcal{E}}(x)=V^{i}(x)\oplus V_{\mathcal{F}}(x),i=0,1

(176)

for the vector spaces associated to the

, setting

\tilde{\lambda}^{\tau}_{V^{1}_{\mathcal{E}}(x,y)}(\tilde{u})(v,w)=\tilde{\lambda}^{\tau}_{V^{1}(x,y)}(\pi_{xy}(\tilde{u})(v)

\tilde{\lambda}_{V^{1}_{\mathcal{E}}(x)}(v,w)=\lambda_{V^{1}(x)}(v),\tilde{\lambda}_{V^{1}_{\mathcal{E}}(x),V^{1}_{\mathcal{E}}(y)}(v,w)=\lambda_{V^{1}(x),V^{1}(y)}(v)
