)).
With this choice the vector spaces defined in (

∎

Behavior of framings of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H,J)
under extension of integralization data

Lemma 45
.

Suppose we are in the situation of Lemma
32
: we have an integralization datum
\bar{\mathfrak{A}}
for

.

Proof.

of

kerD^{V_{\mathfrak{P}}}_{u}\to\ker D^{V_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}_{new}}}_{w(u)}

(177)

covering the identification

(

) of thickenings.

∎

Remark 62
.

Note that we do
not
state in Lemma
45
that the framing on the flow category
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H,J)_{\mathfrak{P}_{new}}
arises from the framing on
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{P}}
by transport of structure from the framing on
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H,J)_{\mathfrak{P},r}
(with the framing on the latter coming from Lemma
20
). Indeed, these framings will not agree; their restrictions to the vertical tangent bundles
\ker D_{u}^{V_{\mathfrak{P}}}
will agree, but the components coming from the framing of the parameter spaces will disagree. However, we will use the embedding coming from Lemma
20
to make sense of the Floer homotopy type of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H,J)_{\mathfrak{P}_{new}}
; since the framings of the obstruction bundles of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H,J)_{\mathfrak{P}_{new}}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H,J)_{\mathfrak{P},r}

do
agree under transport of structure and since the floer homotopy type only depends on the embedding, we will be able to use the conclusion of Proposition
6
in this setting (see e.g. Proposition
35
). Thus, this discrepancy does not affect our ability to prove the invariance statements that we require in the geometric setting of Section
10
. A somewhat tedious geometric argument can be used to show that these two framings are in fact connected by an (essentially canonical) homotopy of framings, but we will not use this in the paper; see also Remark
32
.

Framings for continuation map moduli spaces.

We now explain how to extend the method of the previous section produce framings of the categories
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H_{s},J_{s})
associated to a convex continuation datum. The basic setup is that of Proposition
20
: there exist
E^{s}
-parameterizations
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}
of
\mathcal{C}(H_{\pm},J_{\pm})
such that
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H_{s},J_{s})|_{\mathcal{C}(H_{\pm},J_{\pm})}\simeq\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H_{s},J_{s})_{\mathcal{E_{\pm}}}
as a virtually smooth flow category. Now Proposition
28
, via Lemma
43
, defines
E
-parameterizations
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}^{f}
of
\mathcal{C}(H_{\pm},J_{\pm})
such that
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H_{\pm},J_{\pm})_{\mathcal{E}_{\pm}^{f}}
are given framings.

Lemma 46
.

With notation as in the above paragraph, there is an
E^{s}
-parameterization
\mathcal{E}^{f}
of
\mathcal{C}(H_{s},J_{s})
such that
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H_{s},J_{s})_{\mathcal{E}^{f}}
has a framing, and moreover we can write
\mathcal{E}^{f}=\mathcal{E}^{f}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{E}^{f}_{+}\oplus\mathcal{E}^{f}_{-}
where
\mathcal{E}^{f}_{\pm}
are some
E^{s}
-parameterizations of
\mathcal{C}(H_{s},J_{s})
which agree with the previously mentioned
\mathcal{E}^{f}_{\pm}
when restricted to
\mathcal{C}(H_{\pm},J_{\pm})
, and under this isomorphism we have that the induced framing on
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H_{s},J_{s})_{\mathcal{E}^{f}}|_{\mathcal{C}(H_{\pm},J_{\pm})}
is agrees with the framing induced via stabilization by
\mathcal{E}^{f}_{0}\oplus\mathcal{E}^{f}_{\mp}\oplus\mathcal{E}_{\pm}
of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H_{\pm},J_{\pm})_{\mathcal{E}_{\pm}^{f}}
.

Proof.

We must explain how we are framing
{\overline{M}}(x,y)
in this setting. After making this choice, the rest of the construction
follows the construction of Proposition
28
, using Lemma
8.7
in the process of the double inductions of Lemma
43
and Step 2 of Proposition
28
in the proof of Lemma
43
to define all choices made whenever we are considering a pair
(x,y)
where both
x
and
y
are in
\mathcal{C}(H_{+},J_{+})
or in
\mathcal{C}(H_{-},J_{-})
.

Let
x\in Fix(H_{-})
and
y\in Fix(H_{+})
. There is a submersion

\pi_{f}:{\overline{M}}(x,y)=\overline{Conf}^{o}_{\underline{\mathfrak{A}}(x,y)}\subset\overline{Conf}_{\mathfrak{A}(x,y)}^{\mathbb{R}}\to\overline{Conf}_{\mathfrak{A}(x,y)}

given by composing the inclusion with the map from the universal curve to the parameter space. We have already chosen trivializations of
T\overline{Conf}_{|\mathfrak{A}(x,y)}|
earlier, and the vertical tangent bundle of
\pi_{f}
is canonically trivialized (one can identify each fiber with a copy of
\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{R}\sqcup\mathbb{R}
up to
s
-translation by moving the point on the corresponding component of the universal curve; the vertical tangent bundle is trivialized by saying that the tangent vector corresponding to moving
s_{A_{c}}
to the right with speed
1
is sent to
1\in\mathbb{R}
.). We then get a trivialization

T\overline{Conf}^{o}_{\underline{\mathfrak{A}}(x,y)}=\pi_{f}^{*}T\overline{Conf}_{\mathfrak{A}(x,y)}\oplus T^{v}_{\pi_{f}}\simeq\underline{T\mathbb{R}^{\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}(x,y)}}\oplus\underline{\mathbb{R}}\simeq\underline{T\mathbb{R}^{\underline{\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}}(x,y)}}

where the last isomorphism identifies the
\underline{\mathbb{R}}
factor with the
\underline{T\mathbb{R}^{\{A_{c}\}}_{+}}
factor. This convention makes the framings satisfy the conditions of Definition
23
, which uses the ordering on the set
S_{\bar{A}}
in the compatibility condition (
23
).
∎

Cyclotomic compatibility of framings.

Now, we explain how to extend framings compatibly upon replacing the (potentially
C_{\ell}
-invariant) data
(H_{s},J_{s})
with (

-invariant data)

of a restratification

Lemma 47
.

Suppose that there is an

Framings for homotopies of continuation map moduli spaces.

∎

Figure 10:
Given two framed manifolds with boundary, we take the product of each manifold with
\mathbb{R}
and use the extra
\mathbb{R}
coordinate together with a normal coordinate vector field to insert a rotation in the framing such that we can glue these two manifolds along their common boundary stratum to a new framed manifold. This is the construction used in Lemma
21
.

Lemma 49
.

Suppose we are in the situation of Proposition
24
: we have perturbation data
\mathfrak{P}_{k}
for
\mathcal{C}(H^{\#k}_{h},J^{\#k}_{h})
and perturbation data
\mathfrak{P}
for
\mathcal{C}(H_{h},J_{h})
such there is a
C_{k\ell/C_{k}}\simeq C_{\ell}
-equivariant isomorphism virtually smooth flow categories between a restriction of
\mathcal{C}(H^{\#k}_{h},J^{\#k}_{h})^{C_{k}}
and a restriction of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H_{h},J_{h})_{r,\mathcal{E}_{k}}
where
\mathcal{E}_{k}
is some
E^{s}
-parameterization and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H_{h},J_{h})_{r}
is a restratification of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H_{h},J_{h})
associated to the extension of perturbation data as in Lemma
20
.

Suppose further that
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H_{h},J_{h})_{\mathcal{E}^{f}_{h}}
has been given a framing as in Lemma
48
. Write
\mathcal{E}^{f}_{h,0,ab}
for the direct sum of all the terms in (
178
) not contributing to
\mathfrak{E}^{f}_{ab}
. Then, fixing an extension of
\mathcal{E}^{f}_{h}
to
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H^{\#k}_{h},J^{\#k}_{h})
, there is an
E^{s}
-parameterization
\mathcal{E}^{f}_{h,k,0}
of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H^{\#k},J^{\#k})
such that, writing
\mathcal{E}^{f}_{h,k}=\mathcal{E}^{f}_{h,k,0}\oplus\mathcal{E}^{f}_{h}
, there is a framing of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H^{\#k}_{h},J^{\#k}_{h})_{\mathcal{E}^{f}_{h,k}}
such that the induced framing of

\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H^{\#k}_{h},J^{\#k}_{h})_{\mathcal{E}^{f}_{h,k}}^{C_{k}}
agrees under the previous isomorphism with the framing of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H_{h},J_{h})_{r,\mathcal{E}_{k}\oplus\mathcal{E}^{f}_{h,k,0}\oplus\mathcal{E}^{f}_{h}}
induced by applying Lemma
45
to the framing of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H_{h},J_{h})_{\mathcal{E}^{f}_{h}}
to get a framing of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H_{h},J_{h})_{r,\mathcal{E}^{f}_{h}}
and subsequently stabilizing. Moreover, when restricting to the objects of
\mathcal{C}(H_{ab},J_{ab})
, the framing of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H^{\#k}_{h},J^{\#k}_{h})_{\mathcal{E}^{f}_{h,k}}
is simply a the stabilization of the framing of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H^{\#k}_{ab},J^{\#k}_{ab})_{\mathcal{E}^{f}_{h,k}}
produced by applying Lemma
47
with
\mathcal{E}^{f}_{k,0}
in that lemma given by
\mathcal{E}^{f}_{h,0,ab}\oplus\mathcal{E}^{f}_{h,k,0}
.

Proof.

This follows completely analogously to Lemma
47
: we first choose a sufficiently large initial
\mathcal{E}^{f}_{h,k,0}
such that all the
E^{s}
-parameterizations of
\mathcal{V}^{1}_{h,k,ab}
underlying the parameterizations of the framings of the obstruction bundles of
\mathcal{C}(H^{\#k},J^{\#k}_{ab})_{r,\mathcal{E}^{f}_{h,k}\oplus\mathcal{E}^{f}_{h,0,ab}\oplus\mathcal{E}^{f}_{h,k,0}}
are sub-
E^{s}
-parameterizations of this; we then do the induction of Lemma
168
arranging that the vector space assigned to an object always contains the vector space assigned to that object by
\mathcal{E}^{f}_{h,k,0}
, and otherwise requiring that the data
\lambda_{V(x,y)}
and
\lambda_{V^{1}(x,y)}
satisfy the conditions of the lemma whenever the lemma statement in fact fixes these data.
∎

9
Background on Homotopy Theory

9.1
Conventions

For the entire paper we fix a Grothendieck Universe
\overline{\mathcal{U}}
. All model categories will consider will have all objects elements of
\overline{\mathcal{U}}
.

All groups denoted
G
will be compact Lie groups; in most cases they are finite groups. In Section
10.6
we will consider the group
C_{p^{\infty}}=(\varprojlim C_{p^{n}})\subset S^{1}
in a general sense, but we will never explicitly consider the action of this group on any spaces.

We will write
Top
for the category of compactly generated weak-Hausdorff (CGWH) spaces,
G-Top
for the category of (CGWH) spaces with continuous
G
-action, and
Top_{*},G-Top_{*}
for their based variants. All of these categories are enriched over themselves, are symmetric monoidal, and admit all small limits and colimits.

9.2
Model Categories

In this section, we briefly review what we need to know about model categories. A reference for most of what we need about model categories is
[
56
]
; note that we can always replace conditions on existence of all colimits with existence of all
\overline{\mathcal{U}}
-small colimits.

A model category
\mathcal{C}
comes with three classes of distinguished morphisms: the
cofibrations
,
fibrations
, and
weak equivalences
. These are each closed under composition. Model categories always admit initial objects
0
and final objects
1
; objects admitting a cofibration from
0
are called
cofibrant
, and objects admitting a fibration to
1
are called
fibrant
. A
trivial (co)fibration
is a morphism which is a (co)fibration and also a weak equivalence.

There is a functor from a model category
\mathcal{C}
to its homotopy category
Ho(\mathcal{C})
; this functor exhibits
Ho(\mathcal{C})
as a localization of
\mathcal{C}
at the weak equivalences. In particular, there is a functor
\mathcal{C}\to Ho(\mathcal{C})
, and given a weak equivalence
f:X\to Y
in
\mathcal{C}
, its image in
Ho(\mathcal{C})
has a canonical inverse.

Model categories
\mathcal{C}
admit
weak factorization systems
: given any morphism
f:X\to Y
, there is a factorization
X\to Z_{1}\to Y
where
X\to Z_{1}
is a cofibration and
Z_{1}\to Y
is a trivial fibration, and also a factorization
X\to Z_{2}\to Y
where
X\to Z_{2}
is a trivial cofibration and
Z_{2}\to Y
is a fibration. Moreover, these weak factorization systems are
functorial
in the sense that if we think of
X\xrightarrow{f}Y
as a diagram, then a map of diagrams extends to a map of factorization diagrams
X\to Y\to Z
.

In particular, applying the weak factorization to the canonical maps
0\to X
gives diagrams
0\to cX\to X
, functorial in
X
, where the first map is a cofibration and the second is a trivial fibration. Thus there is a cofibrant replacement functor
c:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
sending objects to cofibrant objects, and a natural transformation
c\to id
with all defining maps being trivial fibrations. Dually there is fibrant replacement functor
f:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
and a natural transformation
id\to f
with all defining maps trivial cofibrations.

We will repatedly use the following

Lemma 50
.

Cofibrant replacement preserves fibrancy of objects, and vice versa.

Proof.

The object
X
is fibrant if
X\to 1
is a fibration; but then
cX\to X
is a trivial fibration, so
cX\to 1
is a fibration because fibrations are closed under composition. The proof for the other statement is similar.
∎

There is a notion of
left homotopy
and
right homotopy
on morphisms from
X
to
Y
in
\mathcal{C}
. It is a fundamental theorem (e.g.
[
56
, Theorem 1.2.10]
) that if
X
is cofibrant and
Y
is fibrant then these notions coincide, homotopic maps are sent to the same morphism in the homotopy category, and the map

\mathcal{C}(X,Y)/\sim\to Ho(X,Y)
is an isomorphism, where the equivalence relation on the domain is homotopy of maps.

9.3
Orthogonal Spectra

In this section, we give a more detailed review of the properties of orthogonal spectra that we need. Throughout,
G
will be a finite group. Helpful references include
[
87
,
88
]
and the foundational
[
73
]
, as well as
[
53
]
. We will use the model structure on orthogonal spectra defined in
[
73
]
, although we will largely avoid any discussion of universes, as explained below.

Universes.

A
G
-universe is a direct sum of orthogonal
G
-representations which, if it contains a copy of an irreducible representation
V
of
G
, contains infinitely many copies of
V
. A complete
G
-universe is one that contains the regular representation.

Throughout this paper, we will fix a single complete
S^{1}
-universe
\mathcal{U}\supset\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
(where the inclusion is equivariant and
G
acts trivially on
\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
). Whenever we consider
C_{p^{k}}
-equivariant stable homotopy theory, we will use the complete
C_{p^{k}}
-universe obtained from
\mathcal{U}
by restricting along the inclusion map
C_{p^{k}}\subset S^{1}
.

For every closed subgroup
G\subset S^{1}
we fix a copy of an embedding of the regular representation
\rho_{G}\subset\mathcal{U}
.

Orthogonal spectra.

Given pair of finite-dimensional dimensional sub-
G
-representations
V,W\subset\mathcal{U}
, we have an associated
G
-space
O_{G}(V,W)
, which is the Thom space of the bundle
\xi(V,W)\to L(V,W)
, where
L(V,W)
is the space of linear isometric inclusions from
V
to
W
and the fiber of
\xi(V,W)
of over
\phi
is
(Im\phi)^{\perp}
. These assemble into into a topological category
O_{G}
; one definition of an orthogonal
G
-spectrum
[
73
, Definition 4.1]
is an functor
X:O_{G}\to G-Top_{*}
enriched over
G-Top_{*}
, the structure maps

O_{G}(V,W)\wedge X(V)\to X(W)

are
G
-equivariant. A map of
G
-spectra (with respect to the universe
\mathcal{U}
) is natural transformation of enriched functors.

Let
O
be the category of
O_{G}
consisting only of the representations
\mathbb{R}^{n}=\mathbb{R}^{n}\oplus 0^{\infty}\subset\mathbb{R}^{\infty}\subset\mathcal{U}
. The functor
X
can functorially and canonically recovered from the functor
X|_{O}:O\to G-Top_{*}
up to a canonical isomorphism, via the map

L(\mathbb{R}^{n},W)_{*}\wedge_{O^{\mathbb{R}^{n}}}X(\mathbb{R}^{n})\simeq X(W);

which arises from a factorization of the structure map
O_{G}(\mathbb{R}^{n},W)\wedge X(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to X(W)
using the fact that
O_{G}(\mathbb{R}^{n},W)=L(\mathbb{R}^{n},W)

[
88
, Remark 2.7]
.

We will
always
use the latter definition of an orthogonal
G
-spectrum as an enriched functor
O\to G-Top
. The category of orthogonal
G
-spectra in this sense will be denoted
G-OSp
. This category is a symmetric monoidal category.

However, when computing various quantities like fixed points we will define the associated functor
O_{G}\to G-Top_{*}
(where we
always use the one
G
-universe
U
) to do the computation, and then if the final object is an
H
-spectrum, we will restrict the resulting object back to
O
to produce a functor
O\to H-Top
. This convention will allow us to make clean statements about the interaction of various functors and change of groups.

In particular, given a closed inclusion of Lie groups
H\subset G
, an
H
-spectrum
X
, and a
G
-spectrum
Y
, it makes sense to talk about an
H
-equivariant map
f:X\to Y
by implicitly viewing
Y
as an
H
-spectrum via the forgetful functor
G-Top\to H-Top
. The associated map on spectra, called ‘group restriction’, is denoted

R^{G}_{H}:G-OSp\to H-OSp.

Thus, in the situation above, when we wish to be more precise we will write
f
as a map

f:X\to R^{G}_{H}Y.

However, due to a number of helpful properties of the group restriction functor will allow us to drop many instances of
R^{G}_{H}
from our notation, which will help both with typesetting and conceptual understanding.

Equivariant Homotopy Groups.

For
j\geq 0
have by definition that

\pi^{G}_{j}(X):=\colim_{n}[\mathbb{S}(\rho_{G}^{\oplus n}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{j}),X(\rho_{G}^{\oplus n})]_{G}

where
\rho_{G}
is the regular representation of
G
and
[\cdot,\cdot]_{G}
denotes equivariant homotopy classes of maps. Here
\mathbb{S}
is the
sphere spectrum
, reviewed below in the paragraph on free spectra.

For
j<0
we have that

\pi^{G}_{j}(X):=\colim_{n}[S(\rho_{G}^{\oplus n}),X(\rho_{G}^{\oplus n}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{j})]_{G},

Equivalences and model structures.

A
level equivalence
of orthogonal
G
-spectra is a map
X\to Y
such that the associated maps
X(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to Y(\mathbb{R}^{n})
are weak
G
-homotopy equivalences.

A
stable equivalence
of orthogonal
G
-spectra is a map of orthogonal
G
-spectra such that the associaed maps on
H
-stable homotopy groups
\pi^{H}_{k}
are isomorphisms for all closed subgroups
H\subset G
and all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.

The stable equivalences of orthogonal
G
-spectra form the the weak equivalences of several model structures on
G-OSp
. We will use the one defined in
[
73
, Theorem 2.4]
. This model structure has the property that the objects
F_{V}X
for all
G
-CW complexes
X
are always cofibrant, where
F_{V}
the free spectrum on the
G
-representation
V\subset\mathcal{U}
, a notion reviewed below. More generally, the cofibrations (called
q
-cofibrations in
[
73
]
) are generated by
F_{V}i
, where
i:(G/H\times S^{n-1})_{*}\to(G/H\times D^{n})_{*}
are the cell cofibrations.

Given a functor
F
on
G-OSp
with values in a category with weak equivalences, we will say that it is
homotopical on
X
if we would get a diagram of weak equivalences relating
F(X)
and
F(cfX)
from the factorization diagram. Similar language will be used for bifunctors; and if the target is not a category with an obvious model-category structure, we simply mean that the weak equivalences of the codomain of
F
are exactly the isomorphisms. A
derived
functor for
F
is a functor on
Ho(F)
that is naturally isomorphic to
Ho(Fcf)
.

Basics.

Given
X\in G-OSp
and
K\in G-Top_{*}
one can form
X\wedge K
by smashing
X
with
K
levelwise, i.e.

(X\wedge K)(V):=X(V)\wedge K,

and also form the mapping spectrum
[
88
, Example 5.2]

X^{K}(V):=G-Top_{*}(K,X(V)).

These form an adjoint pair:

G-OSp(X,Y^{K})\simeq G-OSp(A\wedge X,Y).

Similarly, given
Z\in G-OSp
, there is a mapping space

map(X,Z)\in G-Top_{*}

given by the set of natural transformations topologized as a subspace of
\prod_{n}G-Top_{*}(X(\mathbb{R}^{n}),Y^{(}\mathbb{R}^{n}))
.

We write
\Omega^{V}X=X^{S^{V}}
.

For the model structure of
[
73
, Theorem 2.4]
, given a cofibrant
X\in G-OSp
and a fibrant
Y\in G-OSp
, a pair of maps
f_{0},f_{1}:X\to Y
are homotopic in the sense of model categories if and only if there is a map

h:f:X\wedge[0,1]_{*}\to Y

such that its restrictions to
X\wedge\{i\}_{*}
agree with
f_{i}
for
i=0,1
.

Free Spectra.

For
V\in O_{G}
, one defines
free spectrum

F_{V}(W)=O_{G}(V,W),\text{ i.e. }F_{V}=O_{G}(V,\cdot).

(Note that the kinds of free spectra allowed
depend
on the universe being considered; but for each
G\subset S^{1}
we have fixed the universe that we are using, so this notation is unambiguous.)

There are canonical isomorphisms of orthogonal spectra
[
88
, Eq. 5.8]

F_{V}\wedge F_{W}\simeq F_{V\oplus W}.

More generally, for
K\in G-Top_{*}
, one sets

F_{V}K:=F_{V}\wedge K,

and we have isomorphisms of orthogonal spectra

F_{V}K_{1}\wedge F_{V}K_{2}\simeq F_{V\oplus W}K_{1}\wedge K_{2}.

The functor

G-Top_{*}\to G-OSp,K\mapsto F_{V}K

is left adjoint to the evaluation functor at
V
; thus there is a natural map

F_{V}A\to\Omega^{V}(\Sigma^{\infty}A)

adjoint to the adjunction unit (in
G-Top_{*}
)
A\to\Omega^{V}(A\wedge S^{V})=\Omega^{V}(\Sigma^{\infty}A)(V)
. This map turns out to be a stable equivalence
[
88
, Proposition 5.12]
.

The sphere spectrum is
\mathbb{S}:=F_{0}
. This makes sense as an object in
G-OSp
for any
G
, and for any closed Lie subgroup
H\subset G
we have
R^{G}_{H}\mathbb{S}=\mathbb{S}
. We write

\Sigma^{\infty}X=F_{0}X\in G-OSp

for a
G
-space
X
.

There are also canonical maps of orthogonal
G
-spectra

F_{W\oplus V}\Sigma^{V}X\to F_{W}X.

(179)

which are weak equivalences when
X
and
Y
are
G
-CW complexes.

Mapping Spectra.

Given
V\in O_{G}
and
X\in G-OSp
, there is a
shift spectrum

sh^{V}X(W)=X(V\oplus W)

with structure maps induced from the inclusions
O_{G}(W)\subset O_{G}(V\oplus W)
which are natural in
W
.

Given
X,Y\in G-OSp
, there is an internal mapping spectrum
F(X,Y)

[
88
, 5.11]
with

F(X,Y)(V)=map(X,sh^{V}Y).

The internal function spectrum makes
G-OSp
into a closed symmetric monoidal category with unit
\mathbb{S}

[
73
, Theorem 3.1]
. As usual, the composition maps

F(X,Y)\wedge X\to Y

are the counit of the adjunction

F(Y\wedge X,Z)\simeq F(Y,F(X,Z))

(valid for each
X
).

The mapping spectrum
F(X,Y)
is homotopical whenever
X
is cofibrant and
Y
is fibrant. This follows from
[
88
, Proposition 5.17]
and the characterization of the fibrant objects as
G-\Omega
spectra
[
73
, Theorem 2.4]
, namely those orthogonal
G
-spectra
X
such that for all closed subgroups
H\subset G
and all pairs of
H
-representations in the universe
\mathcal{U}
, the map
X(V)\to\Omega^{W}X(VoplusW)
adjoint to the structure map
X(V)\wedge S^{W}\to X(V\oplus W)
is a weak
H
-equivalence.

Geometric Fixed Points.

Given a closed
normal
subgroup
H\subset G
, there is a monoidal geometric fixed point functor

\Phi^{H}:G-OSp\to H-OSp.

There are several differing definitions which all give isomorphic functors on homotopy categories. We always use variant of the definition
[
73
]
which gives a monoidal functor;
this definition agrees with the definition in
[
53
]
under a nontrivial isomorphism, and differs from the definition in
[
88
]
.

We have the helpful propositions:

Proposition 29

(Proposition 4.5
[
73
]
)
.

For
X\in G-Top_{*}
, there is a natural isomorphism in
H-OSp

\Phi^{H}(F_{V}X)\simeq F_{V^{H}}X^{H}.

(180)

which preserves (
q
-)cofibrations and acyclic (
q
-)cofibrations.

The argument of
[
73
, Proposition 4.5]
shows also that

Lemma 51
.

The functor
\Phi^{H}
commutes with the formation of mapping telescopes (see below.)

Proposition 30

(Proposition 4.5
[
73
]
)
.

For
X,Y\in G-OSp
, there is a natural map in
G/H-OSp

\Phi^{H}X\wedge\Phi^{H}Y\to\Phi^{H}(X\wedge Y)

which is an isomorphism if one of
X
and
Y
are cofibrant.

The functor
\Phi^{H}X
is homotopical whenever
X
is cofibrant
[
73
, Prop. 4.12, Corr. 4.13]
.

Interactions with change of group.

We now compile several results about change of group.

Lemma 52

(Theorem IV.1.2
[
73
]
)
.

Let
H\subset G
be a closed subgroup of a compact Lie group
G
, and let
R^{G}_{H}
be the restriction functor from orthogonal
G
-spectra to orthogonal
H
-spetra. Then
R^{G}_{H}
preserves stable equivalences,
q
-fibrations, and
q
-cofibrations. In particular, they preserve fibrant and cofibrant objects.

It follows immediately from the definitions above there is an equality of bifunctors

F(R^{G}_{H}X,R^{G}_{H}Y)=R^{G}_{H}F(X,Y).

(181)

It follows immediately from
[
73
, Definition 4.1]
that we have an equality of functors

\Phi^{H}R^{G}_{G^{\prime}}=R^{G}_{G^{\prime}}\Phi^{H}.

(182)

Mapping Telescopes.

We will collate some results about mapping telescopes below. First, a mapping telescope of a family of spaces is

X_{0}\xrightarrow{f_{0}}X_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{f_{n-1}}X_{n}

is the pushout

Tel(X_{0}\to\cdots\to X_{n}):=(X_{0}(V)\times[0,1])\cup_{f^{\prime}_{1}}(X_{1}(V)\times[0,1])\cup_{f^{\prime}_{2}}\cdots\cup_{f^{\prime}_{n-1}}(X_{n}\times\{0\})

where
f^{\prime}_{k}:X_{k}\times\{1\}\to X_{k+1}\times\{0\}
is is just the action of
f_{k}
on the first factor. The mapping telescope of an infinite sequence

X_{0}\to X_{1}\to\cdots

(183)

is the colimit of the mapping telescope of the finite subsequences, i.e.

Tel(X_{0}\to X_{1}\to\cdots)=\colim_{n}(X_{0}\to X_{1}\to\cdots\to X_{n})

under the natural closed embeddings of these spaces. When all the
X_{i}
are
G
-spaces and
f_{i}
are
G
-maps then mapping telescopes inherit a
G
-action, and if all spaces involved are
G
-CW complexes and
G
-CW maps then the mapping telescope is a
G
-CW complex.

There are collapse maps

coll_{0,1}:Tel(X_{0}\to\cdots\to X_{n})\to Tel(X_{1}\to\cdots\to X_{n})

induced by the collapse map
X_{0}\times[0,1]\to X_{1}
, and they are (equivariant) homotopy equivalences, with explicit homotopy-inverse the closed inclusion

i_{1,0}:Tel(X_{1}\to\cdots\to X_{n})\subset Tel(X_{0}\to\cdots\to X_{n})

(184)

Taking colimits in
n
shows that the analogous collapse and inclusion maps (denoted with in the same way) on infinite mapping telescopes are
(G-)
-homotopy equivalences as well. One defines
coll_{a,b}=coll_{a,a+1}coll_{a+1,a+2}\cdots coll_{b-1,b}
and similarly for
i_{b,a}
.

Given a diagram like (
183
) of orthogonal
G
-spectra, the mapping telescope is defined by applying the above constructions level-wise. One thus immediately sees that if all
X_{i}
are cofibrant
G
-spectra then so is the mapping telescope. Taking mapping telescopes manifestly commutes with group restriction, and the collapse maps and their canonical homotopy inverses when applied level-wise define corresponding maps of mapping telescopes of orthogonal
G
-spectra which are also level equivalences.

The following lemma is standard:

Lemma 53
.

Consider a diagram of
X_{i}\in G-Top_{*}

X_{0}\subset X_{1}\subset X_{2}\subset\ldots\subset X=\colim_{i}X_{i}.

Then if all inclusions above are closed inclusions then the canonical map from the mapping telescope
Tel(X_{0}\to\ldots)\to X
is a weak equivalence.

We have the following useful standard result:

Lemma 54
.

Given a diagram of orthogonal
G
-spectra equipped with levelwise nondegenerate basepoints

Y_{0}\to Y_{1}\to\cdots

(with either finitely or infinitely many terms) where all maps are level-wise closed embeddings, we have a functorial isomorphism

\colim\pi_{G}^{j}(Y_{i})\simeq\pi_{G}^{j}(\colim Y_{i})

(185)

of equivariant homotopy groups.

Proof.

This is proven exactly like its analog for
G=1
, which is explained in
[
87
, Proposition 1.24]
. For
j\geq 0
, by
[
56
, Prop 2.4.2]
, any map from
S(\rho_{G}^{\oplus n}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{j})
to
\colim_{i}Y_{i}(\rho_{G}^{\oplus n})]_{G}
factors through some
Y_{i}(\rho_{G}^{\oplus n})]_{G}
, as does any homotopy between a pair of such maps. Thus the canonical map

\colim_{i}[S(\rho_{G}^{\oplus n}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{j}),Y_{i}(\rho_{G}^{\oplus n})]_{G}\to[S(\rho_{G}^{\oplus n}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{j}),\colim_{i}Y_{i}(\rho_{G}^{\oplus n})]_{G}

is an isomorphism.
Passing to colimits in
n
we conclude that the canonical map (
185
) is an isomorphism.

For
j<0
,
and a directly analogous argument goes through.

∎

This result easily implies the following helpful lemmata on mapping telescopes of equivariant orthogonal spectra:

Lemma 55
.

Given a (strictly) commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}

(186)

of orthogonal
G
-spectra with all the vertical arrows stable equivalences, the induced map on mapping telescopes

Tel(X_{0}\to X_{1}\to\cdots)\to Tel(X^{\prime}_{0})\to Tel(X^{\prime}_{0}\to X^{\prime}_{1}\to\cdots)

is a stable equivalence.

Proof.

A stable equivalence of orthogonal
G
-spectra is one for which the induced maps on
\pi^{H}_{k}
are isomorphisms for all subgroups
H\subset G
and all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. If the diagram (
186
) has finitely many entries and terminates with a morphism
X_{n}\to X^{\prime}_{n}
for some
n
, then there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tel(X_{0}\to X_{1}\to\cdots)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Tel(X^{\prime}_{0}\to X^{\prime}_{1}\to\cdots)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}_{n}}

(187)

where the horizontal arrows are the inclusion maps (and are thus level equivalences), the left vertical arrow is the induced map on mapping telescopes, and the right vertical arrow is the map in the diagram (
186
). In the case where (
186
) has infinitely many entries, the statement for finite-size diagrams implies that the map

\colim_{n}\pi^{H}_{j}(Tel(X_{0}\to\cdots\to X_{n}))\to\colim_{n}\pi^{H}_{j}(Tel(X^{\prime}_{0}\to\cdots\to X^{\prime}_{n}))

is an isomorphism for all
H,j
; the previous lemma then implies that the map between mapping telescopes is a stable equivalence.
∎

Lemma 56
.

Given a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{2}}

(188)

of orthogonal
G
-spectra which is
not
commutative, but such that one has chosen homotopies
h_{i}:X_{i}\wedge[0,1]_{*}\to X^{\prime}_{i+1}
from
f_{i+1}a_{i}
to
b_{i}f_{i}
, there is a map

Tel(X_{0}\to X_{1}\to\cdots)\to Tel(X^{\prime}_{0})\to Tel(X^{\prime}_{0}\to X^{\prime}_{1}\to\cdots)

(189)

defined by as the induced map on colimits of finite mapping telescopes by maps which, when evaluated on the representation
V
, map
X_{i}(V)\times\{0\}
to
X^{\prime}_{i}(V)\times\{0\}
via
f_{i}(V)
, and more generally map
X_{i}(V)\times[0,1]
to

X^{\prime}_{i}(V)\times[0,1]\cup_{b_{i}(V)}X^{\prime}_{i+1}\times\{0\}
via by sending
(x,t)\mapsto(h_{i}(x,2t-1),0)
for
t\geq 1/2
, and otherwise
(x,t)\mapsto(f_{i}(x),t/2)
for
t\leq 1/2
.

If all the vertical arrows of (
188
) are stable equivalences then the map (
189
) is a stable equivalence.

Proof.

The proof of this lemma is
identical
to the proof of the previous lemma.
∎

10
Floer Homotopy and its Properties

In this section, we apply the tools that we have developed in this paper to construct the genuine equivariant Floer homotopy type, as well as the cyclotomic structure on symplectic cohomology. First, in Section
10.1
, we will combine the results we have established so far and use these to define the (genuine equivariant) Floer homotopy and cohomotopy types
CF_{\bullet}(H,J;\mathbb{S})
and
CF^{\bullet}(H,J;\mathbb{S})
for admissible Floer data
(H,J)
. A-priori these depend on many choices, which we will refer to as the
homotopy definition data
; in this same section, we describe a stabilization of homotopy definition data, and explain how the geometric results we have established allow us to establish maps and relations between maps between these Floer homotopy types. Then, in Section
10.2
, we describe some general results about flow categories (which in fact are used in Section
10.1
), and use these and other arguments regarding the framings of our virtually smooth flow categories
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H,J)
to show that on homology, the spectra and maps of spectra defined in Section
10.2
define, when the homotopy definition data are chosen appropriately (and in particular when the Floer data are sufficiently regular), spectra and maps of spectra which correspond to the Floer chain complex and the Floer continuation maps on Floer homology. Subsequently, in Section
10.4
, we use these aforementioned results to prove invariance of the full genuine equivariant Floer homotopy types
CF_{\bullet}(H,J;\mathbb{S})
from all choices made.

We then proceed with the discussion of the cyclotomic structure on symplectic cohomology. In order to construct symplectic cohomology, we imitate the usual construction of symplectic cohomology as a colimit of Hamiltonian Floer homology groups.

Let us recall the construction of symplectic cohomology. Suppose that
(H,J)
is a
strongly nondegenerate
convex linear Floer datum on
(M,\omega,\lambda)
. Thus, the Floer data
(H^{\#k},J^{\#k})
are also convex linear nondegenerate Floer data, with the slope of
H^{\#k}
being
k
times the slope of
H
. Fix a prime
p
. If we choose a convex continuation datum
(H_{s},J_{s})
from
H_{1}
to
H^{\#p}
, then
(H^{\#p^{k}}_{s},J^{\#p^{k}}_{s})
is a convex continuation datum from
(H^{\#p^{k}},J^{\#p^{k}})
to
(H^{\#p^{k+1}},J^{\#p^{k+1}})
. If all the Floer data and continuation data are also regular, then we have a diagram of Floer cohomology groups

CF^{*}(H,J)\to CF^{*}(H^{\#p},J^{\#p})\to\cdots.

The symplectic cohomology
SH^{*}(M)
is the colimit of the corresponding cohomology groups

HF^{*}(H,J)\to HF^{*}(H^{\#p},J^{\#p})\to\cdots.

(190)

It turns out to be independent of all the data chosen above up to canonical isomorphism
[
4
]
.

To construct spectral symplectic cohomology
SH^{\bullet}(M;\mathbb{S})
one imitates this construction. The geometric constructions of the paper (reviewed in Section
10.1
below) allow us to define Floer cohomotopy types

CF^{\bullet}(H^{\#k},J^{\#k};\mathbb{S})_{\mathfrak{H}^{k}}:=F(|\mathcal{C}(H^{\#k},J^{\#k})|_{\mathfrak{H}^{k}},\mathbb{S}).

where
|\mathcal{C}(H^{\#k},J^{\#k})|_{\mathfrak{H}^{k}}
is the orthogonal spectrum associated to a virtual smoothing of the proper flow category
\mathcal{C}(H^{\#k},J^{\#k})
, together with a framing, an embedding of the framing, and so forth, as needed in Sections
4
-
8
; all the choices made in the construction are denoted by
\mathfrak{H}^{k}
, and are referred to as the
homotopy definition data
. Here, the function spectrum
F(\cdot,\mathbb{S})
is taken in the derived sense. The constructions of this paper allow us to define a diagram of orthogonal spectra

CF^{\bullet}(H,J;\mathbb{S})_{\mathfrak{H}^{1}}\to CF^{\bullet}(H^{\#p},J^{\#p};\mathbb{S})_{\mathfrak{H}^{p}}\to CF^{\bullet}(H^{\#p^{2}},J^{\#p^{2}};\mathbb{S})_{\mathfrak{H}^{p^{2}}}\to\cdots

(191)

These maps lift the maps (
190
) in the sense that upon taking (derived) smash product with
H\mathbb{Z}
and subsequently taking stable homotopy groups one recovers the maps (
190
). Thus, the spectral symplectic cohomology reduces to the usual symplectic cohomology upon smashing with
H\mathbb{Z}
and taking homotopy groups as well.

Constructing the cyclotomic structure is somewhat more challenging due to the potentially large numbers of compatibility conditions involved. As stated in the introduction, we use the definition of a
genuine
p
-cyclotomic spectrum
, originally defined by Hesselholt-Madsen
[
51
]
; we use closely related variant of this definition introduced by Nikolaus-Scholze
[
77
]
which still uses genuine equivariant spectra, but uses
\infty
-categories to keep track of some of the coherence data required. We wish to show that
SH^{\bullet}(M;\mathbb{S})
is the underlying spectrum of a genuine
p
-cyclotomic spectrum for every
p
. (We do not work out the compatibility conditions between different primes
p
in this paper, although it is essentially a tedious exercise given the tools developed in this paper.) The notion of a genuine
p
-cyclotomic spectrum, reviewed in Section
10.6
, is purely
\infty
-categorical, while our constructions are concrete and geoometric. Now, the symmetries of the geometric situation translate furnish part of the data needed for the
\infty
-categorical notion. Indeed, a a genuine
p
-cyclotomic spectrum
X
is, for each
\ell
, a
C_{p^{\ell}}
-spectrum
X_{\ell}
, such that
X_{\ell-1}
is weakly equivalent to
F^{C_{p^{\ell}}}_{C_{p^{\ell-1}}}X_{\ell}
, together with cyclotomic structure equivalences

X_{\ell-1}\to\Phi^{C_{p}}X_{\ell}

(192)

such that all this data is compatible up to homotopy as
k
varies. The geometry of the virtual smoothings of the flow categories of Floer moduli spaces that we have established in the earlier portions of this paper gives us each piece of this structure. To define
X_{\ell}
, we consider the diagram (
191
) over all
k\geq\ell
, and note that the relevant flow categories are naturally
C_{p^{\ell}}
-equivariant, and all the smoothings, framings, etc. can be chosen to be so as well. As such, the construction of this paper give us a lift of that part of the diagram (
191
) thought of as a diagram in the homotopy category of spectra to a diagram of
C_{p^{\ell}}
-spectra; taking the homotopy colimit in this latter category defines
X_{\ell}
. The equivalences (
192
) are then defined by taking the map on homotopy colimits induced by the weak equivalences

CF^{\bullet}(H^{\#p^{k-1}},J^{\#p^{k-1}};\mathbb{S})_{\mathfrak{H}^{p^{k-1}}}\simeq\Phi^{C_{p}}CF^{\bullet}(H^{\#p^{k}},J^{\#p^{k}};\mathbb{S})_{\mathfrak{H}^{p^{k}}}

which arise essentially for free from Theorem
8
once one has done all constructions equivariantly, as detailed earlier in this paper.

Thus the geometric constructions give the input to the data of a genuine
p
-cyclotomic spectrum. The challenge is to verify that the compatibility conditions betwen these various maps required by the definition of a genuine
p
-cyclotomic structure hold. All geometric choices to be made are made in the straightforward Section
10.3
(which combines many geometric results in the paper). This data is then carefully repackaged in Sections
10.5
and Section
10.6
. The first basic issue is that one is working with many different categories of genuine equivariant spectra (specifically, orthogonal
C_{p^{k}}
spectra for varying
k
)
for which the notions of cofibrant and fibrant spectra are all distinct
. Using summary of the geometric results of Section
10.1
, we explain, in Section
10.5
, how to take a sequence of cofibrant-fibrant replacements of the resulting objects and maps in the varying model categories of orthogonal spectra, as well as explicit models of homotopy colimits of the resulting diagrams, in order to define certain relatively concrete representatives of the objects
X_{\ell}
in the categories of
C_{p^{\ell}}
-orthogonal spectra. Subsequently, using the explicit nature of the homotopy colimits and the sequences of fibrant-cofibrant replacements taken, we show in Section
10.6
that this process also gives the compatibility data needed for these maps to define a lift of
SH^{\bullet}(M;\mathbb{S})
to the category of genuine
p
-cyclotomic spectra.

We now proceed with the construction.

10.1
Basic Results on Floer Homotopy

In this section we use the earlier geometric geometric constructions of this paper in order to define Floer homotopy types and define relationships between them. We will work with two kinds of homotopy types:
homological
and
cohomological
. One will be the Spanier-Whitehead dual of the other, and the relationship between them be analogous to the relationship between Floer
homology
and Floer
cohomology
. The dualization from homological Floer homotopy types to cohomological Floer homotopy types will be introduced to reverse the directions of various maps defined by virtually smooth flow categories, which is important when defining symplectic cohomology since in that setting the continuation maps used in the definition of symplectic cohomology only go one way, in the direction of increasing slope of the Hamiltonian.

Throughout, we are working with an admissible symplectic manifold.

Remark 63
.

Below, whenever we consider free spectra
F_{V}
for
V
a
C_{p^{k}}
-representation, we find an
S^{1}
-representation
V^{\prime}
which gives
V
upon restriction, and fix an embedding of
V^{\prime}
into
\mathcal{U}
. Often we will have pairs of representations
V_{1}\subset V_{2}
where
V_{1}
is a
C_{p^{k}}
-representation,
V_{2}
is a
C_{p^{\ell}}
representation for
\ell\geq k
, and the embedding is
C_{p^{\ell}}
-equivariant. In that case, if we have already picked a lift of
V_{1}
to an
S^{1}
-represetation
V^{\prime}_{1}
and an embedding
V^{\prime}_{1}\subset\mathcal{U}
, we proceed by choosing a lift of
V_{2}
an
S^{1}
-representation
V^{\prime}_{2}
which contains
V^{\prime}_{1}
as a sub-representation, and extend of
V^{\prime}_{1}
to an embedding of
V^{\prime}_{2}
. In the cases we consider it will be straightforward to see that such embeddings exist and that their choices of embeddings do not affect the results up to isomorphism.

Proposition 31
.

Let
(H,J)
be an admissible Floer datum on
M
. Then, given a choice of integralization data
\mathfrak{A}
, there is a proper flow category
\mathcal{C}(H,J)
. There exists a compatible regular system of perturbation data
\mathfrak{P}
with underlying integralization data
\mathfrak{A}
and a smooth structure on the virtual smoothing
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H,J)
of
\mathcal{C}(H,J)
associated to
\mathfrak{P}
. If the polarization class
\rho\in KO^{1}(LM)
of Section
8.6
vanishes, then, by choosing a trivialization of the polarization map
\tilde{\rho}
, the virtual smoothing can be framed, and admits extension
\bar{\mathbf{E}}
of an embedding
\mathbf{E}
. If
(H,J)
are
C_{k}
equivariant, and the polarization class
\rho\in KO^{1}_{C_{k}}(LM)
of Section
8.6
vanishes, then all of the above data can be chosen to be equivariant. The associated stable homotopy type is the orthogonal spectrum

|\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H,J)|=F_{\tilde{V}^{1}}\{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H,J)\}

(193)

where
\tilde{V}^{1}
is the
shift space
associated to
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
(Remark
38
and Definition
63
) and
\{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H,J)\}
is a
C_{k}
-space with the equivariant homotopy type of a
C_{k}
-CW complex. This orthogonal spectrum depends on all of the data described above; we call the data
\mathfrak{H}=(\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{P},\tilde{\rho},\mathbf{E},\bar{\mathbf{E}})
the
homotopy definition data
, and we will refer to
\tilde{V}^{1}=\tilde{V}^{1}_{\mathfrak{H}}
and
\{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H,J)\}_{\mathfrak{H}}
when we wish to keep track of the dependence on the homotopy definition data.

We will define the
Floer homotopy type
associated to
\mathfrak{H}
to be

CF_{\bullet}(H,J)_{\mathfrak{H}}:=|\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H,J)|_{\mathfrak{H}}:=F_{\tilde{V}^{1}_{\mathfrak{H}}}\{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H,J)\}_{\mathfrak{H}}.

The
Floer cohomotopy type
associated to
\mathfrak{H}
is the (equivariant) Spanier-Whitehead dual of the above object in the (equivariant) stable homotopy category, and is represented in the equivariant stable homotopy category by the function spectrum

CF^{\bullet}(H,J)_{\mathfrak{H}}:=F(|\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H,J)_{\mathfrak{H}}|,f\mathbb{S}),

where
f
denotes fibrant replacement.

Proof of Proposition
31
.

This follows from a combination of the results we have established in this paper. Namely, we invoke Propositions
7
,

16
, and
28

to produce the virtually smooth framed flow category
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H,J)
. To produce the embedding and its extension we invoke Propositions
3
and Lemma
19
. The definition of
\{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H,J)\}
is given in Definitions
33
and
46
, and the statement that it has the homotopy type of a
G
-CW complex is Proposition
2
.
∎

In order to relate these homotopy types, we will use the continuation map construction, for which virtual smoothings are constructed in Section
7.8
. We first need to introduce the notion of a stabilization of a homotopy definition datum:

Definition 90
.

Let
\mathfrak{H}^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{H}
be homotopy definition data for
\mathcal{C}(H,J)
. We say that
\mathfrak{H}^{\prime}
is a stabilization of
\mathfrak{H}
if the following conditions hold:

1.

The integralization datum
\bar{\mathfrak{A}}^{\prime}
underlying
\mathfrak{H}^{\prime}
is an extension of the integralization datum
\bar{\mathfrak{A}}
underlying
\mathfrak{H}
in the sense of Lemma
32
, and

2.

Writing
\mathfrak{P}
and
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}
for the perturbation data underlying
\mathfrak{H}
and
\mathfrak{H}^{\prime}
, respectively, we have that there is an isomorphism
f
of virtually smooth equivariant flow categories from
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H,J)_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}
to (a restriction of)
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H,J)_{\mathfrak{P},r,\mathcal{E}}
, which is the stabilization by an
E^{s}
-parameterization
\mathcal{E}
of the restratification
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H,J)_{\mathfrak{P},r}
of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H,J)_{\mathfrak{P}}
associated to the fact that
\bar{\mathfrak{A}}^{\prime}
is an extension of
\bar{\mathfrak{A}}

3.

There is an isomorphism
f^{E}:\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{E}^{s}
, of
E^{s}
-parameterizations, where
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
is the
E^{s}_{2}
-parameterization underlying
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H,J)_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}
, and
\mathcal{E}^{s}
is the
E^{s}_{2}
-parameterization underlying the framing of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H,J)_{\mathfrak{P},r,\mathcal{E}}
induced from that of
\mathcal{C}(H,J)^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{P}}
by Lemmas
45
and
8.7
. Moreover,
under the identifications
f
and
f^{E}
, the framing of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H,J)_{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}
agrees with the framing of
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H,J)_{\mathfrak{P},r,\mathcal{E}}
induced by these same Lemmas.

4.

Furthermore,the embeddings underlying
\mathfrak{H}^{\prime}
agree (under
f
and the map
f^{F}
on
F^{s}_{2}
-parameterizations induced by
f^{E}
) with the embeddings induced from the embeddings underlying
\mathfrak{H}
via Lemma
20
and
17
, and similarly the extension of the embedding underlying
\mathfrak{H}^{\prime}
is given (under
f^{F}
) by applying Lemmas
20
and
17
.

The same definition applies to make sense of stabilizations of homotopy definition data for
\mathcal{C}(H_{s},J_{s})
or
\mathcal{C}(H_{h},J_{h})
, where
(H_{s},J_{s})
are convex continuation data and
(H_{h},J_{h})
are convex continuation homotopy data.

Proposition 32
.

Say
\mathfrak{H}^{\prime}
is a stabilization of of the homotopy definition datum
\mathfrak{H}
. Using the notation of Definition
90
, write
V^{1}_{\mathcal{E}}
and
\mathcal{V}^{1}_{\mathcal{E}^{s}}
for the shift spaces underlying
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{E}^{s}
, and write

\Delta\bar{A}_{max}=|\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}|\setminus|\mathfrak{A}|

where
(\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{A}^{\prime})
are the marked actions underlying the integralization data
(\bar{\mathfrak{A}},\bar{\mathfrak{A}}^{\prime})
, respectively. Then there is an equivariant homeomorphism

\{\mathcal{C}(H,J)\}_{\mathfrak{H}^{\prime}}=\Sigma^{V^{1}_{\mathcal{E}}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{\Delta\bar{A}_{max}}+M}\{\mathcal{C}(H,J)\}_{\mathfrak{H}}

(194)

and an isomorphism of orthogonal spectra

|\mathcal{C}(H,J)|_{\mathfrak{H}^{\prime}}\simeq F_{V^{1}_{\mathcal{E}}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{\Delta\bar{A}_{max}}}\Sigma^{V^{1}_{\mathcal{E}}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{\Delta\bar{A}_{max}}+M}|\mathcal{C}(H,J)|_{\mathfrak{H}},

(195)

where
M
is the restratification embedding shift of Lemma
20
.
Moreover we can replace
(H,J)
with
(H_{s},J_{s})
or with
(H_{h},J_{h})
in any of the statements above.

Proof.

The homeomorphism (
194
) follows immediately from Property (2) of Definition
90
together with Proposition
6
and Lemma
18
.

The isomorphism (
195
) follows from (
194
) together with the statement that, writing
V^{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{0}}
for the shift space associated to
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H,J)_{\mathfrak{P}}
, we have an equivariant isomorphism

V^{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{0}}\oplus V^{1}_{\mathcal{E}}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{\Delta\bar{A}_{max}}\simeq V^{1}_{\mathcal{E}^{s}}.

(196)

But this follows from Property (3) of Definition
90
together with the fact that in the context
of Lemma
45
, the shift space associated to the part of the
E^{s}
-parameterization
V^{1}_{can}
of that lemma changes exactly by
\mathbb{R}^{\Delta\bar{A}_{max}+M}\oplus V^{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{1}}
for some semi-free parameterization
\mathcal{E}_{1}
(see Remark
50
, in the context of which
M=\Delta\mathfrak{A}_{lower}
) while in the context of Lemma
8.7
, we simply add
\mathcal{V}^{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{2}}
to the shift space for some semi-free parameterization
\mathcal{E}_{2}
; and in the end we have
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{E}_{2}
. Combining these statements gives exactly (
196
). The proofs with
(H,J)
replaced by
(H_{s},J_{s})
or
(H_{h},J_{h})
are identical.
∎

Below we summarize the geometric data associated to continuation maps:

Proposition 33
.

Let
(H_{\pm},J_{\pm})
be a pair of admissible floer data for
M
, and let
(H_{s},J_{s})
be admissible continuation data from
(H_{-},J_{-})
to
(H_{+},J_{+})
. If the polarization class
\rho\in KO^{1}(LM)
vanishes, then, given homotopy definition data
\mathfrak{H}_{\pm}
for
|\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H_{\pm},J_{\pm})|
, there exists a homotopy definition datum
\mathfrak{H}
defining a virtual smoothing
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H_{s},J_{s})
of
\mathcal{C}(H,J)
, such that the restrictions of
\mathfrak{H}
to
\mathcal{C}(H_{\pm},J_{\pm})
are stabilizations of
\mathfrak{H}_{\pm}
, and such that the integral actions are given by shifts of the original integral actions for
\mathcal{C}(H_{\pm},J_{\pm})
as in (
102
). Moreover, if either of
\mathfrak{H}_{\pm}
are
C_{k}
-equivariant, and the polarization class
\rho\in KO^{1}_{C_{k}}(LM)
vanishes then the restriction of
\mathfrak{H}
to the corresponding flow subcategory
\mathcal{C}(H_{\pm},J_{\pm})
can be chosen to carry a
C_{k}
equivariant structure, with the stabilization from
\mathfrak{H}_{\pm}
to
\mathfrak{H}|_{\mathcal{C}(H_{\pm},J_{\pm})}
an equivariant stabilization.In particular, the shift spaces
(\tilde{V}^{1}_{+},\tilde{V}^{1}_{-},\tilde{V}^{1})
associated to
(\mathfrak{H}_{-},\mathfrak{H}_{+},\mathfrak{H})
satisfy
\tilde{V}^{1}=\tilde{V}^{1}_{\pm}\oplus\tilde{V}^{1}_{\pm,s}
, where this decomposition is a decomposition into
C_{k}
-representations if
(H_{\pm},J_{\pm})
is
C_{k}
-equivariant.

We have an inclusion

\Sigma^{V^{1}_{+,s}}\{\mathcal{C}(H_{+},J_{+})\}_{\mathfrak{H}_{+}}\subset\{\mathcal{C}(H_{s},J_{s})\}_{\mathfrak{H}}

(197)

which is an equivariant homeomorphism onto its image, and the quotient by the inclusion is equivariantly homeomorphic to a shift of the space underlying
|\mathcal{C}(H_{-},J_{+})|
:

\{\mathcal{C}(H_{s},J_{s})\}_{\mathfrak{H}}/\left(\Sigma^{V^{1}_{+,s}}\{\mathcal{C}(H_{+},J_{+})\}_{\mathfrak{H}_{+}}\right)\simeq\Sigma^{\mathbb{R}\oplus V^{1}_{-,s}}\{\mathcal{C}(H_{-},J_{-})\}_{\mathfrak{H}_{-}}\simeq\{\mathcal{C}(H_{-},J_{-})\}_{\mathfrak{H}}.

(198)

Proof.

Write
\mathfrak{P}_{\pm}
for the perturbation data underlying
\mathfrak{H}_{\pm}
. We choose integralization data via Lemma
30
. We then initially choose perturbation data via Lemma
19
, and then subsequently a trivial stabilization of these perturbation data which gives the associated topological smoothing a smooth structure via Lemma
20
(see Definition
83
). This virtual smoothing is framed via Lemmas
39
and
46
. Choosing
\mathcal{C}_{1}=\mathcal{C}(H_{-},J_{-})
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}=\mathcal{C}(H_{+},J_{+})
in Proposition
4
and using Lemma
17
to define
\mathbf{E}_{i}
and
\bar{\mathbf{E}}_{i}
in this Proposition defines the embedding and the extension of the embedding.

We now apply Lemma
59
below; the inclusion (
197
) is simply (
208
) applied to this setting. Similarly, the homeomorphism (
198
) is simply (
210
) combined with Proposition
32
(here
M=1
, as per Remark
50
; see also (
196
)).

∎

We can use this geometric construction to produce a map of orthogonal spectra:

Proposition 34
.

The data
\mathfrak{H}
as in Proposition
33
determines a map
f
, unique up to (equivariant) homotopy, of the form

\Sigma^{\mathbb{R}\oplus V^{1}_{-},s}\{\mathcal{C}(H_{-},J_{-})\}_{\mathfrak{H}_{-}}\xrightarrow{f}\Sigma^{\mathbb{R}\oplus V^{1}_{+},s}\{\mathcal{C}(H_{+},J_{+})\}_{\mathfrak{H}_{+}},

(199)

which in turns defines the map
\bar{f}=F_{\mathbb{R}\oplus V^{1}_{\mathfrak{H}}}f
on orthogonal spectra sitting in the diagram below:

\begin{gathered}|\mathcal{C}(H_{-},J_{-})|_{\mathfrak{H}_{-}}\leftarrow F_{\mathbb{R}\oplus V^{1}_{-,s}}\Sigma^{\mathbb{R}\oplus V^{1}_{-,s}}|\mathcal{C}(H-,J_{-})|_{\mathfrak{H}_{-}}\simeq\\
F_{\mathbb{R}\oplus V^{1}}\Sigma^{\mathbb{R}\oplus V^{1}_{-},s}\{\mathcal{C}(H_{-},J_{-})\}_{\mathfrak{H}_{-}}\xrightarrow{\bar{f}}F_{\mathbb{R}\oplus V^{1}}\Sigma^{\mathbb{R}\oplus V^{1}_{+},s}\{\mathcal{C}(H_{+},J_{+})\}_{\mathfrak{H}_{+}}\\
\simeq F_{\mathbb{R}\oplus V^{1}_{+,s}}\Sigma^{\mathbb{R}\oplus V^{1}_{+,s}}|\mathcal{C}(H_{+},J_{+})|_{\mathfrak{H}_{+}}\to|\mathcal{C}(H_{+},J_{+})|_{\mathfrak{H}_{+}}.\end{gathered}

(200)

Here the top arrow pointing left and the bottom arrow pointing right denote the canonical weak equivalences (
179
). In particular, the above diagram defines a map in
[f]
in the homotopy category from
|\mathcal{C}(H_{-},J_{-})|_{\mathfrak{H}_{-}}\to|\mathcal{C}(H_{+},J_{+})|_{\mathfrak{H}_{+}}
.

Proof.

The existence of the map
f
follows from Lemma
60
stated and proven below. To produce
f
, we apply that Lemma with
\{\mathcal{C}\}=\{\mathcal{C}(H_{s},J_{s})\}_{\mathfrak{H}}
, so
\{\mathcal{C}_{2}\}=\{\mathcal{C}(H_{+},J_{+}\}_{\mathfrak{H}}
, and thus
\Sigma^{V_{2}}\{\mathcal{C}_{2}\}=\Sigma^{V^{1}_{+},s}\{\mathcal{C}(H_{+},J_{+})\}_{\mathfrak{H}_{+}}
. Meanwhile, one gets that
\{\mathcal{C}_{1}\}=\{\mathcal{C}(H_{-},J_{-})\}_{\mathfrak{H}}\simeq\Sigma^{\mathbb{R}\oplus V^{1}_{-,s}}\{\mathcal{C}(H_{-},J_{-})\}_{\mathfrak{H}_{-}}
by (
198
). Thus the map
f:\{\mathcal{C}_{1}\}\to\Sigma^{\mathbb{R}\oplus V_{2}}\{\mathcal{C}_{2}\}
produced by that lemma takes the the form of (
199
).

To conclude it suffices to explain the isomorphisms on the top right and the bottom left of (
200
). The top right follows from the fact that
|\mathcal{C}(H_{-},J_{-})|_{\mathfrak{H}_{-}}=F_{V^{1}_{-}}\{\mathcal{C}(H_{-},J_{-})\}_{\mathfrak{H}_{-}}
and the fact that
V^{1}_{-,s}\oplus V^{1}_{-}=V^{1}
; and the one on the bottom left follows by a similar argument.
∎

Finally, we can use the constructions regarding continuation homotopy data of Section
7.10
to produce commutative diagrams associated to maps of Floer homotopy types. Below we summarize the geometric results proven in the paper.

Proposition 35
.

Suppose that the polarization class
\rho\in KO^{1}_{C_{k}}(LM)
vanishes, and we are given
C_{k}
-equivariant homotopy continuation data
(H_{h},J_{h})
as well as

•

Homotopy definition data
\mathfrak{H}_{a}
,

for the associated floer data
(H_{a},J_{a})
, and

•

homotopy definition data
\mathfrak{H}_{ab}
,
(a,b)\in\{(1,2),(1,3),(2,4),(3,4)\}
for the associated continuation data
(H_{ab},J_{ab})
, such that for every such
(a,b)
the homotopy definition data

obtained from

by restriction to

are stabilizations of
\mathfrak{H}_{a}
, and similarly the homotopy definition data

are stabilizations of
\mathfrak{H}_{b}
, and

•

The integralization data underlying the
\mathfrak{H}_{ab}
satisfy the conditions of Lemma
37
.

Moreover, all of the above are assumed to be
C_{k}
-equivariant. Then there exists a homotopy definition datum
\mathfrak{H}_{h}
for
\mathcal{C}(H_{h},J_{h})
which such that the homotopy definition data

for
\mathcal{C}(H_{ab},J_{ab})
obtained from
\mathfrak{H}_{h}
by restriction are all stabilizations of
\mathfrak{H}_{ab}
, for
(a,b)\in\{(1,2),(1,3),(2,4),(3,4)\}
. Write
(V^{1}_{h},\{V^{1}_{ab}\},\{V^{1}_{a}\})
for the shift spaces assoicated to

. Then there are
G
-vector spaces
(\{V^{1}_{ab,s}\},\{V^{1}_{a,\tilde{s}}\},\{V^{1}_{a,s_{ab}}\})
and decompositions

V^{1}_{h}=V^{1}_{ab}\oplus V^{1}_{ab,s}=V^{1}_{a}\oplus V^{1}_{a,\tilde{s}},V^{1}_{ab}=V^{1}_{a}\oplus V^{1}_{a,s_{ab}}

(the last decomposition are the decompositions of shift spaces described in Proposition

). This forces additional isomorphisms

V^{1}_{a,\tilde{s}}=V^{1}_{a,s_{ab}}\oplus V^{1}_{ab,s}=V^{1}_{a,s_{ab^{\prime}}}\oplus V^{1}_{ab^{\prime},s}

V^{1}_{b,\tilde{s}}=V^{1}_{b,s_{ab}}\oplus V^{1}_{ab,s}=V^{1}_{a^{\prime},s_{a^{\prime}b}}\oplus V^{1}_{a^{\prime}b,s}.

Writing
\mathcal{C}_{i}
for

for the subcategory on the objects of

as

\Sigma^{V^{1}_{4,\tilde{s}}}\{\mathcal{C}_{4}\}_{\mathfrak{H}_{4}}\to\{\mathcal{C}_{234}\}_{\mathfrak{H}},\{\mathcal{C}_{234}\}_{\mathfrak{H}}/\Sigma^{V^{1}_{4,\tilde{s}}}\{\mathcal{C}_{4}\}_{\mathfrak{H}_{4}}\simeq\Sigma^{\mathbb{R}\oplus V^{1}_{2,\tilde{s}}}\{\mathcal{C}_{2}\}_{\mathfrak{H}_{2}}\vee\Sigma^{\mathbb{R}\oplus V^{1}_{3,\tilde{s}}}\{\mathcal{C}_{3}\}_{\mathfrak{H}_{3}}.

(201)

Moreover there is an inclusion
\{\mathcal{C}_{234}\}_{\mathfrak{H}}\to\{\mathcal{C}(H_{h},J_{h})\}_{\mathfrak{H}}
and composing the previous inclusion with this one gives an inclusion

\Sigma^{V^{1}_{4,\tilde{s}}}\{\mathcal{C}_{4}\}_{\mathfrak{H}_{4}}\to\{\mathcal{C}(H_{h},J_{h})\}_{\mathfrak{H}},\{\mathcal{C}(H_{h},J_{h})\}_{\mathfrak{H}}/\Sigma^{V^{1}_{4,\tilde{s}}}\{\mathcal{C}_{4}\}_{\mathfrak{H}_{4}}\simeq\{\mathcal{C}_{123}\}_{\mathfrak{H}};

(202)

In particular by the previous two claims there is an inclusion

\Sigma^{\mathbb{R}\oplus V^{1}_{2,\tilde{s}}}\{\mathcal{C}_{2}\}_{\mathfrak{H}_{2}}\vee\Sigma^{\mathbb{R}\oplus V^{1}_{3,\tilde{s}}}\{\mathcal{C}_{3}\}_{\mathfrak{H}_{3}}\xrightarrow{i_{23}}\{\mathcal{C}_{123}\}_{\mathfrak{H}},\{\mathcal{C}_{123}\}_{\mathfrak{H}}/Imi_{23}\simeq\Sigma^{\mathbb{R}\oplus\mathbb{R}\oplus V^{1}_{1,\tilde{s}}}\{\mathcal{C}_{1}\}_{\mathfrak{H}_{1}}.

(203)

Proof.

We choose the integralization data via

, the perturbation data via

, the smoothings via

, and the framings via

and Lemma

.

\mathcal{C}_{1}=\mathcal{C}(H_{1},J_{2})
and

in Proposition

and using Lemma

to define
\mathbf{E}_{i}
and

in this Proposition defines the embedding and the extension of the embedding.

All homeomorphisms are produced (as above in the proof of Proposition

giving rise to the

and

Lemma 57
.

Given two maps of pointed Hausdorff spaces
g_{1},g_{2}:X\to Y
, when
\overline{g_{1}^{-1}(*)^{c}}\cap\overline{g_{2}^{-1}(*)^{c}}=\emptyset
, write
g_{1}\underline{\vee}g_{2}X\to Y_{*}
for the map defined by
g_{1}\underline{\vee}g_{2}(x)=g_{i}(x)
if
x\in g_{i}^{-1}(*)^{c}
for
i=1,2
, and otherwise
g_{1}\underline{\vee}g_{2}(x)=*
; by the non-intersection condition this is a well-defined continuous map. When
X
and
Y
are pointed CW complexes, this map represents the class of
g_{1}+g_{2}
in the stable homotopy category.

Proof.

This occurs because of a canonical homotopy. Indeed, given any two maps
g_{1},g_{2}:X\to Y
, there one can define the class of the map
g_{1}+g_{2}
concatenating the maps
\Sigma g_{1}
and
\Sigma g_{2}
along the suspension coordinate, i.e. by composing
\Sigma g_{1}\wedge\Sigma g_{2}
with the natural map
\Sigma X\to\Sigma X\wedge\Sigma X
; and then formally desuspending this map. But this latter map is homotopic to
\Sigma g_{1}\underline{\vee}\Sigma g_{2}
whenever the latter makes sense.
∎

Lemma 58
.

We can choose the maps associated by Proposition
34
to the data (
201
) to be
\Sigma^{V^{1}_{24,s}}f_{24}\vee(-\Sigma^{V^{1}_{34,s}}f_{34})
, where the
(-\Sigma^{V^{1}_{34,s}}f_{34})
denotes the composition of reflection in
\mathbb{R}
shift coordinate of
\Sigma^{R\oplus V^{1}_{3,\tilde{s}}}\{\mathcal{C}_{3}\}_{\mathfrak{H}_{3}}
with
(\Sigma^{V^{1}_{34,s}}f_{34})
. Moreover,
\{\mathcal{C}_{123}\}_{\mathfrak{H}}
is homotopy equivalent to the cone on
\Sigma^{V^{1}_{12,s}}f_{12}\underline{\vee}\Sigma^{1}_{13,s}f_{13}
, and the map
\{\mathcal{C}_{123}\}_{\mathfrak{H}}\to\Sigma^{V^{1}_{4,\tilde{s}}\{\mathcal{C}_{4}\}_{\mathfrak{H}_{4}}}
associated by Proposition
34
to (
202
) can be taken to agree with
\Sigma^{V^{1}_{24,s}}f_{24}\vee\Sigma^{V^{1}_{34,s}}f_{34}
on the images of
\Sigma^{\mathbb{R}\oplus V^{1}_{2,\tilde{s}}}\{\mathcal{C}_{2}\}_{\mathfrak{H}_{2}}\vee\Sigma^{\mathbb{R}\oplus V^{1}_{3,\tilde{s}}}\{\mathcal{C}_{3}\}_{\mathfrak{H}_{3}}
in
\{\mathcal{C}_{123}\}_{\mathfrak{H}}
.

Proof.

The first claim follows from the following reasoning. Given a pushout diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

with the map to
X\to Z
called
C
and both
a
and
b
being cofibrations, if we have chosen homotopy equivalences
h_{1}:Y_{1}/X\to Cone(a)
and
h_{2}:Y_{2}/X\to Cone(b)
then the wedge of these defines an equivalence
h_{3}:Y_{1}/X\wedge Y_{2}/X\to Cone(c)
. Moreover, using this definition, the map give by the compositio of
h_{3}
with the Puppe map is manifestly the wedge products associated to the compositions of the maps associated to
h_{i}
and the corresponding Puppe maps for
i=1,2
.

The next claim goes as follow. If we consider
\{\mathcal{C}_{123}\}_{\mathfrak{H}}/\Sigma^{\mathbb{R}\oplus V^{1}_{2,\tilde{s}}}\{\mathcal{C}_{2}\}_{\mathfrak{H_{2}}}
then this is homeomorphic to
\Sigma^{\mathbb{R}\oplus V^{1}_{13,s}}\{\mathcal{C}_{13}\}_{\mathfrak{H}_{13}}
; by the fact that every triple of maps in a long exact puppe sequence is homotopy equivalent to
X\xrightarrow{f}Y\to C(f)
, we have that
\Sigma^{\mathbb{R}\oplus V^{1}_{13,s}}\{\mathcal{C}_{13}\}_{\mathfrak{H}_{13}}
is homotopy equivalent to
\Sigma^{V^{1}_{13,s}}f_{13}
. This statement together with the corresponding statemtn with
2
and
3
exchanged implies the claim.

The last claim goes as follows: if we extend the earlier diagram as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

where the map
Z\to W
is a cofibration, and we call the map
X\to W
as
d
, then there is an inclusion
Cone(c)\to Cone(d)
, and we can choose the equivalence
W/X\to Cone(d)
to restrict to the previously chosen equivalence
Z/X=Y_{1}/X\wedge Y_{2}/X\to Cone(c)
.
∎

Proposition 36
.

Using the notation of Proposition
35
, let

f_{ab}:\Sigma^{\mathbb{R}\oplus V^{1}_{a,s_{ab}}}\{\mathcal{C}_{a}\}_{\mathfrak{H}_{a}}\to\Sigma^{\mathbb{R}\oplus V^{1}_{b,s_{ab}}}\{\mathcal{C}_{b}\}_{\mathfrak{H}_{b}}
be the maps associated by Proposition
34
to the homotopy definition data
\mathfrak{H}_{ab}
.
Then we have a homotopy

\Sigma^{V^{1}_{24},s}f_{24}\Sigma^{V^{1}_{12},s}f_{12}\underline{\vee}(-\Sigma^{V^{1}_{34},s}f_{34})\Sigma^{V^{1}_{13},s}f_{13}.

(204)

With notation as in Proposition
34
, we have equality in the stable homotopy category

[f_{24}][f_{12}]=-[f_{34}][f_{13}].

(205)

Proof.

First, we will explain why the domains and codomains of the maps in (
204
) match appropriately so that this equation make sense. Indeed, the map
\Sigma^{V^{1}_{ab},s}f_{ab}
defines a map

\Sigma^{\mathbb{R}\oplus V^{1}_{a,\hat{s}}}\{C_{a}\}_{\mathfrak{H}_{a}}\simeq\Sigma^{V^{1}_{ab,s}}\Sigma^{\mathbb{R}\oplus V^{1}_{a,s_{ab}}}\{C_{a}\}_{\mathfrak{H}_{a}}\xrightarrow{\Sigma^{V^{1}_{ab},s}f_{ab}}\Sigma^{V^{1}_{ab,s}}\Sigma^{\mathbb{R}\oplus V^{1}_{b,s_{ab}}}\{C_{b}\}_{\mathfrak{H}_{b}}\simeq\Sigma^{\mathbb{R}\oplus V^{1}_{b,\hat{s}}}\{C_{b}\}_{\mathfrak{H}_{b}},

(206)

using the identities
V^{1}_{a,s_{ab}}\oplus V^{1}_{ab,s}=V^{1}_{a,\hat{s}}
; we use these isomorphisms to make sense of the compositions in (
204
).

Then, we will explain how (
204
) implies (
205
). Indeed, the identity (
205
) holds if the identity

\Delta[f_{24}]\Delta[f_{12}]=\Delta[f_{34}]\Delta[f_{13}]

(207)

holds, where
\Delta
denotes the functor homotopy category
X\to F_{\mathbb{R}\oplus V^{1}_{h}}\Sigma^{\mathbb{R}\oplus V^{1}_{h}}X
on the stable homotopy category. This latter identity holds in turn of we an find representing maps for each of the
\Delta f_{ab}
in the category of orthogonal spectra and a homotopy between the composed maps. Now, we can apply the functor
F_{\mathbb{R}\oplus V^{1}_{h}}
to the maps in (
206
) and apply the isomorphism

F_{\mathbb{R}\oplus V^{1}_{h}}\Sigma^{\mathbb{R}\oplus V^{1}_{a,\hat{s}}}\{C_{a}\}_{\mathfrak{H}_{a}}\simeq F_{\mathbb{R}\oplus V^{1}_{a,\hat{s}}}\Sigma^{\mathbb{R}\oplus V^{1}_{a,\hat{s}}}|\mathcal{C}(H_{a},J_{a})|_{\mathfrak{H}_{a}}

to interpret these maps potentially being representing maps for the
\Delta[f_{ab}]
; given this claim, the homotopy (
204
) establishes (
207
) and thus (
205
). To see this latter claim about representing maps, we simply note that applying
F_{V^{1}_{ab,s}}\Sigma^{V^{1}_{ab,s}}
to the map
\bar{f}_{ab}
of (
200
) and applying isomorphisms in (
206
) to the maps inside the desuspension functors, and the isomorphisms
V^{1}_{ab,s}\oplus V^{1}_{ab}=V^{1}_{h}
gives exactly
F_{\mathbb{R}\oplus V^{1}_{h}}
applied to the maps in (
206
). This proves the claim about representing maps.

It remains to justify (
204
). But this follows from Lemma
58
. Indeed, given maps
X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xrightarrow{g}Z
, an extension of
g
to a map
Cone(f)\to Z
specifies a homotopy of
g\circ f\sim*
. This lemma states that we are precisely in this position with
f=\Sigma^{V^{1}_{12,s}}f_{12}\underline{\vee}\Sigma^{V^{1}_{13,s}}f_{13}
and
g=\Sigma^{V^{1}_{24,s}}f_{24}\wedge\Sigma^{V^{1}_{34,s}}f_{34}
. So we have a homotopy
\Sigma^{V^{1}_{24},s}f_{24}\Sigma^{V^{1}_{12},s}f_{12}\underline{\vee}(-\Sigma^{V^{1}_{34},s}f_{34})\Sigma^{V^{1}_{13},s}f_{13}\sim*
, as desired.
∎

10.2
Comparison with Floer Homology

Lemma 59
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a virtually smooth semi-freely framed proper flow category together with an embedding
\mathbf{E}
and its extension
\bar{\mathbf{E}}
. If
\mathcal{C}_{2}\subset\mathcal{C}
is a downwards-closed subposet of
\mathcal{C}
that is sent to itself by the
G
-action, then, letting
\mathcal{C}_{2}
also denote the full virtually smooth flow subcategory on the objects of
\mathcal{C}_{2}
equipped with the restrictions of
\mathbf{E}
and
\bar{\mathbf{E}}
, we have a q-cofibration

\Sigma^{V_{2}}\{\mathcal{C}_{2}\}\subset\{\mathcal{C}\}

(208)

where
V_{2}
is the perpendicular to the image of the shift space associated to
\mathcal{C}_{2}
in the shift space associated to
\mathcal{C}
. Similarly, we have a level
q
-cofibration
[
73
, Theorem 2.4]

F_{V_{2}}\Sigma^{V_{2}}|\mathcal{C}_{2}|\subset|\mathcal{C}|.

(209)

Moreover, we have that there is an (equivariant) homeomorphism

\{\mathcal{C}\}/\Sigma^{V_{2}}\{\mathcal{C}_{2}\}\simeq\{\mathcal{C}_{1}\}

(210)

where
\mathcal{C}_{1}
is the full flow subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
on the objects
Ob(\mathcal{C}_{2})\setminus Ob(\mathcal{C}_{2})
, and is also equipped with the restricted embedded framings, embeddings, and extensions of embeddings.

Thus, in particular, given
a grading
\alpha:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbb{Z}
on the poset
\mathcal{C}
, i.e. an integer-value function on the objects of
\mathcal{C}
such that
\alpha(x)\geq\alpha(y)
if
x>y
and
\alpha(gx)=\alpha(x)
for all
x\in\mathcal{C}
and all
g\in G
, then there is a filtration by sub-spectra, i.e. there is are diagrams of
G-CW
-subspectra

\{\mathcal{C}\}_{\leq k-1}\subset\{\mathcal{C}\}_{\leq k}\subset\{\mathcal{C}\}_{\leq k+1}\subset\ldots,

(211)

and a diagram of orthogonal
G
-spectra

\cdots\to|\mathcal{C}|_{\leq k-1}\to|\mathcal{C}|_{\leq k}\to|\mathcal{C}|_{\leq k+1}\to\cdots

(212)

where each map is a level
q
-cofibration, and

\{\mathcal{C}\}=\cup_{k}\{\mathcal{C}\}_{\leq k},|\mathcal{C}|=\colim_{k}|\mathcal{C}|_{\leq k}.

Proof.

The inclusion (
208
) follows from the construction, since, writing
V^{1}_{\mathcal{C}}
for the shift space associated to
\mathcal{C}
, we have that
V^{1}_{\mathcal{C}_{2}}(x)^{\perp}\oplus V_{2}=V^{1}_{\mathcal{C}}(x)^{\perp}
; thus the attaching maps (
36
) for the the cells of
\{\mathcal{C}\}
coming from objects of
\mathcal{C}_{2}
are therefore just suspensions of the corresponding attaching maps in
\{\mathcal{C}_{2}\}
. The inclusion (
209
) then follows immediately from the fact that
V_{2}\oplus V^{1}_{\mathcal{C}_{2}}=V^{1}_{\mathcal{C}}
. The claim (
210
) follows from the fact that when defining
\{\mathcal{C}_{1}\}
one collapses all parts of the boundaries of the cells associated to objects of
\mathcal{C}_{1}
that would have mapped to the cells associated to objects of
\mathcal{C}_{2}
to basepoint instead (see Condition F of Definition
32
for extensions of embeddings). The remaining claims follow immediately from the definitions.
∎

Lemma 60
.

In the setting of Lemma
59
, there is a map defined up to contractible choice

f:\{\mathcal{C}_{1}\}\to\Sigma^{\mathbb{R}\oplus V_{2}}\{\mathcal{C}_{2}\}

as well as

\bar{f}=F_{V}f:|\mathcal{C}_{1}|\to F_{V_{2}}\Sigma^{\mathbb{R}\oplus V_{2}}|\mathcal{C}_{2}|.

Proof.

This follows from (
209
), (
210
) by composing the last map in the Puppe sequence

\Sigma^{V_{2}}\{\mathcal{C}_{2}\}\to\{\mathcal{C}\}\to Cone(\Sigma^{V_{2}}\{\mathcal{C}_{2}\}\to\{\mathcal{C}\})\to\Sigma^{\mathbb{R}V_{2}}\{\mathcal{C}_{2}\}

with an equivariant homotopy inverse of the equivalence (
210
).
∎

Lemma 61
.

Let
\mathcal{C}_{2}
be a downwards-closed flow-subcategory of a virtually smooth flow category
\mathcal{C}
with an
E^{s}_{2}
framing and an extension of an embedding; equip
\mathcal{C}_{2}
with the restrictions of all of this data. Let
\mathcal{C}_{1}
be the full subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
on
Ob(\mathcal{C})\setminus Ob(\mathcal{C}_{2})
, and equip it with the restrictions of all the data associated to
\mathcal{C}
. Suppose that we are given gradings
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
of the posets
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}
, and
\mathcal{C}

respects
these gradings in the sense that if
x_{i}\in\mathcal{C}_{i}
for
i=1,2
and
x_{i}>x_{2}
in
\mathcal{C}
then
\alpha_{1}(x_{1})\geq\alpha_{2}(x_{2})
. Then the map

f:\{\mathcal{C}_{1}\}\to\Sigma^{\mathbb{R}\oplus V_{2}}\{\mathcal{C}_{2}\}

defined by Lemma
60

where
V_{2}
as in that lemma, respects the filtrations on the domain and codomain induced by suspending the filtrations of Equation
211
, Lemma
59
. Moreover, the space of choices of such
f
which respect these filtrations is contractible.

Proof.

Define
\alpha:\mathcal{C}\to\mathbb{Z}
by
\alpha(x)=\alpha_{1}(x)
if
x\in\mathcal{C}_{2}
, and otherwise
\alpha(x)=\alpha_{2}(x)
if
x\in\mathcal{C}_{2}
.
Then we have a filtration of
\{\mathcal{C}\}
(
211
) associated to
\alpha
, and the restriction of this filtration to
\Sigma^{V_{2}}\{\mathcal{C}_{2}\}
is the suspension of the filtration by
\alpha_{2}
of
\{\mathcal{C}_{2}\}
. This defines a filtration by
\mathbb{Z}
of
Z=Cone(\Sigma^{V_{2}}\{\mathcal{C}_{2}\}\to\{\mathcal{C}\})
and the projection (
210
) sends this filtration onto the filtration of
\{\mathcal{C}_{1}\}
associated to
\alpha_{1}
. Thus the inverse map associated to (
210
) can be resepects these filtrations as well, and it is an inverse to an explicit homeomorphism so there are no choices to make. One then sees that the Puppe map
Z\to\Sigma^{\mathbb{R}\oplus V_{2}}\{\mathcal{C}_{2}\}
respects filtrations as well (with the latter filtered by the suspension of the filtration associated to
\alpha_{2}
). The space of choices of a Puppe map is contractible, thus proving the lemma,
∎

Recall that the homology groups of a cofibrant spectrum
X
as
H_{*}(X,\mathbb{Z})=\pi_{*}(X\wedge H\mathbb{Z})
, where
H\mathbb{Z}
is the Eilenberg-MacLane spectrum. Moreover, if
X
is a colimit over
k
of q-cofibrant spectra
X_{\leq k}
along q-cofibrations, then there is a spectral sequence

E^{1}_{s,t}=H_{s+t}(Cone(X_{\leq s-1}\to X_{\leq s}),\mathbb{Z})\Rightarrow H_{*}(X,\mathbb{Z}),

(213)

which converges if the colimit is finite.

An elementary computation shows that, using the notation of Lemma
59
if
X=|\mathcal{C}|
,
X_{\leq k}=|\mathcal{C}|_{\leq k}
as in (

), and
\alpha
is
stict
(i.e. if

), then there is a level equivalence

Cone(X_{\leq s-1}\to X_{\leq s})\simeq\bigvee_{x\in\mathcal{C}:\alpha(x)=s}\mathbb{S}^{\mu}(x)

(214)

where

is the grading on
\mathcal{C}
. Indeed, we have that
X_{\leq s}=F_{V^{1}_{max}}\{\mathcal{C}\}_{\leq s}
, and cone on the inclusion

\{\mathcal{C}\}_{\leq s-1}\to\{\mathcal{C}\}_{\leq s}

is

\bigvee_{x\in\mathcal{C}:\alpha(x)=s}\mathbb{S}^{\mathbb{R}^{\bar{A}(x)+1}\oplus V^{0}(x)\oplus V^{1}(x)^{\perp}};

by the grading condition (
27
) we have that

\mu(x)=\dim V^{0}(x)-\dim V^{1}(x)+(\bar{A}(x)+1)=\bar{A}(x)+1+\dim V^{0}(x)+\dim V^{1}(x)^{\perp}-\dim V^{1}_{max}

which proves the equivalence (

).

Now, given an orientation of

,
V^{1}(x)
, and

, we get an isomorphism of

in the relevant degree.

Thus, after choosing

and

, the

(215)

If we are in the situation of Lemma

, the map
f
induces a map
\bar{f}=F_{V^{1}_{max}}f:|\mathcal{C}_{1}|\to F_{V_{2}}\Sigma^{\mathbb{R}\oplus V_{2}}|\mathcal{C}_{2}|
, which in turn induces a map of spectral sequences

\bar{f}^{1}_{s,t}:(E^{1}_{\mathcal{C}_{1}})_{s,t}\to(E^{1}_{\mathcal{C}_{2}})_{s,t+1}.

(We will find that this shift by

A choice of orientation data in turn gives orientations of

for

(by requiring that the map

Lemma 62
.

Suppose that for all
s
and all

and

with

, the map

associated to the embedding of

has isolated zeros in its interior. Then
the map

sends the fundamental class of

to a sum of fundamental classes of

for

such that

and

, with the coefficient of the fundamental class of

being exactly the signed number of zeros of

on the interior, with the signs defined via the standard orientation of
\mathbb{R}^{\bar{A}(x,y)}
and the orientations of
V^{i}(x,y)
assigned above.
\blacksquare

Now, let us suppose that we are in a geometric setting, i.e.
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}(H,J)
for some convex nondegenerate Floer data
(H,J)
, or
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}(H_{s},J_{s})
for convex nondegenerate continuation data

. Then, the orientation data

actually trivializes the

associated to the objects of

(
[

,

,

]
); in other words, it defines orientation data in the sense of Section

), which lets us associate signs
s_{u},s_{u^{\prime}}\in\pm
to regular Floer trajectories
u\in\mathcal{C}(H,J)(x,y)
or regular continuation map trajectories

via usual coherent orientations procedure
[
42
]
outline in Section

. Indeed, the orientation lines

of that section are canonically isomorphic to the determinant lines of the operators

(

); since

(

) is a stabilization of

by
