, one has that

o(x)\simeq\det D_{x}\simeq\det D_{x}^{\mathfrak{P}}\otimes\det V^{1}(x)=\det V^{0}(x)\otimes\det V^{1}(x).

We are now able to explain how to compare usual computations in Floer homology with the maps on homology of Floer homotopy defined above:

Theorem 15
.

Suppose that the admissible Floer data
(H,J)
are regular. In the setting of Lemma
59
, let us choose
\alpha
to be the grading
\mu
by the Conley-Zehnder index
\tilde{CZ}
(see Section
6.1
). Then we can choose homotopy definition data
\mathfrak{H}
and orientation data
\mathfrak{o}
for
\mathcal{C}(H,J)
such that the differential on the first page of the first pace of the spectral sequence (
215
) agrees with the Floer differential.

Similarly, suppose that we have a continuation map category
\mathcal{C}(H_{s},J_{s})
such that for some strict filtrations
\alpha_{i}
of
\mathcal{C}(H_{i},J_{i})
(with
(H_{-},J_{-})=(H_{1},J_{1})
and
(H_{+},J_{+})=(H_{2},J_{2})
) with
\mathcal{C}(H_{s},J_{s})
respecting this strict filtration, we have that for every
x\in\mathcal{C}(H_{1},J_{1})
and
y\in\mathcal{C}(H_{2},J_{2})
with
\alpha_{1}(x)=\alpha_{2}(y)
and
\mu^{CZ}(x)=\mu^{CZ}(y)
, every unbroken continuation map trajectory
u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(H_{s},J_{s})(x,y)
is regular. Then the coefficient of the fundamental class of
\mathbb{S}^{\mu(y)+1}
in
\bar{f}^{1}([\mathbb{S}^{\mu(x)}])
is exactly
\sum_{u^{\prime}}s_{u^{\prime}}
where the sum runs over these trajectories (up to a global sign).

Proof.

We will prove the statement about the Floer differential; the proof of the second statement is similar. Write
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(x,y)=(T(x,y),V(x,y),\sigma(x,y))
. There is a map
\phi:\mathcal{C}(H,J)(x,y)\to T(x,y)
which is a homeomorphism onto the zero-set of
\sigma(x,y)
, and thus a map
\mathcal{C}(x,y)\to{\overline{M}}(x,y)
,
u\mapsto a(\phi(u))
. In particular, regular index
1
Floer trajectories
u
are mapped by
\phi
to isolated zeros of
\sigma(x,y)
, and to the top open stratum of
{\overline{M}}(x,y)
. We will show the claim in the theorem under the condition that the perturbation map
\lambda_{V^{1}(x,y)}
(
85
) is identically zero in an open neighborhood of
a(\phi(u))
, for every (regular) index
1
Floer trajectory
u
; this can be achieved in the proofs of Propositions
7
(and
19
in the continuation map case). The choice of orientation data means that there is an integer assigned to the zero of
e_{x,y}
corresponding to
u
; this is the same integer as one assigned to the zero
\phi(u)
of
\sigma(x,y)
, when we give
V(x,y)
and
TT(x,y)
the orientations coming from the framings of these spaces and the orientation data. We will first argue that this integer
s^{\prime}_{u}
is
\pm 1
; we will then argue that in fact
s^{\prime}_{u}
agrees with
s_{u}
, the sign assigned to
u
by Floer homology.

Let us argue the first point. By Equation
155
and the condition on the triviality of
\lambda_{V^{1}(x,y)}
near
u
we have an isomorphism

\begin{gathered}TT(x,y)_{\phi(u)}=T(x,y)_{a(\phi(u))}\oplus V^{1}(x,y)_{u}\oplus\ker D_{u}=\mathbb{R}^{\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}(x,y)}\oplus V^{1}(x,y)\oplus\mathbb{R}\end{gathered}

(217)

where
D_{u}
is the linearization of the
unperturbed
Floer equation. Now one can produce a convenient chart around
\phi(u)=(\tilde{u},a(\phi(u)),0)
with domain
V^{1}(x,y)\oplus\mathbb{R}^{\bar{A}(x,y)+1}
by: translating the map
\tilde{u}
in the
s
direction, moving the
\bar{A}(x,y)+1
marked points on its domain (up to global simultaneous translation), or deforming
0\in V^{1}(x,y)
to a nonzero element. In this chart, using the definition of
\sigma(x,y)
together with the description

(V(x,y)^{1}_{T})_{u}=V^{1}(x,y)\oplus V^{1}_{can}(x,y)

(218)

the
V^{1}(x,y)
component of
\sigma(x,y)
is simply the composition of the projection to
V^{1}(x,y)
with the identity map
V^{1}(x,y)\to V^{1}(x,y)
, and the second component of
\sigma(x,y)
is the projection to the
\mathbb{R}^{\bar{A}(x,y)+1}
component of the chart together with a map
\mathbb{R}^{\bar{A}(x,y)+1}\to\mathbb{R}^{\bar{A}(x,y)+1}
determined by
\delta A
. This latter map is always manifestly orientation-preserving; thus,
we have that
s^{\prime}_{u}=\pm 1
, with the sign given by the difference in orientations of
V^{1}(x,y)
coming from the framing of the obstruction bundles, and of
V^{1}(x,y)
induced from the inclusion
V^{1}(x,y)\oplus\mathbb{R}\simeq\ker D_{u}^{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}
.

We will now compare this sign with
s_{u}
. To do that we must consider how the framings we produce orient
T(x,y)_{u}
and
V(x,y)_{u}
. The latter are produced from the orientation data and the construction of Section
8.6
, for which
V^{1}_{can}(x,y)
and the component
\mathbb{R}^{\bar{A}(x,y)}
(this is all but the last two terms of (
217
)) are framed in a canonical way while the terms
\ker D_{u}^{\mathfrak{P}}=V^{1}(x,y)_{u}\oplus\ker D_{u}
and
V^{1}(x,y)_{u}
get induced orientations (from the framing) via the procedure described in that section, starting from the orientations of of the
\ker D_{x}^{\mathfrak{P}}
and
V^{1}(x)
– i.e., the orientation data. On the other hand, in Floer homology, one has that the orientation line

o(x)\simeq\det\ker D_{x}^{\mathfrak{P}}\otimes\det V^{1}(x)

receives an orientation from the orientation data, and the sign
s_{xy}
is the comparison sign from comparing the orientation of
o(x)
produced from orientation data from the orientation of
o(x)
produced from the canonical orientation of the determinant line
\ker D_{u}
of
u
and the orientation of
o(y)
via the linear gluing map
\ker D_{u}\otimes o(y)\to o(x)
. But we have that the framings
a_{xy}
of Proposition
are chosen precisely such they induce the orientations on
\ker D_{u}^{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}
induced via the stabilized gluing maps
D^{\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}}_{V^{1}(x),V^{1}(y)}
of (
166
); this shows that the Floer comparison sign and the sign
s^{\prime}_{u}
agree. The proof for continuation maps maps is similar.
∎

Proposition 37
.

Let
(H,J)
be admissible regular Floer data. Then there exists homotopy definition data
\mathfrak{H}
such that if we define the Floer homotopy type
|\mathcal{C}(H,J)|
using
\mathfrak{H}
we have an isomorphism

\pi_{*}(|\mathcal{C}(H,J)|\wedge H\mathbb{Z})\simeq HF_{*}(H,J).

In fact, recall that the Eilenberg-MacLane spectrum
H\mathbb{Z}
has a single nonzero homotopy group, namely
\pi_{0}(H\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}
; thus given the chain complex
CF_{*}(H,\mathbb{Z})
and choosing orientation data
\mathfrak{o}
so that we get isomorphisms
CF_{k}(H,\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}^{a_{k}}
, there is an associated
H\mathbb{Z}
-module spectrum which we denote
|CF_{*}(H,J)|
which is built by attaching wedges of suspensions of
H\mathbb{Z}
via the maps given by the coefficients of the differential, and one has a weak equivalence

|\mathcal{C}(H,J)|\wedge H\mathbb{Z}\simeq|CF_{*}(H,J)|.

Proof.

Choosing
\alpha
to be the Conley-Zehnder index, this follows by induction from (
214
) and (
15
).
∎

10.3
Choosing data compatibly with the cyclotomic action

We will show how to imitate the colimit (
190
) in the setting of Floer homotopy types in the setting where none of the Floer data or continuation data are required to be regular. First, fix a
complete
S^{1}
-universe

\mathfrak{U}
; by restriction of representations this universe is also a complete
C_{k}
-universe for all
k
. Assume that the
S^{1}
-equivariant polarization class
\rho\in KO^{1}_{S^{1}}(LM)
vanishes, and inductively choose a sequence of cyclotomically compatible trivializations of the polarization map for all
(H^{\#p^{k}},J^{\#p^{k}})
(see Lemma
39
) once and for all.

By repeatedly applying Proposition
33
, we produce a sequence of homotopy definition data
\mathfrak{H}_{k}^{cont}
for
\mathcal{C}(H_{s}^{\#p^{k}},J_{s}^{\#p^{k}})
which have the following property. Write

\mathfrak{H}^{+}_{k}=\mathfrak{H}_{k}^{cont}|_{\mathcal{C}(H^{\#p^{k}},J^{\#p^{k}})},

\mathfrak{H}^{-}_{k}=\mathfrak{H}_{k}^{cont}|_{\mathcal{C}(H^{\#p^{k+1}},J^{\#p^{k+1}})},

\mathfrak{H}_{k}=\mathfrak{H}^{-}_{k-1}\text{ for }k\geq 1,\mathfrak{H}_{0}=\mathfrak{H}^{+}_{0}.

Proposition
33
allows us to require that for
k\geq 1
,
\mathfrak{H}_{k}^{+}
is a
C_{k}
-equivariant stabilization of
\mathfrak{H}_{k-1}^{-}
(where
\mathfrak{H}_{k-1}
was chosen such that
\mathfrak{H}_{k-1}^{-}
has a
C_{k}
-equivariant structure). Write

\tilde{V}(\mathfrak{H}^{cont}_{k})=\tilde{V}(\mathfrak{H}_{k}^{-})=\tilde{V}(\mathfrak{H}_{k-1}^{-})\oplus\tilde{V}(\mathfrak{H}_{k-1}^{-},s)

(219)

for the corresponding
C_{k}
-equivariant decomposition of shift spaces.

Using (
197
) and (
198
), the map associated to a Puppe sequence defines, for every
k\geq 0
, a
C_{p^{k}}
-equivariant map

g_{1}^{k}:\Sigma^{\mathbb{R}}\{\mathcal{C}(H^{\#p^{k+1}},J^{\#p^{k+1}})\}_{\mathfrak{H}^{-}_{k}}\to\Sigma\{\mathcal{C}(H^{\#p^{k}},J^{\#p^{k}})\}_{\mathfrak{H}^{+}_{k}}\simeq\Sigma^{\mathbb{R}\oplus\tilde{V}(\mathfrak{H}_{k-1}^{-},s)}\{\mathcal{C}(H^{\#p^{k}},J^{\#p^{k}})\}_{\mathfrak{H}_{k-1}}.

(220)

where the second equivalence only makes sense for
k\geq 1
.

Using (
179
), the decompositions (
219
) define associated maps

g_{2}^{k}:F_{\mathbb{R}\oplus\tilde{V}(\mathfrak{H}^{-}_{k})}\Sigma^{\mathbb{R}\oplus\tilde{V}(\mathfrak{H}_{k-1}^{-},s)}\{\mathcal{C}(H^{\#p^{k}},J^{\#p^{k}})\}_{\mathfrak{H}^{-}_{k-1}}.\to F_{\mathbb{R}\oplus\tilde{V}(\mathfrak{H}_{k}-1)}\Sigma^{\mathbb{R}}\{\mathcal{C}(H^{\#p^{k}},J^{\#p^{k}})\}_{\mathfrak{H}^{-}_{k-1}}.

(221)

Define

|\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H,J)|^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{H}}=F_{\mathbb{R}\oplus\tilde{V}^{1}_{\mathfrak{H}}}\Sigma^{\mathbb{R}}\{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H,J)\}

The composition of (
220
) and (
221
) is a map

g^{k}=g_{2}^{k}\circ(F_{\mathbb{R}\oplus\tilde{V}(\mathfrak{H}^{-}_{k})}g_{1}^{k}):|\mathcal{C}(H^{\#p^{k}},J^{\#p^{k}})|^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{H}_{k}}\to|\mathcal{C}(H^{\#p^{k-1}},J^{\#p^{k-1}})|^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{H}_{k}};

(222)

this map makes sense even for
k=1
by simply avoiding (
221
).

Dualizing, we get
C_{k-1}
-equivariant maps

f_{k}:F(|\mathcal{C}(H^{\#p^{k-1}},J^{\#p^{k-1}})|^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{H}_{k}},\mathbb{S})\to F(|\mathcal{C}(H^{\#p^{k}},J^{\#p^{k}})|^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{H}_{k}},\mathbb{S}).

(223)

for all
k\geq 1
.

Now, we have one more geometric proposition:

Proposition 38
.

The homotopy continuation data
\mathfrak{H}_{k}
can be chosen such that

\{\mathcal{C}(H_{s}^{\#p^{k}},J_{s}^{\#p^{k}})\}^{C_{p}}\simeq\Sigma^{\tilde{V}(\mathfrak{H}^{-}_{k-1},s)^{C_{p}}}\{\mathcal{C}(H_{s}^{\#p^{k-1}},J_{s}^{\#p^{k-1}})\}.

Using this proposition, we can choose the maps
g_{1}^{k}
(which come from Puppe sequences) such that

(g_{1}^{k})^{C_{p}}=\Sigma^{\tilde{V}(\mathfrak{H}^{-}_{k-1},s)^{C_{p}}}g_{1}^{k-1}.

Writing

|H^{k}|:=|\mathcal{C}(H^{\#p^{k-1}},J^{\#p^{k-1}})|^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{H}_{k}},

and

\mathbb{S}|H^{k}|:=F(|\mathcal{C}(H^{\#p^{k-1}},J^{\#p^{k-1}})|^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{H}_{k}},\mathbb{S})

for short, we have a diagram of maps

\mathbb{S}|H^{\#p^{0}}|\xrightarrow{t_{0}}\mathbb{S}|H^{\#p^{1}}|\xrightarrow{t_{1}}\mathbb{S}|H^{\#p^{2}}|\xrightarrow{t_{2}}\cdots.

(224)

Here the map
t_{k}
is
C_{p^{k}}
-equivariant.

Proof of Proposition
38
.

We simply make sure that the integralization data for
\mathcal{C}(H_{s},J_{s})
is chosen such that it satisfies the conditions of Lemma
33
; this is always possible by choosing
c(s)
to decrease a sufficient amount. This statement is proven by induction on
k
with the base case at
k=0
being Proposition
33
. Given the case for
k-1
, the case for
k
follows from
follows from Proposition
21
, Lemma
36
, Lemma
47
, Proposition
5
(after first using Lemma
20
and Lemma
17
to induce an embedding and the extension of the embedding for
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H_{s}^{\#p^{k}},J_{s}^{\#p^{k}})^{C_{p}}
from that chosen for
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H_{s}^{\#p^{k-1}},J_{s}^{\#p^{k-1}})
), and subsequently using, Theorem
8
, Lemma
18
, and Proposition
6
. One simply notes that now the perturbation data satisfy the hypotheses of Lemma
33
for
\mathcal{C}(H_{s}^{p^{k}},J_{s}^{\#p^{k}})
, and one proceeds onwards with the induction.
∎

10.4
Invariance of equivariant Floer homotopy

Proposition 39
.

Suppose that
H_{\pm}
are parts of admissible Floer data
(H_{\pm},J_{\pm})
, with
H_{\pm}
having the same slope at infinity if
M
has contact boundary. Then, choosing an admissible continuation datum
(H_{s},J_{s})
between
(H_{\pm},J_{\pm})
(assuming the relevant condition on polarization classes) choosing homotopy definition data
\mathfrak{H}_{s}
as in Proposition
33
, the map
f
defined by Proposition
34
on Floer homotopy types is an equivalence of (nonequivariant) spectra.

Proof.

Choose a continuation homotopy datum
(H_{h},J_{h})
such that
(H_{1},J_{1})=(H_{3},J_{3})=(H_{4},J_{4})=(H_{-},J_{-}),(H_{2},J_{2})=(H_{+},J_{+})
, and
(H_{13},J_{13})
and
(H_{34},J_{34})
are
s
-independent. By Proposition
35
we can find a homotopy definition datum
\mathfrak{H}_{h}
for
\mathcal{C}(H_{h},J_{h})
such that

•

The associated homotopy definition datum
\mathfrak{H}_{h,12}
for
\mathcal{C}(H_{12},J_{12})
is a stabilizations of
\mathfrak{H}_{s}
,

•

For every
x\in Fix(H_{-})
, we get three distinct objects
(x_{1},x_{3},x_{4})\in\mathcal{C}(H_{h},J_{h})
with
x_{i}\in\mathcal{C}(H_{i},J_{i})
corresponding to the Hamiltonian trajectory
x
of
H_{i}=H_{-}
. Because the actions of
x_{i}
are all the same, both
\mathcal{C}(H_{13},J_{13})(x_{1},x_{3})
and
\mathcal{C}(H_{34},J_{34})(x_{3},x_{4})
are each a single point corresponding to the (regular) constant continuation map trajectory at
x
. We can thus arrange for the perturbation function
\lambda_{V^{1}(x_{i},x_{j})}
for the perturbation data
\mathfrak{P}_{h}
underlying
\mathfrak{H}_{h}
associated to
(x_{1},x_{3})
and to
(x_{3},x_{4})
to be zero; this is possible because in the proof of Proposition
35
(or more saliently in the proof of Proposition
22
) there are no constraints arising from boundary strata to
\mathcal{C}(x_{3},x_{4})
or
\mathcal{C}(x_{1},x_{3})
in the inductive procedure for choosing perturbation data.

•

The homotopy definition data
\mathfrak{H}_{i}
associated to
\mathcal{C}(H_{i},J_{i})
for
i=1,3,4
are all stabilization of the same homotopy definition datum
\mathfrak{H}_{-}
.

Choose a strict grading
\alpha_{-}
on
\mathcal{C}(H_{-},J_{-})
, i.e. a map
\alpha_{-}:\mathcal{C}(H_{-},J_{-})\to\mathbb{Z}
such that if
x>y
then
\alpha_{-}(x)>\alpha_{-}(y)
. By Lemma
59
and Equation
214
this defines filtrations of

|\mathcal{C}(H_{i},J_{i})_{\mathfrak{H},x}|
. An inductive argument shows that the posets
\mathcal{C}(H_{13},J_{13})\simeq\mathcal{C}(H_{34},J_{34})\simeq\mathcal{C}(H_{s},J_{s})
respect these filtrations in the sense of Lemma
61
.
Write

f_{ij}:F_{\mathbb{R}\oplus V^{1}_{i,s_{ij}}}\Sigma^{\mathbb{R}\oplus V^{1}_{i,s_{ij}}}|\mathcal{C}(H_{i},J_{i})_{\mathfrak{H}_{i}}|\to F_{\mathbb{R}\oplus V^{1}_{j,s_{ij}}}\Sigma^{\mathbb{R}\oplus V^{1}_{j,s_{ij}}}|\mathcal{C}(H_{j},J_{j})_{\mathfrak{H}_{j}}|

for a choice of such maps associated to
\mathfrak{H}_{ij}
by Lemma
34
; by Lemma
61
these can be chosen to preserve the filtrations associated to the gradings. In particular, we see that for both
f_{13}
and
f_{34}
, the maps (
216
) induced in the spectral sequences (
213
) are isomorphisms, since these satisfy the condition Theorem
15
and the only trajectories contributing to the maps on spectral sequences are the constant continuation map trajectories. Thus the maps induced by
-F_{V^{1}_{34,s}}\Sigma^{V^{1}_{34,s}}f_{34}F_{V^{1}_{12,s}}\Sigma^{V^{1}_{12,s}}f_{13}
(see Proposition
36
) are isomorphisms on integral homology; by Proposition
36
, that implies that the map induced by
F_{V^{1}_{24,s}}\Sigma^{V^{1}_{24,s}}f_{24}F_{V^{1}_{12,s}}\Sigma^{V^{1}_{12,s}}{f_{12}}
is also an isomorphism on integral homology. So
f_{12}
induces an isomorphism on integral homology, and thus a weak equivalence of spectra.

A similar argument but now with a choice of continuation homotopy datum
H_{h}
and homotopy definition datum
\mathfrak{H}_{h}
such that
(H_{1},J_{1})=(H_{3},J_{3})=(H_{4},J_{4})=(H_{+},J_{+})
, and
(H_{2},J_{2})=(H_{-},J_{-})
, with
\mathfrak{H}_{34}
a stabilization of
\mathfrak{H}_{s}
, shows that the map
f
is a surjection on integral homology. So
f
is an isomorphism on integral homology; but a map of finite spectra which is an isomorphism on integral homology is a weak equivalence.
∎

Proposition 40
.

Let
p
be a prime.
Suppose that we are in the setting of Proposition
39
, but now all data chosen are
C_{p^{k}}
-equivariant (and the corresponding assumption on polarization classses holds). Then the map of
C_{p^{k}}
-spectra
f
defined by Proposition
34
on Floer homotopy types is an equivalence of
C_{p^{k}}
-spectra.

Proof.

Write
(H_{0,\pm},J_{0,\pm},H_{s,0},J_{s,0})
for the data which upon
p^{k}
-iteration give
(H_{\pm},J_{\pm},H_{s},J_{s})
, respectively. We have that
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H_{s},J_{s})^{C_{p^{r}}}
for
0<r\leq k
is exactly
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H_{s,0}^{\#p^{k-r}},J_{s,0}^{\#p^{k-r}})
, where the latter is obtained by taking the
C_{p^{r}}
invariant parts of the integralization data perturbation data in the natural fashion, with framing as in Lemma
3
; the latter category is equipped with embeddings and extensions of embeddings by taking
C_{p^{r}}
-invariants. Similar results hold for
(H_{s},J_{s},H_{s,0},J_{s,0})
replaced by
(H_{\pm},J_{\pm},H_{0,\pm},J_{0,\pm})
, respectively, and we see that
V_{\pm,s}^{C_{p^{r}}}
is exactly the
C_{p^{k-r}}
representation
V_{\pm,s}
arising in Proposition
34
when applied to
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H_{s,0}^{\#p^{k-r}},J_{s,0}^{\#p^{k-r}})
. Thus see in this setting that
f^{\Phi C_{p^{r}}}
is a map that would be assigned by Proposition
34
to
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(H_{s,0}^{\#p^{k-r}},J_{s,0}^{\#p^{k-r}})
.

Thus the claim follows, from the above, together with Proposition
39
applied to each of the maps
f^{\Phi C_{p^{r}}}
(which is sensible by the previous observation), together with the fact that a map of
C_{p^{k}}
-spectra that is a weak equivalence on ordinary spectra of all geometric fixed point spectra is a weak equivalence of
C_{p^{k}}
-spectra
[
88
]
.
∎

10.5
Constructing symplectic cohomology and the cyclotomic structure maps.

We now wish to make sense of the homotopy colimit of the diagram (
191
) where all spectra and maps are defined with the constructions of Section
10.1
, choosing the homotopy definition data
\mathfrak{H}_{k}
such that the statements in Proposition
38
hold. This would be the spectral analog of the definition of symplectic cohomology. To to this we should take a “fibrant-cofibrant” replacement of this diagram, and then subsequently form the mapping telescope on it. However, this desire is complicated by the fact that we are repeatedly changing groups throughout the diagram, so the diagram does not sit in any one model category; and moreover that none of the function spectra we have written are homotopical because
\mathbb{S}
is generally not fibrant. We will make the desired “cofibrant-fibrant replacement” precise below.

-spectra. Explicitly, we will replace the (

10.6
Comparison with Nikolaus-Scholze.

Let
N
denote the nerve of a
1
-category and let
N^{hc}
denote the homotopy-coherent nerve of a simplicially-enriched category.
[
72
,
Tag 00KM
]
. Given a category
\mathcal{C}
, there is a simplicially enriched category associated to
\mathcal{C}
with underlying category
\mathcal{C}
and the morphisms from
x
to
y
the simplicial set obtained by taking the constant simplicial object on
\mathcal{C}(x,y)
; we will also denote this category by
\mathcal{C}
. There is a map of simplicial sets
[
72
,
Tag 00KZ
]
.

N(\mathcal{C})\simeq N^{hc}(\mathcal{C}).

(235)

Given a model category
\mathcal{C}
with objects contained in the Grothendieck universe
\overline{\mathcal{U}}
, we will denote the associated hammock localization by
L^{H}(\mathcal{C})

[
39
,
55
]
. Thus, writing
L^{H}(\mathcal{C})^{f}
for the Begner fibrant replacament
[
22
]
, there is a simplicial set
N^{hc}(L^{H}(\mathcal{C})^{f})
which is an
\infty
-category, and we call the
\infty
-category associated to
\mathcal{C}
.
.

There is thus a functor of
\infty
-categories from
N(\mathcal{C})
to the
\infty
-category associated to
\mathcal{C}
. In fact, the latter is a localization of the
\infty
-category
N(\mathcal{C})
at the weak equivalences
[
39
,
54
]
. In particular, our notion of of the
\infty

Definition 91
.

(Nikolaus-Scholze
[
77
]
)
The
\infty
-category of genuine
C_{p^{\infty}}
-spectra is the
\infty
-category

C_{p^{\infty}}Sp:=\varprojlim C_{p^{k}}Sp

where the inverse limit is taken along the group restriction functors
F^{C_{p^{k+1}}}_{C_{p^{k}}}
(or rather the functors obtained from applying
N^{hc}(L^{H}(\cdot)^{f})
to these functors).

The functor
\Phi^{C_{p}}
induces functors

\Phi^{C_{p}}:C_{p^{k}}Sp\to C_{p^{k}}/{C_{p}}Sp\simeq C_{p^{k-1}}Sp.

for all
p
, and commutes with
F^{C_{p^{k+1}}}_{C_{p^{k}}}
. It thus induces a functor

C_{p^{\infty}}Sp\to(C_{p^{\infty}}/C_{p})Sp\simeq C_{p^{\infty}}Sp

The
\infty
-category of genuine
p
-cyclotomic spectra is the equalizer of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{p^{\infty}}Sp}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{p^{\infty}}Sp.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{C_{p}}}

(236)

Theorem 17
.

The data of Theorem
16
define an associated genuine
p
-cyclotomic spectrum in the sense of Nikolaus-Scholze.

Proof.

This is essentially an exercise in standard
\infty
-categorical combinatorics. We first recall two notions and some useful theorems.

First, recall that a category can be regarded as a simplicial category with the simplicial sets of morphisms being discrete; we will use this identification to speak about functors from categories to simplicial categories. Equivalently, a functor from a category to a simplicial category is a functor from the category to the underlying category of the simplicial category.

The simplicial category
QCat
has objects all small
\infty
-categories and morphisms
QCat(X,Y)
the core of the
\infty
-category of functors
Fun(X,Y)
. The
\infty
-category of
\infty
-categories
\mathcal{QC}
is the homotopy-coherent nerve of
QCat
.

Suppose we have a category
\mathcal{C}
and a functor
\mathcal{F}:\mathcal{C}\to QCat
. To this data, one can associate
the weighted nerve
N_{\bullet}^{\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{C})}

[
72
,
Tag 025X
]
, which admits a map

U:N_{\bullet}^{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{C})

which is a cocartesian fibration Moreover, objects of the
\infty
-category
N_{\bullet}^{\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{C})}
are tuples
(C,x)
, where
C\in Ob(\mathcal{C})
and
x
is an object of
\mathcal{F}(C)
, and
1
-morphisms are tuples
(f,e):(C,x)\to(D,y)
where
f:C\to D
is a morphism in
\mathcal{C}
and
e:\mathcal{F}(f)(x)\to y
is a 1-morphism in
\mathcal{F}(D)
. Finally,
(f,e)
is
U
-cocartesian if and only if
e
is an isomorphism in the
\infty
-category
\mathcal{F}(D)
.
[
72
,
Tag 046Y
]
.

The weighted nerve is an
\infty
-category, and there is a map of
\infty
-categories

N_{\bullet}^{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{C})\xrightarrow{\theta}\int_{\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{F})}\mathcal{N}^{hc}(F).

which is an equivalence of
\infty
-categories and gives a bijection on
U
-cocartesian morphisms
[
72
,
Tag 027J
]
.

Now, Theorem
16
produces the data

SH(M,\mathbb{S})_{0}\xleftarrow{i_{01}}SH(M,\mathbb{S})_{1}\xleftarrow{i_{12}}SH(M,\mathbb{S})_{2}\xleftarrow{i_{23}}\cdots

(237)

where
SH(M,\mathbb{S})_{r}\in C_{p^{r}}-OSp
and

i_{ab}:F^{C_{p^{b}}}_{C_{p^{a}}}SH(M,\mathbb{S})_{a}\to SH(M,\mathbb{S})_{b}

for
b>a
are the corresponding inclusions of mapping telescopes, and are all stable equivalences of orthogonal spectra.

Write
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
for the category corresponding to the poset of nonnegative integers ordered by magnitude There is a functor

\mathcal{F}:\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{op}\to 1-Cat

mapping
n
to
C_{p^{n}}-OSp
and mapping
n\to m
to
F^{C_{p^{n}}}_{C_{p^{m}}}
. This defines an associated functor of
\infty
-categories

which sends each
1
-morphism
n\to m
to
N(F^{C_{p^{n}}}_{C_{p^{m}}})
.
There is another functor

N^{hc}(L^{H}(\mathcal{F})^{h}):Z^{op}\to QCat

which sends each
1
-morphism
n\to m
to
N^{hc}(L^{H}(F^{C_{p^{n}}}_{C_{p^{m}}})^{f})
where we are are using the fact that
F^{C_{p^{n}}}_{C_{p^{m}}}
sends
C_{p^{n}}-
stable equivalences to

-stable equivalences to define

This defines a functor

(239)

The data (

) produces an element of

. There is a corresponding element of

To argue that the data of the cyclotomic structure maps enhances this to an genuine

-cyclotomic spectrum, we argue similarly. We first detail the construction of the functor

\Phi^{C_{p}}:C_{p^{\infty}}Sp\to C_{p^{\infty}}Sp.

[

We now have the strictly commutative diagram of

∎

Using this data, one can define the
p
-typical topological cyclic homology groups
TR(SH(M,\mathbb{S}),p),TC(SH(M,\mathbb{S}),p)
as in
[

]
(see
[

, Definition II.4.4]
). There is a functor from genuine
p
-cyclotomic spectra to
p
-cyclotomic spectra in the sense of Nikolaus-Scholze which is an equivalence on the subcategory of bounded below objects; however, in general symplectic cohomology is bounded neither above nor below.

Proof of Theorem
3
.

It suffices to compute the homotopy groups of
H\mathbb{Z}\wedge SH^{\bullet}(M,\mathbb{S})
as an ordinary non-equivariant spectrum. But it is constructed via the homotopy colimit (
227
); smashing commutes with homotopy colimits, and on homology, by the functorial isomorphisms
H\mathbb{Z}\wedge F(X,\mathbb{S})\simeq F(X,H\mathbb{Z})
for finitely-built spectra
X
, Proposition
39
identifies
\pi_{*}H\mathbb{Z}\wedge F(|H^{i}|,\mathbb{S})
with
HF^{-*}(\widetilde{H^{\#p^{k}}};\mathbb{Z})
, where
\widetilde{H^{\#p^{k}}}
is a non-equivariant perturbation of
H^{p^{k}}
which is now nondegenerate, and the new homotopy definition data for
F(|widetilde{H^{\#p^{k}}}|,\mathbb{S})
are chosen such that that proposition holds. To identify the action of
H\mathbb{Z}\wedge[t_{i}]
on homotopy groups with the corresponding action of continuation maps on
HF^{-*}(H^{\#p^{k}};\mathbb{Z})
, one constructs commutative diagrams via Proposition
35
and Proposition (
36
) where the new horizontal maps are induced by
regular, non-equivariant
admissible continuation data, which satisfy the regularity hypotheses of Theorem
15
letting us make the comparison with the usual Floer-theoretic continuation map.
∎

Remark 65
.

While this document does not contain a complete proof that the cyclotomic spectrum
SH^{\bullet}(M,\mathbb{S})
is independent of the choices made, verifying this should simply be a slightly tedious exercise in the technology of this paper. One first argues that
SH^{\bullet}(M,\mathbb{S})
is unchanged up to equivalence if one compatibly stabilizes all homotopy definition data (a fairly simple type of stabilization, for which one allows only stabilization of perturbation data by parameterizations with trivial perturbation functions, is sufficient). One then uses Proposition
35
, and an analog of Proposition
38
for homotopy definition data to build a ‘map of diagrams’ of the form (
224
). Performing the functorial replacements of Proposition
16
then defines maps which give maps of diagrams (
227
) satisfying compatibility conditions with respect to the geometric fixed point functors, as in (
229
). These maps are pieced together to define a map of the mapping telescopes defining equivariant maps of the spectra
SH(M,\mathbb{S})_{\geq 0}
, and this should define exactly a map in the corresponding infinity category of genuine
p
-cyclotomic spectra. Checking that it is an equivalence is then done on homotopy groups, noting that the cyclotomic structure and comparison with symplectic homology (Theorem
3
) immediately implies that it is an equivalence on homology after taking geometric fixed points for any
C_{p^{k}}
, and is thus an a map of genuine
p
-cyclotomic spectra which is an equivalence of underlying
C_{p^{\infty}}
-spectra, i.e. an equivalence of genuine
p
-cyclotomic spectra.

11
Appendix

11.1
Classifying spaces for
K
theory

(Equivariant) Grassmannians.

We first recall the construction of the space
BO
and
BU
, as well as their equivariant analogs. Given a map
A:V\to W
between real inner product spaces, let
A^{T}:W\to V
denote its transpose. If instead
(V,W)
are Hermitian inner product spaces and
A
is complex linear, let
A^{\dagger}:W\to V
denote its Hermitian transpose. For a real inner product space
V
and a Hermitian inner product space
W
, let

EO(k,V)=\{A\in Hom(\mathbb{R}^{k},V)|A^{T}A=id\},\;EU(k,W)=\{A\in Mat_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{C}^{n},W)|A^{\dagger}A=id\}.

(241)

For
k\leq\dim V
and
k\leq\dim_{\mathbb{C}}W
, there are fibrations

EO(k,V)\to EO(k+1,V\oplus\mathbb{R})\to S^{n},EU(k,W)\to EU(k+1,W\oplus\mathbb{C})\to S^{2n+1}

(242)

show inductively that for any
C
there is an
N=N(C,k)
such that for
\dim V>N
or
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}W>N
, we have
\pi_{i}(EO(k,V))=\pi_{i}(EU(k,W)=0
for
0\leq i\leq C
. There is a free
O(k)
action on
EO(k,n)
by precomposition and a similar free
U(k)
action on
EU(k,n)
. Thus

EO(k)=\colim_{n}EO(k,\mathbb{R}^{n}),EU(k)=\colim_{n}EU(k,\mathbb{C}^{n})

(243)

are contractible spaces (which can be given a CW stucture, since all above maps are embeddings of manifolds) with free actions of
O(k)
and
U(k)
. Therefore

BO(k)=EO(k)/O(k)=Gr(k,\mathbb{R}^{\infty}),BU(k)=EU(k)/U(k)=Gr_{\mathbb{C}}(k,\mathbb{C}^{\infty})

are classifying spaces for
O(k)
and
U(k)
, respectively. They manifestly parameterize
k
-planes in
\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
and
\mathbb{C}^{\infty}
, respectively. In particular, there are
\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
\mathbb{C}^{k}
bundles
\eta_{k}^{\mathbb{R}}\to BO(k),\eta_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}\to BU(k)
called universal bundles.

The inclusions

\mathbb{R}^{\infty}\simeq 0\oplus\mathbb{R}^{\infty}\subset\mathbb{R}^{\infty},\mathbb{C}^{\infty}\simeq 0\oplus\mathbb{C}^{\infty}\subset\mathbb{R}^{\infty}

(244)

induce closed inclusions

BO(k)\subset BO(k+1),BU(k)\subset BU(k+1)

(245)

covered by inclusions

EO(k)\subset EO(k+1),EU(k)\subset EU(k+1)

(246)

where we send
A\in EO(k)
to the map sending first
k
coordinate vectors
e_{1},\ldots,e_{k}
to the images of
A(e_{1}),\ldots A(e_{k})
under (
244
), and send
e_{k+1}
to the first coordinate vector in
\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
or
\mathbb{C}^{\infty}
respectively. These inclusions equivariant with respect to the inclusions of groups

O(n)\subset O(n+1),U(n)\subset U(n+1)

(247)

given on matrices by
A\mapsto A\oplus 1
.

by taking quotients by
O(k)\subset O(k+1)
,
U(k)\subset U(k+1)
thought of as matrices with the top left
k\times k
entries nonzero. In particular, the colimits

EO=\colim_{k}EO(k),EU=\colim_{k}EU(k)

(248)

are contractible spaces (which can be given a CW structure, using the filtrations arising from (
243
)) with free actions of the topological groups

O=\colim_{n}O(n),U=\colim_{n}U(n)

(249)

and thus
BO=EO/O=\colim_{k}BO(k)
and
BU=EU/U=\colim_{k}BU(k)
are classifying spaces (also CW complexes) for
O
and
U
. Manifestly, any map from
f:K\to BO
from a compact
CW
complex
k
factors through some
BO(k)
, and thus the pullback
f^{*}\eta^{\mathbb{R}}_{k}
to
K
will be a vector bundle; the indeterminacy of the choice of
k
implies that the virtual vector bundle
[f^{*}\eta^{\mathbb{R}}_{k}]-[\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{k}]
is well defined. The fact that vector bundles admit complements then implies that
\mathbb{Z}\times BO
and
\mathbb{Z}\times BU
are classifying spaces for
KO^{0}
and
KU^{0}
, respectively.

An equivariant analog of the entire construction is straightforward. We will only do the orthogonal case as the unitary case is completely analogous. Fix a compact Lie group
G
, and let
\mathcal{U}
be a complete
G
-universe. The previous discussion are
(EO(k),EU(k))
are free contractible
G
-CW complexes with free actions of
O(k)
and
U(k)
commuting with the
G
-actions.

Fix an isomorphism

\mathcal{U}\simeq V_{1}^{\mathbb{Z}}\oplus V_{2}^{\mathbb{Z}}\oplus\ldots

(250)

where the
\{V_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
are a choice of irreps of
G
such that each irreducible representation occurs exactly once. Write

\mathcal{U}_{a_{1},\ldots,}\simeq V_{1}^{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq-a_{1}}}\oplus V_{2}^{\mathbb{Z}_{\geq-a_{2}}}\oplus\ldots\subset\mathcal{U}.

for
(a_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
any sequence of nonnegative integers with only finitely many nonzero terms.

Let
\mathcal{Z}
be the poset of such sequences with
(a_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}\leq(b_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
if
a_{i}\leq b_{i}
for all
i
. Then the inclusions
\mathcal{U}_{(a_{i})}\subset\mathcal{U}_{(b_{i})}
for
(a_{i})\leq(b_{i})
define a functor

F:\mathcal{Z}\to G-Top,F((a_{i}))=E(\mathbb{R}^{a_{1}}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{a_{2}}\oplus\ldots,\mathcal{U}_{(a_{i})})

with all morphisms closed inclusions (which can be refined to inclusions of
G
-CW complexes). The colimit of this functor over this filtered diagram is a space that is homeomoprhic to
EO
. This colimit is what we mean by
EO\in G-Top
, and it is straightforward to show that it is a classifying space for
KO^{0}_{G}
.

Lemma 63
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be the completion of a complete
G
-universe. There is a
G
-homotopy equivalence

\mathbb{Z}\times BO\to\Fred H

which induces a natural isomorphism between the functors (
121
) and (
123
).

Proof.

The argument for
G=1
is straightforward: the proof of Theorem
9
allows one to build a map
c(FredH)\to\mathbb{Z}\times BO
which is a weak equivalence. The result then follows by Theorem
12
. For general
G
-the equivariant analog of the proof of Theorem
9
and Theorem
8.2
.
∎
